They all confess to having at least $25K in student loan debt. They’re all seniors in college. None has a job lined up after graduation. They don’t seem worried about that. They don’t care that their futures are being leveraged away. They’re in Vegas, playing beer pong and looking for their next Big Ass Beer. Is this the American dream? 

After my line of questioning, they’re annoyed. I crossed the line when I asked them whether they’d wear the hats into South Philly. I can see the venom rising in one of them, the big one. They think I’m being condescending. I am, but it’s in response to their provocation. I don’t like being provoked. After my questions, I tried to understand their political beliefs. I wanted to understand their values. But just as this is the new era in American capitalism, this is the new era in politics as well. You don’t have values. Entire political parties barely have a platform. You rally around 

something that will never happen. Just take a side, declare your allegiance, and defend it to the death. 

The MAGA bros decide to raise the stakes. They challenge us to beer pong. 

They move in this direction for revenge. We know that. They want to crush us. They didn’t like my line of questioning and feel I got the upper hand. They want to revert back to their strength to maximize their advantage: beer pong and a touch of toxic masculinity. 

But Lifecoach is on fire. She makes four of her first six shots and sets the tone. They look confused. They’re surprised. They start calling me “old man.” But I decide to divide and conquer. I ask the big one why he chose his partner. He’s obviously a terrible player, the weak link. “You can’t have a weak link in life,” I tell him. We are getting in their heads. 

We win game one. 

They up the ante. They challenge us again. But this time there are going to be consequences. The losers have to do three shots of tequila each, one after the other. I’m not sure how to respond. My instincts tell me to leave. This only has downside. However, I like tequila. In fact, I love tequila, and I’m competitive. And Lifecoach is worse. She says “Done. We shoot first.” 

The game is hostile. The Buckeyes want revenge and are focused. Lifecoach is not as crisp this game. I need her to come through, so I goad her by telling her not to worry about it; I can carry the game. This works. 

We win. Lifecoach almost skips to the bar and comes back all smiles with a tray filled with shots of tequila and presents it to the MAGA boys as if she’s one of the women presenting a winning suitcase on _Deal or No Deal_ . She displays them like the crown jewels. She ordered rail tequila. This is not Don Julio 1942. This is not even Jose Cuervo. This is Montezuma out of a plastic bottle. Warm. She’s in her glory. They begin to take the shots. After drinking two, the big guy vomits on the table. _Right on the fucking table_ . 

“Let’s go,” I say to Lifecoach. We use the chaos to head back out into 

the sunshine. “These people at the LINQ are fun for a bit,” I say, “but what a bunch of animals. I gotta get back to the Wynn. Back to safety.” 

As we walk, we laugh. Basking in our victory. As we get closer to our destination, we talk about Resorts World. It’s the new development across the street, now in view. It’s funded by a Malaysian company whose plan is to build one of the largest Chinese-themed resorts in the world to cater to the middle-income residents of Asia. Crowned the most expensive resort property ever developed in Las Vegas, costing a whopping $4.3 billion, the ultra resort has surpassed the cost of The Cosmopolitan ($4.18 billion) as well as the Wynn Las Vegas ($3.26 billion). When the project was announced, the planned features included a panda exhibit and a replica of the Great Wall of China. 

Yes, the most expensive resort in Vegas history to cater to the rising Chinese middle class! All a smart investment. After all, China has one of the fastest growing middle-class populations in the world, and now they have their very own piece of the Nevada desert. 

We get to the Wynn and see the Rabbi and Jerry in the lobby. The Rabbi walks over. “Let’s go downtown before the show,” he says. “I want to play some single deck. Maybe we can hit Dino’s on the way back and do some quick karaoke before the show.” 

“Yeah, good idea. Let’s stop at the D for a coney. I’m hungry.” 

The afternoon flies by, and we are soon rushing to get to the show. It’s in the Colosseum in Caesars Palace. This is not a big arena, more of an intimate venue. We are in the pit, right up front. There are only a couple of hundred people, so we feel like we’re on stage with the band. The concert starts on time, which is probably the biggest change the band has made over the past decade. Axl doesn’t make you wait until midnight anymore. 

The show is fantastic. Axl’s voice is strong, and he’s moving around the stage well. Slash is a virtuoso. And Duff looks great and is probably recommending a short duration IG trade to his clients. He’s a licensed 

financial advisor now. I love him for reinventing himself that way and going back to school. A great lesson in retooling, staying healthy. 

We leave the concert, singing. “Take me down to the Paradise City …” Everyone is feeling great. Maybe Paradise City is the American dream? “Give me your tired, your poor, your huddled masses yearning to breathe free …11 Rags to riches or so they say. Ya gotta keep pushin’ for the fortune and fame. You know it’s all a gamble when it’s just a game.”12 

***** 

I’m scheduled to meet early with Jerry at our pool cabana. I’m doing one of my mentoring sessions, and I want to focus on the role of central banks. Pool season is almost over here, but I want one last afternoon of lounging in the sun. 

I get down to the cabana early to check the news. The markets are still sitting at their highs. The S&P is up 6% since October 1 and rates are moving higher. The 2s/10s have steepened by twenty-four basis points and ten-year yields are nearly thirty basis points higher. GBP has moved from 1.22 to 1.28. Everything is working right now. The back-up in rates will continue and all of this is not a surprise. The Fed’s balance sheet has increased $300 billion in less than two months. A total of $800 billion of stimulus in six months. The quickest pace since QE1 began in 2009. The election is the next big catalyst for the markets now that the trade deal looks resolved. February 3 is the Iowa caucus and Super Tuesday is a month later on March 3. Both are important dates for the markets. I call Elias and leave a message telling him to check the volatility markets around those dates. 

Jerry joins me as I’m finishing my message. “Morning, Jerry.” “Hey, Boss.” 

“For today, let’s focus on the role of the central banks. Let’s start here. Governments need money. They have lots of bills.” I’ve given this speech 

before, to a class I used to teach, so it comes out on autopilot as my mind wanders to other topics. 

Jerry places his phone face up on the table. It lights up. 

“Governments generally get money to pay those bills in one of three ways. They can impose taxes, they can create more currency, or they can borrow the money. Obviously, most people don’t like taxes. Central banks were created to help deal with the other two options. And central bankers are unique animals. They’re not elected officials and they have the power to create money with virtually no oversight. Shit, Jay Powell over at the Fed has a group of 200 traders working for him. He’s running one of the biggest trading firms in the fucking world!” 

“That’s crazy.” 

“I realize you know some of this. But it’s important to remember the basics. Early on in the Fed’s history,” I tell him, “the top guys were business leaders who understood conditions on the ground. Now it’s all academics who wax philosophic and rely on back-fitted models that may or may not be applicable to current times. There’s a reason economics is called the dismal science.” 

I pull out a handout I put together for my class. The first slide goes through the history of the Fed, founded by Congress in 1913, pretty boring stuff back then, but its mission was expanded after the Great Depression. 

Jerry’s phone lights up again. 

“Today,” I say, “the Fed has unbelievable powers. To start with, they get to manage the total outstanding supply of US dollars. They control the printing presses. In fact, the Fed can create or destroy billions of dollars whenever they want. They don’t even need to print them anymore. They just push a button. They digitally debit and credit them to the banks. They create new bank reserves, like a video game. But this is serious, really important stuff. Creating more currency historically has created inflation. Inflation is scary.” 

“Yeah,” he says. “When it starts, it’s really hard to stop.” 

I flip to the next page. “Here’s the catch, though,” I say. “The Fed can’t spend the money they’re creating anyway they want. They can just use the dollars to buy things in the big public markets on Wall Street, like bonds or mortgage-backed securities.” 

It lights up again. “You going to get that?” I ask. 

“Nah. It’s just my wife,” he says. 

I turn to the next page. “The Fed’s balance sheet is now back above $4 trillion. This makes them one of the biggest players in the markets. This all started in 2008 to combat the GFC. The Fed’s assets ballooned from $900 billion to a peak of $4.5 trillion, but now it’s the norm to prop up the markets.” 

The Rabbi has now joined us in the cabana. He sits down and pours a Bloody Mary. He asks Jerry if he wants a tequila. He has been listening, and I can tell that he wants to jump in. 

“That’s one thing the Fed really loves doing,” he says. “The Fed loves to expand its balance sheet and buy things. That’s the essence of QE.” 

“Why do they call it QE?” Jerry asks. 

Jerry should know this. This is one of my beefs with him. He works hard and slaves away on the models. But sometimes I wonder whether he ever picks his head up to see what’s going on in the world, whether he really has the passion for this job. You need to want to know everything. You need to want to be the best. You need to mentally prepare yourself every day. I reply, “The name comes from the huge sums of money it involves. Once a quantity is released into the market, there’s downward pressure—the easing bit—on interest rates. They say that an economist named Richard Werner may have been the first to use the term. You should read about him. He has some good theories on Japan.” 

I turn back to the deck. “The Fed expands its balance sheet by creating more currency, by printing money _ex nihilo,_ literally out of nothing. The Fed takes this new money created out of thin air and goes to the open 

market to buy stuff. This is the mechanism by which the Fed expands its balance sheet.” 

We turn the page. “In theory, the Fed has three key goals. The first is price stability. This is a fancy way of saying it needs to keep an eye on inflation. Inflation is like the oatmeal for Goldilocks: you don’t want it too hot or too cold. Inflation around 2% is just right. After the disastrous experience of Germany in the 1930s, and in the USA in the ’70s, this became the top priority. But inflation is tricky. It’s hard to understand. It’s like the plague. Inflation causes revolutions, even wars. Our modern Fed has chronically mismodeled inflation. It doesn’t really understand it, yet inflation is the Fed’s top priority. There’s very little accountability in central banking.” 

I turn the page again. “The second goal is a stable real economy. I’m talking Main Street, not Wall Street.” 

“But that’s just marketing,” the Rabbi says. “Wall Street is what matters.” 

“And here lies the problem,” I say. “They do their best to promote low unemployment, but their mandate doesn’t include things like wealth inequality or a living wage. If the economy creates a trillion minimumwage jobs, that’s success. If entire generations of people are being left behind on Main Street, that’s success so long as people have minimumwage-paying jobs.” 

I turn to the next slide. “The third goal is financial stability. This encompasses an efficient and smoothly running payments system and the prevention of financial crises.” 

“What exactly does this mean?” Jerry asks. 

“Things like being the lender of last resort,” I answer. 

“Hold on,” the Rabbi says. “It’s way more than that. In the old days maybe, but now they’re the liquidity provider whenever the market needs it.” 

“Yeah,” I say. “It’s hard to argue with that.” 

The Rabbi goes on. “And now whenever we get a crisis, they adopt new policies. They try new things. We are in the midst of that now, from Keynes to Friedman to who knows what.” 

“Oh,” I say. “One last thing. The Fed is responsible for regulating the banks and keeping the system safe for us.” 

The Rabbi jumps back in on this. “Just think how well they did on that during the run-up to the GFC. It was as if Greenspan himself was giving the ‘Always Be Closing’ speech in the movie _Glengarry Glen Ross_ .” He stands up and mimics the famous scene in the movie. He knows the speech by memory. 

Chairman Greenspan to the heads of the Wall Street banks in mid-2007: Gentleman, thank you for joining me today here at the offices of the New York Fed … Please remember that the “Coffee’s for closers only …” A-B-C. A-always, B-be, C-closing. Always be closing. Always be closing … You got the prospects comin’ in. You think they came in to get out of the rain? Guy doesn’t walk on the lot unless he wants to buy. Sitting out there waiting to give you their money! Are you gonna take it? Are you man enough to take it? 

He sits back down. I applaud. 

I continue. “In the old days central banks didn’t have these powers. They were created to manage the gold standard. The gold standard meant that each country defined its currency in terms of a fixed weight of gold. Central banks held physical gold to ensure their currency could be converted to it. This meant that the currency had real value. It was backed by something more than a promise.” 

The Rabbi jumps in. “Nixon killed the gold standard, the Bretton Woods system, in 1971, back when impeachment was still a novelty. It was when inflation was choking our economy. This was replaced by freely floating fiat currencies.” 

“This was great for macro investors,” I say. “Just imagine all of these new freely floating instruments, like kids in a candy store.” 

The Rabbi chimes in again. “Now we live in a world of fiat currency. Fiat is a nice way of saying wampum, bullshit, no value, worth only the paper it’s printed on, like playing _Monopoly_ . And let me tell you,” the Rabbi goes on, “this is the scary part. We are turning it into a self-fulfilling loop of more and more. Just more debt and more QE.” 

“He’s right,” I say. “Easy money used to be for tough times, for big events like recessions. Now it’s normal, everyday stuff. It just keeps getting bigger.” 

“But why?” Jerry says. 

“It’s complicated,” I say. “When you start QE, you have good intentions. You have to save jobs, save the economy. But then, over time, it has a disproportionate impact. Stocks go higher, but the economy stagnates. The market becomes disconnected from the real economy.” 

The Rabbi looks over his shades. “It’s like doing a deal with the devil. Imagine. You’re the chairman of the fucking US Federal Reserve Bank, one of the most powerful men in the universe. But all of a sudden, the bubble is popping, the system is breaking, you need to save millions of jobs, the world is melting around you, you’re at your weakest point, your legacy is at risk. But just then, a QE devil appears on your shoulder. He has an offer. He can save things. You do the deal. And for that moment it does save things. The markets rip back, jobs are saved. But then it never goes away. It begins to rot things out from the inside out. The rich get richer; everyone else suffers. A really perverse ending that started with good thoughts.” 

The Rabbi keeps talking as he walks over to the bar. “It’s just another drug. Start with a little and you feel pretty good. But after a while …” He pours another Bloody and sits down. 

***** 

The Rabbi is continuing his sermon to Jerry as he flips channels looking for CNBC. We are all back in the cabana after a quick lunch in my suite. 

“So, let me make this simple for you. Since the GFC, the Fed has followed a pretty simple recipe: 

Step 1: They take interest rates to zero. 

Step 2: They expand their balance sheet and buy stuff to drive up prices. 

Step 3: They make sure the market knows the Fed put is alive and kicking. 

That’s it. Pretty simple. The problem is it really only helps the rich. Main Street gets screwed.” 

Lifecoach is playing blackjack at one of the outdoor tables by the pool. I wander over to see if she’s having any luck. I hear a ruckus coming from the cabana. 

“What’s going on?” I yell back to the Rabbi. 

“Big D. At the New York economic club.” 

“What’s he saying?” I ask. 

“He’s pushing again for negative rates in the US. You gotta hear this. He’s really ripping the Fed. You gotta get in here.” 

I walk back in. The Big D is in a full rant. He says other countries “openly cut interest rates so that many are now actually getting paid when they pay off their loan, known as negative interest. Give me some of that. Give me some of that money. I want some of that money.” 

The Rabbi is now doing a full-on jig in front of the TV. “Wow. Did you hear that? He just went full-on Jerry McGuire on us. ‘Show me the money!’ Hey Jerry, let me hear you say it. That’s how you got the nickname after all!” 

As the excitement wears down, the latest polls from the UK hit the wire. The Tories have a ten-point lead. The Labour party’s message is not resonating. Free Wi-Fi and nationalization are not selling. BoJo is going to win this election. We are still long cable—pound sterling or GBP—but we 

need to get longer. There’s more money to be made. Looks to be about the cheapest asset globally. 

Elias and the Rabbi begin talking about the best way for us to add to our position. Trade structuring is important in this business. Our GBP trade is more directional in nature. We think it’s going higher. But the markets give us the ability to structure this in many different ways. For example, it can be a simpler delta one trade, and we can bet it will go higher but bear the risk of being wrong on a dollar for dollar basis. Or we can structure the trade via options to be long gamma when our event comes to fruition or limit our loss to the premium paid if we are wrong. If we really want to get fancy, we can structure this in some conditional format to take advantage of misplaced correlation or skew (if market A moves to a certain level, then market B is likely to move differently, to the priced distribution). Elias is an expert in these things. 

And as in all things, you need to calculate your base odds. If you did the same trade every day for the past twenty years, how often would you have been right, or wrong. An important lesson for any market participant is that some trades simply work more often than others. 

Finally, sizing that trade is critically important. Like everything else in this business, it can’t be done in a random, willy-nilly manner. You need discipline. People who wing it always underperform. Some form of the Kelly criterion is best for this. The Kelly formula was developed back in 1956 by a former Bell Labs researcher named John Larry Kelly Jr. The formula generally finds that people bet too much when their odds are low, and not enough when their odds are high. In a nutshell, if you have a 51% chance of winning, you should bet 2% of your budget; if you have a 65% chance of winning, you should bet 30%; and if you have a 90% chance of winning, you should bet a lot more: 80%. And of course, this all assumes that you never bet in situations where you have a negative expected return. Gambler’s ruin is not something to be proud of. 

As the conversation comes to an end, the Rabbi looks at me over his 

sunglasses. He’s lying on the couch in the cabana. “And hey, that new Oyo hotel is a complete fucking mess. Those $1 blackjack tables are not going to pay the bills.” 

“You think we sell more?” 

“Yeah, I don’t know what they’re thinking.” 

Elias says, “I’ll get on that.” He goes on with a slight smile on his face: “And Boss, I couldn’t find a single stripper last night who owned rental properties.” 

“Ha, thanks for doing the diligence for us. Not surprising though. The banks have cut out all the mortgage shenanigans since the GFC. But you know what?” 

“What?” 

“I bet all of these casinos took on even more debt and have been buying their stock back with it.” 

“Confirmed,” the Rabbi says. “That’s a fact.” 

Christine LaGarde, the new head of the European Central Bank (ECB), is giving her first speech today, and our eyes are now glued to the TV in the cabana. As macro guys, we love these. We parse every word. We look for secret meanings. We analyze the outfits the central bankers are wearing and study their body language to try to get a leg up on everyone else in the markets. Heck, during the Greenspan Fed, traders would monitor the size of his briefcase for clues on his next move. A couple years ago, we hired a former FBI agent to school us on how to read body language and detect lies. There is something perverse to getting this far in the weeds. You risk missing the big picture. But LaGarde’s big picture is clear. She speaks of a “new order in the world economy.” Yeap, you guessed it. Her exciting new world order is more QE and more spending. Meet the new boss, same as the old boss. 

We go back to Planet Hollywood for dinner at Strip House and more blackjack. The casino is packed. Today I vow to go to bed early. No more Drai’s. I leave at midnight. Vee and the Rabbi are back in true form. 

I’m in a deep sleep. The phone rings again. 

I answer. “No, I’m not going back to Drai’s tonight.” 

“Boss, it’s, it’s, it’s Jerry. I don’t want to go to Drai’s. I have a problem.” “What’s the matter?” 

“We got rolled. We were at the bachelor party at the Palms, me and my friends, up in one of the penthouses. Some of the guys called some girls. It was crazy. But then they started to disappear, one by one, and we realized they had everything: our wallets, our watches, my passport. Boss, my shoes. I don’t have any freaking shoes.” 

I can’t help but laugh. 

“And Boss, I can’t take any more tequila. It’s making me sick. My stomach is a mess.” 

“Go downstairs. I’ll send a car over to get you. And go drink some water or Gatorade or something.” I send a car and try to go back to sleep. I have an early flight. It will take Jerry a few days to sort out the passport, and I can’t help but think of that line from _Bright Lights, Big City_ —that “he’s all messed up and no place to go.” 

I can’t get back to sleep. I just twist and turn. I can feel I have a bigger problem with Jerry now, more bad judgment on his part: Why would he call me and make this my problem? 

I get out of bed at 5 a.m. Vegas is on Pacific time. That means you have to wake up early. I don’t know how the guys on the West Coast do it. Milken’s old crew and the guys at PIMCO. I’m used to being in London, five hours ahead of New York. By the time the US markets open, you’re ready. You’re ahead of everyone. But not in Vegas. You wake up and are immediately behind. I check my phone and already have hundreds of messages. Another macro legend has decided to call it quits, Louis Bacon from Moore Capital. He had an incredible thirty-year run, annualized at 17%, managed downside. Just shows you how distorted the markets have become when the legends are all giving back the outside capital. Oh, and the Fed pumped in another $100 billion in “temporary” liquidity. 

The melt-up in stocks continues. Interestingly, the problems we saw in repo are now spreading to China. The three-month interbank borrowing costs (known as Shibor) are spiking. The USA and China are all connected these days, and it’s another sign of the danger that lurks right around the corner, all a sign of disaster in the next downturn, another omen. But you know what this means. The central bank will step in. The PBOC will need to inject more liquidity. It will need to support the markets. Stay long risk. We are in a bull market! Buy some Chinese stocks into month end. The central banks are engaged. 

But as I head to the airport, I realize that Vegas has not made me feel better about anything. We are back in a bubble. 

## **Notes** 

10 Bonfire of the Vanities, Tom Wolfe. 

11 “The New Colossus,” Emma Lazarus. 

12 “Paradise City,” Guns N’ Roses. 

## **Chapter 3 The Star Tavern and Life in Knightsbridge** 

## **December 2019** 

**12/5/19—** _Impeachment proceedings begin in the US House of Representatives._ 

**12/6/19—** _Payrolls beat at 266K; unemployment back down to 3.5%; average hourly earnings +3.1%._ 

**12/11/19—** _Fed on hold. SEP says it may not move rates at all in 2020._ 

**12/18/2019—** _House of Representatives impeaches President Trump._ 

**12/20/19—** _Andrew Bailey appointed next governor of the BoE._ 

**12/26/19—** _Nasdaq hits 9K for the first time._ 

**12/31/19—** _Wuhan: China’s Municipal Health Commission reports a cluster of pneumonia cases._ 

**Month-end: UST 10s at 1.92%; yield curve steeper; dollar index -1.9% (EM up); S&P +3.0%; Nasdaq +3.5%.** 

**Year-end: S&P +31.5%; 10s -72 bps on the year.** 

T oo much booze, too much drinking. I need to get back in shape. I need more yoga, more cardio. I get up early to go running. It’s still dark. There are a few street cleaners out on the morning shift. I spot one picking up an empty can of Pringles that littered a bench. I’ve always wondered how it is that London’s streets always look so clean in the morning, as if the people there don’t excrete trash as they do in other places. 

I start down Rotten Row in Hyde Park. A morning dew overhangs the park, and as I run further, Fleetwood Mac blaring into my eardrums, the grass becomes dryer, and eventually, a morning light sets in. It’s too early for the tourists to come out and see what was once Henry VIII’s deer hunting grounds. Now there are no deer, and instead, just squirrels and hissing swans the tourists like to feed. I’ve never understood that. 

I run around the Serpentine, past the Princess Di fountain, back past One Hyde Park, once the most expensive real estate in the world. As I cross the street by the Bulgari Hotel, I see a vintage Porsche 911. It’s bright grey, a color they call Arctic silver. It makes me think back to the very first Porsche I bought. It looked almost identical. I loved that car. I bought it with my first seven-figure bonus check. In those days, I couldn’t believe it. I was getting paid so much money to do something that was so fun. But somehow, that feeling wears off. You feel you’re still doing something important for your investors, but you start to lose interest in the policy ramifications. It all becomes about the money. And all of a sudden, the fun is gone. 

I remember a story that a good tech investor told me. I was sitting in his office bullshitting about the markets when he said, “Look, compensation is a funny thing on Wall Street. My first year, I couldn’t believe they paid me that much. I thought I was so lucky. The next year, I thought what I received was fair. Every year since then, I thought I got fucked.” Wall Street has this effect on you. It warps your mind. And once that happens, you lose all sense of reality. 

Lately, it has all become a slog for me. Years of placidly upward- 

trending stock and bond markets, ever-lower volatility, predictably friendly central bank responses to even the slightest of market disruptions. Yes, in the wake of the GFC, the world had achieved some level of homeostasis, and that can be a good thing. But it has all made my job more difficult and, more importantly, painfully boring. Say what you want about Brexit and the Trump election, at least they spiced things up a bit. 

I think back to how I ended up here in the supposed center of the financial universe at the mercy of the market gods. I started as an undergrad with an eye toward law school, expecting to be some sort of litigator. But this was 1990, and the world was changing. Only two and a half years after President Reagan stood at the Brandenburg Gate imploring Mikhail Gorbachev to “Tear down this wall,” the wall had fallen, and with it, the Soviet Union’s credibility. The Evil Empire was teetering like a Jenga tower, and people were transfixed on what the world would look like once it finally imploded. Three hundred million people freed from the binds of communism, countries reasserting their autonomy, new frontiers, and new markets. The fervor seeped into popular culture and, for the first time since Vietnam, music and geopolitics cohabitated within the zeitgeist. The Scorpions’ monster ballad “Wind of Change” played on loop; Jesus Jones excitedly meditated on “watching the world wake up from history.” It was an exhilarating time in the world, and I wanted to be part of it. The world was more promising. That was back when capitalism was at its peak. 

Global macro drew me in along these lines when graduation came around. It gave me a seat at the table, a chance to be intimately involved with issues that mattered, the very stuff that shapes the contours of our world. While my peers were going to the big consulting firms and trying to figure out how many widgets some nameless company was selling in Whocaresville, USA, I’d be grappling with world-altering economic and political developments and expressing my views in markets that seamlessly stretched from New York to London to Tokyo and back again—history in 

real time, James Bond shit. That’s global macro in its romantic ideal. But these days, I’m feeling less like 007 and more like Don Quixote. 

Whenever I speak with analysts on their first day, I avoid these sorts of wide-eyed musings and opt to give them the dry version of macro instead. It’s probably better to set the bar appropriately low. At its most mechanical level, macro is about investing in assets on the basis of changes in the fundamental landscape: the ups and downs in growth and inflation and interest rates. It’s about understanding business cycles, and how government spending and central bank policies will impact those cycles. We analyze an enormous amount of macroeconomic data and spend hours focused on central banks, attempting to predict both fiscal and monetary decisions and the impact of those decisions on financial markets, ranging from bonds to currencies to equities and commodities. 

I keep running down the alley past Zuma, cross the street to Harrods and back to my mews, my home, my own den of thieves. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

A mews is basically an alley without trash. They used to be the horse stables and servants’ quarters for the mansions. That was back before the London real-estate boom. Now, every square inch is developed. It’s expensive. People moved in and kicked the horses out. 

Knightsbridge is a place where the rich are eaten by the ultrarich. It’s a place where people still dress well, particularly the men, and yoga pants are not considered a wardrobe staple for women. It’s a long way from where I grew up. My neighbors in the mews are one of the convicted LIBOR manipulators, a footballer, and a bunch of Russians who keep odd hours. 

I like Knightsbridge and the time zone is the best for macro guys like me. It allows us to see the Asian markets in the morning, Europe during the day, and the USA in the evening. That’s why I live here. Plus, all my friends 

live here. Well, maybe not friends, but the people who invite me to cocktail parties and dinners. 

When you walk around Knightsbridge, you’re struck by how few of the homes seem occupied. That’s because these aren’t homes in the traditional sense; nobody really lives here for more than maybe a few weeks a year. They’re bank vaults, not literally stuffed with cash, but their main purpose is to be a store of value. Russian oligarchs, Middle-Eastern royals, Chinese billionaires, and other assorted global 0.001%-ers hailing from politically unstable countries buy these houses as a way to move capital out of their home countries and onto UK soil, where the rule of law reigns supreme. They can sleep well at night, knowing there won’t be a revolution and HRH Elizabeth II won’t suddenly confiscate their property in some power grab. Of course, the delicious irony in all of it is that they don’t actually own their property! They might have paid for the house, but the land it sits on, more likely than not, is owned by some duke or lord whose great (times twenty) grandfather either bought the land in the sixteenth century or was gifted it for his heroics in some esoteric imperial battle. The descendants own these large swaths of London and collect lease payments from the homeowners every ninety-nine years for the right to keep their bank vault on the descendants’ land. Our mews and the surrounding properties sit on this kind of leased land. 

Caroline is standing outside the house when I arrive from my run. She’s bent over and untangling the dog from its leash. 

“Morning,” I say. The dog looks much more excited to see me than my wife, its tail wagging and neck jerking from the leash’s hold. I kiss Caroline’s cheek and reach for her hand to take the dog’s leash from around her wrist. 

“You’re back,” she says, her pin-plucked brows raised in annoyance at me. 

“I can walk the dog with you,” I say. We walk the dog in mostly silence. 

Caroline likes to be outdoors doing errands and walking the dog. It’s her way of relaxing. One of our neighbors once asked her if she was a dog walker. Caroline is not the type to be offended by such a misunderstanding. She takes pride in her way of being at ease with people. She’s never been drawn in by the hype of Knightsbridge or glorified the latest Saint Laurent runway piece. 

We stop outside the posh Waitrose grocery store. This is where I used to see Lindsay Lohan loading up on bottles of French wine when she was living in London. Caroline goes into the store because we’re out of milk and coffee pods. 

I wait outside the neighboring pub with the dog. It’s a place called The Star Tavern, where even the window washer, who is out that morning, knows me. 

The pub is discreet, tucked away down another little alley, the Belgrave Mews. It’s comfortable. Dogs are welcome. My dog thinks it’s going inside this morning, and he trots up to the door as if he owns this place. 

“It’s closed, little guy,” I say to the dog. “But I’ll be back a bit later with Elias,” I add. And yes, believe it or not, I, too, talk to the dog. 

I cup my hands around my eyes and press them against the window of the pub to get a better look at it in the morning. It’s one of my favorite spots. You walk in the main entrance to a small bar with good beer. There is a dining room to the left with stained glass windows, a library upstairs. It has a fireplace. You can sit at the bar, on barstools, which is rare in London. I like to meet Elias there on Sundays to get a jump on the markets. We talk about our positions and our risk. We sit at the table under the chalkboard. 

The Star Tavern has a long history. Its menu, which features the best fish and chips in London, tells you the story: During the ’50s and ’60s, it served as the hangout for London’s inner circle of master criminals, who quaffed Dom Perignon here alongside stars of the era such as Diana Dors, Peter O’Toole, Albert Finney, and Alexander Korda the famous film director. This is where most of the planning took place for what was then the 

century’s biggest heist: the Great Train Robbery, netting the eighteen-man gang £2.6 million, about £40 million in today’s money. 

After a tip-off from a bent solicitor’s clerk that up to £6 million was going to be on a Glasgow to London mail train on August 8, 1963, Bruce Reynolds, a well-known and successful robber, contacted Buster Edwards, who was part of the South Coast Raiders train-robbing gang, and the plan was formed. 

Reynolds, who coordinated the robbery, regularly drove his Aston Martin from his Streatham home to meet Edwards, and one or two other members of the gang, in The Star to go over details during the run-up to the robbery. Four was the maximum number of gang members who met in public at any one time, in case the police were observing them. Reynolds’s friend Terry Hogan introduced him to The Star following the Eastcastle Street mailbag robbery of 1952, in which they both took part. Reynolds felt he’d broken into the upper echelons of the criminal fraternity and remembered meeting many of the gentlemen robbers of the time, such as Peter Scott. It was Scott who stole jewelry worth $200,000 from Sophia Loren and who came here and said, “I hear poor Sophia has been robbed,” before getting out a huge roll of bank notes. Reputedly, he always stole a pair of his victim’s underwear at the same time. 

The landlord was a larger-than-life Irishman called Paddy Kennedy, who swore at his customers regardless of who they were or what they were. Reynolds reflected years later that “the posh drinkers”—the stars, the debs, the escorts—found it most entertaining hanging around with known criminals and being sworn at by a landlord who had no regard for their status or wealth. Occasionally Kennedy would single out a customer for the special treatment of curses and insults the whole evening, much to the amusement of the entire bar. After the robbery, Reynolds fled to Mexico, where he lived the playboy life for five years, spending his entire £150,000 share of the robbery, before returning to the UK. When the gang was 

eventually caught, he was sentenced to twenty-five years in jail but eventually only served ten. 

This is all fitting for the area, for Knightsbridge. Very few of the people who live here earn their money. Most got theirs through a heist. But they’re all being put to shame by the new era of QE, _the greatest robbery in history,_ wealth inequality on an epic scale. 

I look down at the dog, which has coiled himself up in a shaft of sun. Caroline comes out of Waitrose with much more than the coffee pods. She’s fallen victim to all the chocolate buttons and Cadbury Christmas trees they’ve strategically lined up by the tills. The dog gets up as soon as he hears her voice. 

“Sorry, it was a mad house in there,” she says. “And I got some snacks for Elizabeth. I have to go to Surrey to pick her up for the end of term.” Caroline knows not to ask me if I’m around to tag along for the journey. She’s stopped asking questions with answers that might disappoint her. 

We walk back with the dog and the chocolate, taking the long way past Harrods. Harrods is probably the biggest winner of the extraordinary wealth that settles in Knightsbridge. Once these guys get the money to London, they lose their minds. There’s no _Goodfellas_ regulator, no one with any discipline to make sure they don’t buy the pink Cadillac or the fur coats. Once they get to Knightsbridge, they go crazy. They buy the million-dollar Rolls-Royce. They give the wife the Harrods black card, the mistress too. Watch them all go crazy. Just walk inside Harrods and watch it happen, people using cash to buy Kelly Birken bags and truckloads of Chanel. But at least they’re spending. At least there’s a trickle-down effect. Believe it or not, this is more effective than QE. 

By the time we get back to the house, the dog is in Caroline’s arms. My driver’s waiting outside the mews to drive Caroline to pick up Elizabeth at school. I put away the groceries. I find it cathartic to remove the coffee pods from their packaging and place them in neat stacks inside the pantry, the same way I color-code my shirts in my closet. 

## ***** 

Elias and I meet at the pub after dinner. We are preparing for the week ahead. We have sixty-four days until the Iowa caucus, the real beginning of the presidential election. The early votes are still a jump ball. It’s possible that the four early states could be won by three or even four different Democratic candidates. No candidate has captured the imagination of voters. The election will be a catalyst for markets next year and between the typical year-end madness and our need to ramp up more investment ideas for next year we need to get focused. 

I sit down and start with some small talk. 

“You’re typing like a madman. Who are you texting?” 

“The three girls from Vegas, Boss. We have a group chat.” 

“What three girls?” 

“Hey, what happens in Vegas, stays in Vegas.” 

“Anything else that needs to stay in Vegas you want to fill me in on?” 

“Nah, my lips are sealed.” 

“Speaking of Vegas, I’m starting to get a bit worried about Jerry.” Elias just looks at me. He doesn’t want to commit to any answer quite yet. I prod more. “I know he has been stressed, but he’s getting more erratic, if you know what I mean. Have you seen anything?” 

“Well, nothing big but there was some stuff I didn’t like.” 

“Like what?” 

“We were out with a sales guy a few days ago. Jerry seemed pretty quiet for most of the dinner. Then he disappeared to go to the bathroom. He came back all fired up. More energized, more talkative. I ended up going home but heard that Jerry and the broker were out ‘til 3 a.m. They went to Cirque and the Sportsman. I think Jerry is having a hard time calling it a night, and you know these sales guys. They either feel obligated to entertain you or hope they can get you compromised so you have to do business with them.” 

“For fuck’s sake,” I say as I sigh. “Thanks for the color. Who were you 

## out with?” 

“The rugby player. You know, the guy who can eat glass.” “Oh fuck,” I say. 

It’s another thing that will be on my mind, another problem to dwell on, right as we hit a critical point in the markets. This week is going to be busy. ~~.~~ 13 These are two It begins with the ISM and market PMIs on Monday important sources on the health of the economy. They measure manufacturing activity based on a monthly survey of purchasing managers. The markets are looking for signs that the manufacturing downturn is bottoming out, that the Fed’s midcycle adjustment is working. But I tend to doubt it. Wednesday brings the ADP Research Institute’s employment report. We need to look for any signs that the labor market is cooling. Friday is payrolls. Payrolls always move the markets. They’re exciting days for people like us. 

The markets feel softer. They had their first down week in six weeks. Whether a coincidence or not, the S&P stopped going higher just as the Fed’s balance sheet shrank for the first time in months. It’s also December now. All the hedge fund guys will start to de-gross. Cut positions and hedge up. They don’t want to fuck up their years. Memories of last December are still fresh. The markets tanked. Lots of guys gave away their profits for the year. They know this year was a gift. The Fed eased three times and ramped its balance sheet. The ECB resumed aggressive QE. All the big boys—the Fed, the ECB, and the BOJ—are all back to expanding their balance sheets in unison like a beautiful harmony. 

In this business, people get paid based on year-end PnL. No one wants to put that at risk. Who needs the hassle? It would be much better to aprèsski in Beaver Creek or Chamonix. Fuck it. Cut your book and enjoy the holidays. 

Elias asks me how I feel about our performance for the year. “Okay,” I say, “but we obviously underperformed the broader markets.” 

“But Boss, we aren’t supposed to beat the index every year.” 

“In theory that’s right,” I tell him. “We are paid to deliver absolute returns. The problem is that investors chase returns, and if you underperform for a number of years, they forget about you. It’s easy to be forgotten on Wall Street.” 

“I understand,” he says. 

“It’s frustrating,” I tell him. As I go on, my own set of pent-up issues boil over. “The markets have morphed into a funny parallel universe. A twisted central-bank-driven reality. In the big public markets, people just chase risk. The markets are flush with cash. We just flood them with liquidity. The credit markets trade as if no one will ever default.” I tell him that sixty Ponzi schemes were unearthed this year by the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC). Sixty fucking mini-Madoffs running around stealing investors’ funds, the largest amount since the GFC. When the markets are flush with cash and people are hungry for yield, the fraudsters come out of the woodwork. They want their fair share. 

“Bailouts and monetary policy have stunted the creative destruction process in the capital markets,” I go on. “Markets can’t go down 5% without handwringing. And so, central bankers cut rates and buy assets, thinking they’re being the good guys, the saviors of the world … arrogance unchecked, naïve compassion. But at what cost? Assets go up in value, but wealth inequality increases. Capital flows to unproductive parts of the economy. The American dream withers on the vine. Underlying problems get pushed into the future for someone else to deal with. It’s an impossible tide to reverse until something really big breaks.” 

I take a breath and keep going. “And on the flip side, these same policies are causing a huge gutting of the middle class. That’s where people are getting ripped to shreds. Thrown head-first into the fucking woodchipper. And we are falling farther and farther behind in the areas that matter, areas like education.” I tell him that the USA has fallen to twentyeighth place in education. “That’s a joke. If that were our performance in 

the Olympics, there would be outrage. We need to get back to real free markets with even playing fields, real social mobility.” 

You can tell he’s taken aback. In fact, I can literally see him moving away from me. This is quite a rant. I never lose my cool. Long ago, I learned to keep my emotions in check, never show them. But I just cracked. He asks me what I think about next year’s election. He says he doesn’t understand American politics, but I think he just wants to get me off my tangent. “It’s too early to have a strong view,” I say. “So much depends on the strength of the economy in an election year. And anyways,” I tell him, “nothing will really change with any of the top contenders. QE and trilliondollar deficits will still be the name of the game.” My voice keeps getting louder. “There’s no more discipline. Austerity is dead. The Swabian housewives have lost. Education won’t get fixed. Inequality will get even worse.” I realize I’ve lost control again. I stop abruptly and look down. 

He asks me if I want a pint. “Yeah, I guess I could use one.” 

We finish our drinks and part ways. It’s getting late. I think back to my rant as I walk. I’m making the same fucking mistake again. I can’t be too sympathetic to the problems. Fuck this. I need to lose the compassion. I fucking know what the new rules are. I understand this new paradigm. I can get back on top. But I obviously need to more forcefully quiet the part of my brain that keeps focusing back on the problems and the long-term consequences. I gotta play the game better. If I want to win at the game, I gotta get over this. 

When I get back home, I find Elizabeth asleep on the couch, a reality show paused on the TV, and an empty bag of Doritos in the crook of my daughter’s arm. I carry her to her bedroom. I notice a lightness to her body, and a layer of pimples dotted across her face. I almost trip on the duffel bags outside her room. 

“Hey Dad,” she says as I place her on top of her covers. 

***** 

Caroline and I have planned a holiday dinner for the team and today is the day. We tell the group to meet us at the mews for drinks. Our house is deceptive from the outside. It looks like a cute little cottage, white brick with blue shutters and a blue door. Inside, it’s bigger than you’d expect. The owners before us dug down three floors. That’s what they do in London. You’re not allowed to build up, so you build down. 

You walk into a nice reception area that turns into a dining area and kitchen. It’s clean and modern looking, a lot of red mahogany, not in a stodgy member’s club way, though; more like the cabin of a nice sailboat. 

There’s a Warhol on the wall, one of Andy’s Mick Jaggers. There’s more expensive art as well, but I like Mick the best. My other daughter majored in art history and told me to pick that one out. She’s now gone off to live in Paris and works in an auction house. 

We meet here, the team and their guests. We have champagne and fancy canapés of peach and prosciutto, smoked salmon mousse, and cranberrygoat cheese. Caroline has made all of the food herself. I keep offering helpers to do this kind of thing, but Caroline insists on doing it all herself. Everyone is in good spirits. The Rabbi is wearing a holiday bow tie. Lifecoach is sporting a new Birkin. 

You can feel some friction between Jerry and his wife. She seems very midwestern and doesn’t quite fit with the Knightsbridge crowd. The clothes, her posture, her bag—they’re all off. She also carries a scowl on her face. I avoid her. It seems like negative energy to me. 

Elias has brought a date. She’s stunning and knows it. He seems proud. He knows that it will draw positive feedback. 

After drinks and the hors d’oeuvres, we walk from my mews to our neighborhood Italian spot, Sale e Pepe, on Pavilion Road. On our way, we pass Harrods. People are out in force, shopping for the holidays. It’s a parade of ultra-high-end cars, a demonstration of wealth that’s only fitting for a ten-year bull market. I’m not talking Porsche or Bentley. I’m talking 

gold-plated Rolls-Royce Phantoms, Lamborghini SUVs, and a Bugatti Divo. 

The Rabbi can’t help himself. “Can you imagine what it’s like when these guys walk into the dealership?” he asks. “Car salesman says, ‘A fine choice. I see you eyeing our new premium Rolls-Royce Phantom.’” 

Then the Rabbi shifts into his oligarch voice, deep, booming, confident. “I want three of them.” 

At this point, Elias chimes in, taking up the salesman’s role, “Great, great. Let me get my manager and we’ll see what we can do.” 

“Is my money not good here?” the Rabbi asks, laughing now. “Why do we need a manager?” 

“You know that sticker on these runs them to £3.3 million before VAT tax.” 

“Does that include the gold plating?” 

“Gold plating?” Elias says with a smile. 

As they’re finishing the skit, the Rabbi breaks into a song. “Oh Lord, won’t you buy me a Mercedes Benz? My friends all drive Porsches. I must make amends. Worked hard all my life, no help from my friends. So, oh Lord, won’t you buy me a Mercedes Benz? ~~”~~ 14 

You can feel the energy as we walk into the restaurant. The air escaping from the door is a blast of vitality and excitement. The place is jam-packed. We sit down, and I raise a toast to the team. I thank their spouses and partners for allowing them to work so hard. I tell them to enjoy the holidays. Lastly, I toast to a new year, new opportunities, and everything it will bring. “Get your rest over the holidays,” I say. “The markets will be hard next year, and we need to put in our best year ever.” 

It’s a good celebration, but I sense something is wrong with Lifecoach. She’s quiet and ducks out early. 

The restaurant is in a small, narrow space, and the tables are so close together they might as well be connected. It feels as if it’s one big dinner for 200. At one point in the evening, we start buying drinks for the people next 

to us, and they join our celebration. Clemente, our waiter, starts to sing. He’s famous for his singing, and he belts out “Volare.” We all join in. “Volare, oh oh. Cantare, oh oh oh oh.” 

## ***** 

December in London is a busy time. The lights are up, people are feeling festive, and holiday drinks are scheduled nearly every night. The Rabbi, Elias, Jerry, and I are standing outside The Footman. We are waiting for a group of people to arrive from one of our biggest counterparties. It’s their favorite place to meet, a famous pub here in Mayfair, right off of Berkeley Square. 

The group arrives, and the pints start flowing. They’re in good spirits. The last three months have been good for them. Trading volumes have picked up considerably, and people have been making money. They want to close the year on a strong note. They tell me that some of my competitors have really crushed it this year. _Fuck_ , _they beat us by more than I thought._ 

We stand outside the pub until closing time and then begin to say our goodbyes. As things are wrapping up, the Rabbi looks over. “Let’s go play some blackjack at Palm Beach Casino,” he suggests. Elias is late for a date, but Jerry and I agree since it’s just around the corner. 

We get to the casino a bit after 10 p.m. We head straight for the blackjack tables. 

By midnight, the Rabbi is on a decent run. Jerry has busted, and I’m bored, so we leave the table. Jerry and I walk to the bar, which is near the entrance. Jerry has had a lot to drink at this point, and the truth serum starts dredging up topics he would not normally bring up. 

“Boss, I’m struggling. My wife and I do nothing but fight. I don’t even want to go home right now. I know she’s just going to bitch at me.” 

“I’m probably not the best guy to give marriage advice,” I tell him. “It’ll get better now she has that job at NHS. It’ll be easier for her to meet people. 

Think about getting her some help with the baby. Let her get out and make friends.” 

He goes on, and it’s clear that he really needs to vent: “And did you see our new sales coverage over at the pub. She was giving me those little flirty tongue smiles. It’s been a long time since my wife gave me one of those.” 

“Ah, don’t make the mistakes I did. They become painful,” I say. I’m not really engaging with him and feel bad about it because he’s looking for advice. But I’m not sure I want my relationship with Jerry to turn into marriage counseling. 

Three vodka sodas later, for each of us, the Rabbi comes to the bar, carrying a wad of cash. The bar is surprisingly full for two in the morning. It’s full of attractive women, carrying expensive bags. It’s now really late, and I head home. 

***** 

I can use a quiet weekend get-away. Caroline has returned from a day-long yoga retreat, and I’m taking her to the Soho Farmhouse in Oxfordshire. We haven’t spoken since the night of the team’s holiday dinner. We used to talk multiple times a day, but that isn’t happening now. Anyways, I hope the weekend isn’t a complete shit show, because I was hoping for some makeup sex. That and catching up on my reading are the only things on my agenda. I want some quiet time to think about markets. 

The markets are acting odd. Last Friday, crude oil really dumped, down over 5%, not a good sign for risk. The S&P was down over 1%, the first decent decline in almost two months. Things are weaker, but GBP is on the move. It’s moving higher. Our options are in the money. The GBP broke above $1.30 this morning, a seven-month high. The fixed income markets are also acting a bit odd. In this environment you would expect to see the yield curve flatten. More uncertainty: stocks are squishy, and those ISM manufacturing numbers are weak. It’s now been four months in a row 

they’ve come in under fifty. It’s the worst set of readings since the GFC. The 2s/10s should be flatter. Instead of flattening, however, the yield curve has steepened over the past couple of days. The steepeners have been a home-run trade for us. But here we are in December, when there’s every reason to flatten the curve, and it gets pushed even steeper. This is good for us, but when a market doesn’t do what it’s supposed to do, we pay attention. We sense opportunity—or a problem. 

We are in the backseat of the car, making our way northwest to Oxfordshire. I pull out my phone and call Lifecoach. 

“Hey, what’s up?” I ask. 

“Not much. Just getting my errands done.” 

“I just wanted to check in. You seemed off at the holiday dinner. You left early.” 

“It’s nothing. You know I hate those things. I always feel like the third wheel. Everyone else comes with someone. I’m always just sitting there alone.” 

“Ah, I get that. You know you can let me know if there is something bigger bothering you. By the way, how is that LIBOR15 task force you’re working on? Any trades that we should be thinking about as a result?” 

“No, still too early. I’ll keep you posted.” 

“Okay, talk to you Monday.” 

Caroline and I are staying in one of the nice cabins on the lake at the Soho Farmhouse. It’s all very rustic. It should be a romantic weekend, but based on the looks I’ve been getting, I’m thinking that isn’t in the cards. 

She seems distracted. She spreads herself out on the bed and is reading something. For the entire car ride, she was on the phone to our daughter about filling out a school application, as if she were in her twenties again. 

“Everything good?” I ask. She nods in the closed-lip way that doesn’t invite a conversation. I walk outside and call the Rabbi. 

One of the reasons I love the Rabbi, and why he’s so good, is that he’s always on-call. I don’t think I’ve ever called him without him picking up. 

He gives everything to this job. It’s his purpose in life. He works so hard that by the time he gets home at night, he’s just a shell of a man. That’s probably why he’s so hard on his family. 

“I don’t love the way fixed income feels,” I say. When things are going well in the markets the bad stuff tends to sneak up on you. You’re used to people buying every dip. And then when things do turn, it’s a big problem. 

“Feels to me like everyone and their brother are in these fucking steepeners,” I say. 

“Yeah, it’s crowded,” he says. “I was at a top ideas dinner last night. Everyone is in them.” 

“We need to reduce. I want to cut.” 

“Okay,” he says. “I’ll work with E on Monday morning to start unwinding.” 

He goes on. “So, at this dinner, it was all these founder and CIO types. I bet the poorest guy at the table was worth $100 million. You should have heard the shit they were talking about. I mean, seriously, when I get floor seats, it’s still a big deal. These guys are buying their own teams.” 

“Yeah, I heard that they just bought that Italian soccer team,” I say. 

“How’s the Farmhouse?” he asks. 

“It’s nice up here, but I think Caroline’s upset at me.” 

“Buy her one of those big Chanel bags,” he says. And without missing a beat, he just keeps talking. “I don’t like these factor reversals. We gotta watch that.” 

“Okay.” 

“We also hit our target on GBP. I think we start scaling out.” 

“Good point,” I say. “Let’s stay disciplined.” 

I hang up and think about what he said. One of the new risks in the market are these quant and factor unwinds. Two months ago, the best and worst performing factors of 2019—growth and value—were hit by a quant quake even worse than the first one back in August 2007. This sent value 

stocks soaring as momentum and growth stocks dumped. The Rabbi is wondering if we are about to see another one. 

My mind starts to wander, mulling over the influence of the quant strategies on the markets. They’re a new force. Go ask Jim Simons. He’s the most successful money maker in the history of modern finance and an amazing European-style chain smoker. There is an old tale—who knows if it’s true or not—that Simons was visiting Goldman. Everyone was sitting in a conference room talking business when Simons pulls out a cigarette and lights up. Some midlevel Goldman VP says something such as “I’m sorry. You’re not allowed to smoke in here.” Simons gave him a quick smirk and a shrug and kept smoking. It was the ultimate power move. There are very few people on planet Earth who could do this. But there are very few people who have posted nearly 40% annualized returns over thirty years, after 5 and 44% fees. Before the fees, his fund delivered an astounding 66.1% annualized return. 

The combination of quants and QE has changed the markets and been toxic for a lot of investors. Many of the best fundamental guys have taken their balls and gone home. Closed up shop. But that’s the game. Stay relevant or get replaced. I should know. And you know that the technology and data are just kicking into high gear. It will get more intense. You can just see Jeff Bezos using Alexa to listen in on guys such as Druckenmiller. I smile as I imagine a scenario. I have the ability to completely retreat into my own thoughts and totally tune out the world. It’s good entertainment. 

Druck: “I’m thinking of adding some more gold.” 

Alexa: “Hey, Jeff, Druck is buying some gold.” 

Druck’s Analyst: “Hey, Druck, gold just popped $2 bucks, some big orders going through.” 

Caroline yells out the door, asking where I am. Since I’ve been on my call, she’s gotten dressed and ready for dinner. I walk in, grab my jacket, and we head over to Pen Yen for some sushi. 

Caroline is on the keto diet, so she asks for her rolls to be wrapped in 

lettuce instead of rice. The one thing she does seem to buy into is all the fitness hype. 

“Doesn’t that defeat the whole purpose of sushi?” I say, not trying to start an argument but trying to make conversation. She still seems distracted and doesn’t appear to hear me. “Will you get your head out of the clouds?” 

“Me? Looks like somebody finally knows how it feels,” she replies. 

## ***** 

Monday morning, I get to work early to sit down with Jerry, Elias, and the Rabbi. We’re updating our global growth models. The overall mood of the group is good. The last ninety days have been good to us, and the guys know the bonus pool will be healthy. In this business, most of what you make is in your bonus, and people begin to obsess about their number. I like to give out bonus numbers right before the holidays, so I know the team will be on their best behavior for the next week. But now, the Fed released some new data that I want to make sure is reflected in our work. 

“I emailed the Excel with the data series and brought the Street reports on the release,” says Elias. 

“Jerry, have you gone through it all yet?” I ask. 

“Yes, bottom line is that the bottom 50% of Americans are getting crushed. Wages are stagnating. They’re taking on more debt just to stay even.” 

“It’s a good reminder,” I say. “It’s easy to forget that two-thirds of the people you see walking down the street are living paycheck to paycheck.” 

“Yeah, I guess I’m lucky I got to London,” Elias says. 

“What do you mean?” Jerry asks. 

“Not much opportunity out there,” Elias says. “At least I found a good seat.” 

Jerry interjects. “I was reading about some new social mobility index. 

It’s put together by the World Economic Forum to measure social mobility. It shows that most people are screwed.” 

He’s right on this. For most people, their opportunities in life remain bolted to their socio-economic status at birth. The index measures eightytwo countries. It looks at whether you moved up or down compared to your parents, whether or not your kid has a better life than you did. 

“Yeah,” I say, “the US isn’t even in the top twenty countries. We are middle of the pack. Fucking twenty-seventh. You’re now more likely to move up economically in the Czech Republic.” 

“Wait! Seriously?” Elias asks. 

“Yeah,” I reply. “The American fucking dream. Horatio Alger wouldn’t have a shot these days. Rags to riches doesn’t work. And Ray Dalio said something similar. He was doing an interview I was watching over the weekend. He said that over a ten-year period he studied, America’s lowestpaid workers had just a 14% chance of rising to the middle class.” 

“Wow!” Jerry says. “The good ol’ US of A used to be the best at that.” 

The Rabbi gives him a cross look. This is the look he gives people when he knows they’re wrong, when he’s about to call them out on something. He turns his head in a half-cocked way and crumples his nose to make his eyes look like lasers. “It was all just bullshit. All propaganda. If you look at the data, I’m not sure it ever really existed.” 

Jerry looks perplexed. “What?” 

“Yeah,” the Rabbi says. “Ask Boss.” 

Jerry looks at me. 

I pause to think about how I’ll reply. It comes out in a low voice, almost a whisper. “Well, there are lots of ways to define the American dream. If you’re talking social mobility, it’s an overstatement of the facts at best,” I tell him. “The Rabbi is right.” 

As I say this, Lifecoach wanders into the room for our morning meeting. I decide to save the rest of the conversation for another time. We are in the small conference room off the main trading floor. The Rabbi has taken a 

seat across from Elias; Lifecoach is standing by the door. I walk to the far side of the room to grab a fresh cup of coffee. 

Elias is talking to the Rabbi. “Good call. SoftBank is a mess. You think we cover some more of the short?” 

“Yeah, keep going. It feels like there’s another big buyer, so let’s close it out.” 

“I can take care of that,” Elias says. “Anyone want to make a bet on payrolls? I say 200K. No one is factoring in the end of that GM strike.” 

The Rabbi jumps on this. “I’ll say 235K. Here’s my twenty quid.” This is how we play the game every month. We all write our initials and payroll guess on a twenty-pound note. Closest to the print wins the pot. 

I walk back to my seat and pass out my agenda for the meeting. It’s a series of bullets I want to discuss. We go over the ECB balance sheet and negative rates and cover European financials, which are a total shit show. Tech valuations, however, are promising. As usual, the Fed meeting is hanging over us, and I suspect more QE is to come. Everyone agrees. We finish, and the group heads out to the desk. 

Trading desks are unique animals, the finance world’s version of the trench or foxhole. They were the original open-concept seating plans. I’m the only one with an office but spend most of my time in my seat on the desk. Next to me is the Rabbi and next to him is Lifecoach. Immediately behind me is Elias, and Jerry sits next to him and behind the Rabbi. Trading desks are designed so that there is no privacy. Everyone hears every conversation and knows exactly what the others are doing. There are very few people in the world I would want sitting this close to me. And trading desks are places where either you know you belong, or they scare the hell out of you. That’s intentional. They begin to weed people out before they even sit down. Something about them strips people’s decisions and actions down to basic instincts. You see straight away who would be willing to stab you in the back and watch you bleed out. 

Trading desks offer a unique type of camaraderie, in part because you 

know everything about the people around you. They allow for a level of shit-talking that you rarely find anywhere else, except, maybe, in a sports team’s locker room. Trading desks also have their own language. 

“Boss, this is ugly. More bull steepening in rates. Reds and greens are +6 to +8 ticks, while we’ve seen relentless central bank buying in the front end. Dealers are saying we’re about to hit a wall of gamma in SPX. Credit moving wider, but rates seem to be sniffing out a strong number. Everyone’s talking about repo. I don’t like the way things feel.” 

“E, I know you’re worried, but we need to get through payrolls tomorrow.” 

Bang! Payrolls is bigger than expected. 266K jobs. Another whipsaw. This will drive the equity markets higher again. Stocks rip back, back to the highs. Global market capitalization is now almost 100% of global GDP, Warren Buffett’s favorite bubble indicator. Everything is overvalued, another omen, another sign we’re in a bubble. No wonder Buffett is sitting on $130 billion in cash. He’ll be patient. He’ll wait for things to crack. 

The markets now expect the Fed will inject at least $500 billion in liquidity by year’s end, $500 billion of more cheap funding. And this was all happening when a headline hits that former Fed chief Paul Volcker died. I have a mentoring session with Jerry tomorrow, and this gives me a good idea of what to cover. 

***** 

“Jerry, I want to run through the people who have headed the Fed. I know you know these names, but I cannot stress how important these people are.” We are sitting in the pub about to have lunch. 

I go on. “They are or have been the most powerful people on the planet and most people don’t know anything about them. They’re more important than the politicians who get all the headlines. They’re the biggest players in the markets, and they make some of the biggest bets for society.” 

I hand Jerry a chart I’ve prepared. It’s a timeline of the last forty years, showing who was the Fed chair at the time, and stock market returns over the entire period. There’s also a shaded section showing periods when the US economy was in recession. 

“I’ll start with Paul Volcker, who died yesterday and who is the first one I can remember from my childhood. He was chairman of the Fed under Jimmy Carter and Ronald Reagan from ‘79 to ‘87, back when you could work for both a Democratic and Republican boss. It was a messy time. America was dealing with oil shocks, the fallout from the Vietnam War, the end of the Bretton Woods system, and then massive tax cuts. Inflation spiked when Nixon decided to leave Bretton Woods, the gold standard. The US dollar’s status as the reserve currency of the world was in serious doubt. Investors were convinced that inflation would never come down and were dumping Treasuries at any price. They bought as much gold as they could. Volcker made a huge bet to fight inflation. He didn’t just hike rates; he fucking ran them as high as he could—to over 20%. This tanked the economy in the early ‘80s, and unemployment skyrocketed, but it beat inflation. In hindsight, he said he didn’t know if it would work. It was all a huge bet. He always hated the banks. He thought they were all greedy and couldn’t be trusted. He wasn’t a believer in QE either. He didn’t think a free lunch existed. He was a believer in austerity and in living within your means. He was basically the last central banker to raise interest rates, and he got President Carter fired as a result. He was the last guy who had the stomach to deliver bad news and take appropriate action. You don’t see stuff like that anymore.” 

“Yeah, can you imagine someone doing that to a guy like Trump?” Jerry asks, with a smirk. 

“After Volcker,” I say, “things took a different turn as if they were playing a role in a Greek tragedy. Did you ever hear of _deus ex machina_ ?” “I guess I remember hearing that, but remind me.” 

“It means ‘the god from the machine.’ At the end of a Greek tragedy, 

when the problem was too big to be solved, they just dropped a god out of nowhere to fix things.” 

“Ah, right,” Jerry says. 

“That’s the role the central bankers took on. The minute the short sellers think that they have the market by the balls, central bankers and politicians go into _deus ex machina_ mode and they run them over. Makes you think you’re going crazy ‘cause it’s all so out of line with the fundamentals. But you gotta play the game.” 

They called Alan Greenspan the maestro. He was chair of the Fed from ’87 to 2006. He had a cool background. Born to Hungarian and Romanian Jews, he went to Juilliard to study music and then to NYU for economics. He wrote his dissertation on a housing bubble. He changed the narrative. He didn’t want any more recessions. Who can blame him? Recessions suck. Back in the early ‘80s, when the farmers drove their tractors to Washington, they felt real pain. They were hurting. Lower for Longer seemed like a good plan, a way to keep the juice flowing. And when Lower for Longer didn’t work, he invented the Fed put to give people confidence to buy the dip. 

“The Fed put was his doing?” Jerry asks. 

“Yeah, it was when he cut rates in 1987 in the middle of the tightening cycle.” 

“Ah, got it.” 

“This was also the start of the greatest bond bull market in history. That period, from the late 1980s onward, has been dubbed the ‘great moderation.’ There were no major recessions and things just chugged along. He was also a bit of a funny guy, not funny in the laugh-out-loud way; funny in the way he communicated. He liked to speak in puzzles, in riddles, like: 

‘Taking steps to increase our national savings through fiscal action to lower federal budget deficits would help diminish the risks that a further reduction in the rate of purchase of dollar asset by foreign investors could 

severely crimp the business investment that is crucial for our long term growth.’ 

All he needed to say was ‘We need to save more.’” Jerry laughs. 

“Anyways, it was also a period when wealth distribution started to change. The rich were taking more and more. It was small dollars in those days, but it was the beginning of where we are now. People used to say that ‘it,’ meaning the Great Depression, could never happen again. Things were different this time. Capitalism had won. But the greed was there, and the bubble was building. Did you watch that bathtub scene I told you about in the _Big Short_ ? Wall Street was getting greedy, and it was all about to pop. Greenspan was lucky. He got out at the top. He retired in 2006, before it all fell apart. It all went to hell soon after.” 

“I didn’t realize he got out right before the mess.” 

“Yeah, he got lucky,” I say. “The guy that stepped into the mess was Ben Bernanke. He was chair of the Fed from 2006 to 2014. They call him Helicopter Ben. He bought into the great moderation propaganda in the mid 2000s. He thought things were different this time, and he made some comments that still haunt him to this day. He said ‘subprime was contained’ back in 2007 before the bubble popped. I bet he wishes that he could take that one back.” 

“Anyways,” I continue, “he got the job right as the bubble was bursting. He cut interest rates from 5.25% to 0.0% in less than a year. But this was not enough. QE was next. He did the deal with the devil. But it’s hard to blame him. He had no experience with the mess he had to deal with, and he didn’t mean for it to become permanent. No one did. He earned the nickname for his comment that ‘the US government has a technology, called a printing press (or, today, its electronic equivalent) that allows it to produce as many US dollars as it wishes at essentially no cost.’ It basically refers to creating more of that digital currency and just dumping money out of a helicopter onto the streets. Think of Floyd ‘Money’ Mayweather 

pouring money over Conor McGregor, making it rain, just pouring money from the sky. That’s what it stands for.” 

I finish my beer and order a fresh pint. 

“Boss, how did Bernanke get comfortable with QE in the first place?” 

“Fair question and the answer is probably surprising. He studied the Japanese and wrote a couple of articles on it.” 

“Seriously?” 

“Yeah. Sort of like Greenspan writing his dissertation on a housing bubble. Folks were happy with Bernanke being in the seat because he had written his thesis on the Great Depression. He didn’t think the Fed did enough back then, and he was not going to make the same mistake. He had also done that other research on Japan’s bubble and thought QE was a good idea for the Japanese.” 

“Interesting,” Jerry says. 

“Then it was Janet Yellen’s turn. She was chair of the Fed from 2014 to 2018. If you met her, she would remind you of a really smart grandma. She also bought into the great moderation propaganda. She defended QE and kept it on autopilot but always said you couldn’t do it forever. You had to revert back to normal policy. This obviously has never happened. Wealth inequality ramps in this period, but it was hard to get mad at her. She was so even-keeled. You could see the negative trends in the data on wealth inequality, but she said they would get rid of it.” 

“It sounds like they wanted to do the right thing,” Jerry says. 

“Well, you can make a number of different arguments,” I reply. “One is that the world we’ve created is a metastasized pile of good intentions. Everyone gets a bailout, the markets aren’t allowed to go down, we should never have a recession. All fine and nice at surface level, but there’s a consequence to this. Asset inflation exacerbates inequality, and you end up with a zombie economy.” 

“So, what comes next?” Jerry asks. 

“Now we have Jay. Jerome Powell, polysci from Princeton and law 

degree from Georgetown.” 

“Did you ever see him at Princeton?” 

“I don’t think so. He’s older than me and was probably in one of the fancy eating clubs. I don’t think he went to Dial Lodge very much. Too much chaos going on there.” 

I continue. “He was nominated by the Big D. He’s _all-in_ on QE. He won’t have a choice. He’ll be known as the guy who created an even bigger bubble. His bubble will put Greenspan’s to shame. He can’t afford a recession and will need to create wealth inequality on an epic scale. Investors have already bid stocks to all-time highs. He’ll be forced to create massive amounts of digital money. He’ll need to roll the QE dice and hope it works, hope it keeps the greatest financial bubble from popping. Don’t worry. He has it under control. Don’t worry about the cracks that are appearing. Don’t worry about the inverted yield curves or that the CLOs16 are including more garbage loans. Don’t worry that the repo markets are breaking or that there were sixty mini-Madoffs running around. Don’t worry that the unicorns are tanking, that global debt is at $250 trillion, that wealth inequality is accelerating. Trust him. It will be okay.” 

I take a swig of beer. 

“Where will all of this end up?” Jerry asks. 

“Well, you probably just need to look at Japan,” I reply. “They started this whole experiment and are the farthest along. Just look where they are: totally fucked. Low growth, zero interest rates, zombie companies, and a massive balance sheet at the Bank of Japan. And they’re trapped now. The more QE they do, the weaker their economy becomes.” 

My phone rings. It’s the Rabbi. 

“Boss, news on the tape. Fed going to inject half a trillion more into the repo markets in the next thirty days. This means that by mid-January, the Fed’s balance sheet will surpass its all-time high of $4.5 trillion. Let the good times roll.” 

What a way to end the year. Find your best 3x leveraged ETF and let ‘er 

ride….

(PMI) compiled by HIS Markit.
