# _**Part 2: The Crash**_ 

## **Chapter 4 The Virus Spreads** 

## **January 2020** 

**1/1/20—** _Huanan Seafood Market closed by local authorities in response to illness outbreak._ 

**1/3/20—** _ISM manufacturing declines to 47.2, its lowest print in a decade._ 

**1/7/20—** _At a closed politburo meeting, Xi Jinping requests prevention and control work to address the new coronavirus._ 

**1/8/20—** _South Korea announces its first case of coronavirus from China: a woman who had recently traveled to Wuhan._ 

**1/10/20—** _Payrolls miss at 145K; unemployment steady at 3.5%; average hourly earnings at 2.9%, first time -3% since July 2018._ 

**1/11/20—** _China reports its first COVID-19 death._ 

**1/11/20—** _WHO announces: “The Chinese government reports that there is no clear evidence that the virus passes easily from person to person.”_ 

**1/15/20—** _Phase 1 of US/China trade deal signed._ 

**1/16/20—** _Trump impeachment trial begins in the Senate._ 

**1/16/20—** _Japan announces first COVID-19 case._ 

**1/20/20—** _First reported case of COVID-19 in the USA._ 

**1/23/20—** _Wuhan goes into lockdown._ 

**1/30/20—** _FOMC leaves rates unchanged, downgrades household spending description to “moderate” from “strong.”_ 

**1/30/20—** _WHO declares “global health emergency.”_ 

**1/31/20—** _President Trump bans Chinese travelers from entering the USA._ 

# **Month-end: UST 10s at 1.51%; S&P -0.04% (up first half; gives back in second half); Nasdaq +2.0%.** 

“T en, nine, eight, seven, six, five, four, three, two, one—Happy New Year!” The little speech bubble pops up on my phone and balloons come flying down the screen. Elizabeth loves those iPhone screen effects. Should old acquaintance be forgot, And never brought to mind? Should old acquaintance be forgot, And old lang syne? For auld lang syne, my dear, For auld lang syne, We’ll take a cup of kindness yet, .17 For auld lang syne 

The decade is over. The teens are done. It started with us coming out of the GFC. It ended with us lurching toward populism and polarization. The haves and have-nots became the name of the game. Income, opportunity, and the skills needed to succeed were all more consolidated than ever before. The USA became the world’s top energy producer. The cost of health care and college education soared. People dumped their land lines and spent more time on social media and streaming services. Netflix was the top stock of the decade. The millennials are now the largest living adult population in the USA. Finally, it was the decade that Americans got comfortable lighting up a joint and making it legal. 

Caroline and I are in a car on the way home from a party. She has her fingers to her temples. Champagne migraine. One moment she was talking my ear off about the psychology program she wants to apply to, the next her head is tilted back on the seat, her eyes shut. At the party, before the headache came on, she was happy. I could tell by the way she waltzed around the room, the charisma in her voice when she had new news to give about herself instead of me. 

The party was just around the corner on Belgrave Square—all you need to know is Upper Belgrave Street, kitty-corner from the Saudi Embassy—at 

the largest mansion on the street. When we arrived, I watched Caroline shrug her coat from her shoulders, eager to reveal the sequin dress that ricocheted to her ankles. 

The door swung open as we approached. We were greeted by a bodyguard with a clipboard and AirPods in his ears, a waiter balancing a tray of champagne flutes, and a woman helping guests with their coats. She took Caroline’s fur and hung it somewhere in a neighboring room, which I’d guess could probably be mistaken for a warehouse of Russian sable. 

“Upstairs,” is all the bodyguard said, pressing the AirPod harder to his ear as he muttered our name into his mic. 

Another server waited at the top of the stairs with a tray of fresh glasses. I could hardly get my bearing in the room because I felt I was in a surrealist film. An original Dali covering the entire wall opposite the foyer glared at me. The painting depicted a beach, glaring sand dunes, a mysterious sky, and a man, in the foreground, who looked just as confused as me to be in the room. 

When I was finally able to break eye-contact with the painting, Caroline was gone. She had already been swept to the other side of the room by a Kenyan princess, the mother of a friend of Elizabeth’s from boarding school. 

This was Knightsbridge at its finest, and the attendees were a who’s who of London. Oligarchs, billionaires, famous footballers, and a rock star. This was not WASP trotting territory on Park Avenue. This was the epicenter of global wealth: Indian businessmen and their heirs, Malaysian royalty, oil money, cravat-wearing Europeans with cigarettes. I’m the minority here: white, American, works on Wall St. 

Across the room, Caroline gestured at me with a finger. “Just one minute” (or two hours), is what she meant. I looked around at the opulence, and for someone I knew. As I scanned the room, I saw a Chihuly display that seemed on a par with the Bellagio. People were dressed to the nines. 

When I say the “nines,” I don’t mean it in a New York, tuxedoed way. I 

mean it in a more frivolous, fun way. If you didn’t know this crowd, you wouldn’t know which one was the rock star. The people looked young, healthy, and more diverse. One of my favorite guests had an armful of beads and bracelets, some of which looked like mere rubber bands. From appearances alone, it was hard to judge his wealth, except for those moments when he talked with his hands and I caught a glimpse of that $500K Vacheron Constantin wristwatch. 

The attendees were so rich that there was a level of understatement. They didn’t name-drop in the traditional way; they didn’t have to. They spoke in a different dialect, where money doesn’t even matter. It’s not part of the equation. You don’t depend on your wages. When you have a favorite restaurant, you don’t just go there; you buy it. 

The party felt as if it was just getting into high gear when we left. The host and hostess were very generous, with amazing parting gifts, and a fleet of Rolls-Royces waiting outside to whisk people home. I was thinking of inviting them over for dinner, but that would have to wait. My place was not ready for them. I needed to upgrade my art. The Mick Jagger would not have cut it with this crowd. 

We arrived home and Caroline headed to bed. I was left alone, so I turned on the TV. It was nearly 1 a.m. The girls were out at a variety of parties. It was pretty sad that they got home later than me. At least Elizabeth had been thinking of her dear old dad. Her text was probably the best part of the night. 

I realized I had had quite a bit to drink. I turn on one of the American New Year’s Eve programs that was just starting, and Blake Shelton was getting ready to perform. It wasn’t 2020 in America yet. My mind raced while I watched the screen, thinking about the world and the past year. And the past decade. 

Successive decades rarely look alike. Dalio is a big believer in this. There is usually a turn, some twist that people aren’t expecting. Sometimes it happens because people are still scarred, still shell-shocked from what 

happened before. Since they grew up in the GFC period, is it any wonder that millennials shy away from finance, just as their great grandfathers never recovered from the Depression? People expect these periods to be similar, but this is not the way they play out in reality. Ten years ago, at the end of 2009, we were coming out of the GFC, and investors were looking back on a decade when stocks had gone nowhere, value shares had outperformed growth, small companies had outperformed large companies, international shares had outperformed US shares, and emerging markets had outperformed developed markets. That was not what we saw in the teens. Total opposite. And the last time we were in the twenties, the 1920s, they were roaring. The 1920s are an important comparison because they ended badly, really badly, in the Great Depression. 

History shows stocks don’t always go up. Bubbles can be created, and they can burst. Assets can be over- and undervalued. Inflation can come and go. As we turn the corner into 2020, we have an interesting situation. Just as we did ten years ago, we are starting the new decade with markets at extremes. Sentiment is unquestionably stretched. But this time, it’s stretched in the opposite direction. This time, everyone is leaning long, building a bigger bubble. The bears are battered and bruised and, if still employed, are in an investor-induced hibernation. The year 2019 broadly felt as if everyone had finally got the joke: don’t fight central banks, don’t fight liquidity, don’t get cute; lay down your swords and play the game. And they played their fucking hearts out. Over the course of 2019, the global pile of negative yielding debt went from $7 trillion to $17 trillion. Staggering. If you lived completely isolated from the rest of the world and were given this fact alone, you’d likely conclude that WWIII had broken out and the good guys were getting their asses kicked. 

One of the biggest hallmarks of the teens was QE, and the focus on the asset channel, the wealth effect. That was the game. Push the markets higher. The world’s largest central banks all played a role: the Fed, ECB, Bank of Japan (BOJ), Bank of England (BoE)—you name it—all the big 

boys. They were all involved in the QE bandwagon. They made some people really rich. 

The 2010s is the first decade since 1850 that the USA didn’t experience a single recession. When will we see the payback? I have a lot on my mind as I head to bed. 

## ***** 

I’m meeting with the team. A new year calls for new ideas. Wipe the slate clean and start fresh. The best place for us to begin is with our structural views. We need to be pragmatic. It’s important to start the year off positive. There’s nothing worse than trying to dig yourself out of an early hole while it’s cold and dark sixteen hours a day. 

The year 2019 was an amazing one for the stock market. Stocks and bonds staged a huge run. They had their biggest gains in more than two decades. The S&P 500 soared 30%. Tech stocks were up 40%. The bond rally pushed the yield on the benchmark ten-year Treasury note down by three-quarters of a percentage point. Everything went up in price. Stocks are at all-time highs. Housing is at all-time highs. Unemployment is at a fiftyyear low. 

And all of this is still dependent on central bank support. 

The Fed had to cut rates three times in 2019 and grew its balance sheet by $400 billion over the last three months of 2019. Bloomberg reports that “the world’s 500 wealthiest people tracked by the Bloomberg Billionaires Index added $1.2 trillion, boosting their collective net worth 25% to $5.9 trillion.” Did anyone else happen to get a 25% raise last year? All because of central banks and QE. 

The first meeting of the year always brings a unique amount of energy, and you can feel it in our large conference room. The team has gotten some rest over the holidays, and I can tell that they’re chomping at the bit to get back at things. 

“Rabbi, you look like you lost some weight over the holidays,” Elias says. 

“Fuck off. You know I put on weight every year” is the reply. 

Most macro traders started their careers in fixed income or in foreign exchange. They’re not usually equity experts. Equity traders are always bullish, always optimistic on the world. But not macro. We are more skeptical, more pessimistic. There is a saying that bonds always get it right. We need to take our own look at what the world is telling us. 

The core of what we do is our investment process. For many macro investors, it can be boiled down to a simple sentence: What will the future state of the world look like and what is already priced by the markets? We do an amazing amount of work to try to answer this question. When we find an outlier, something that’s not priced by the markets, we get excited. 

I have asked the Rabbi to put together a deck and walk the team through his views for the upcoming year. He loves having the spotlight. He even dressed up for this. 

He turns to his summary slide. It tells us that the global economy is flatlining. The Big D’s trade war with China, while now resolved, still has the global supply chain heaving. Data has stabilized in the USA, but globally, it’s fragile; global GDP growth is the lowest since the GFC. Only 10% of the world is in expansion and world trade actually declined last year. That rarely happens. In the USA, industrial production is down, the third straight year-over-year decline. 

“Overall, this is not good,” he says. “And it’s all compounded by the debt that we’ve piled on. Globally, it’s up to $250 trillion. A quarter of a _quadrillion dollars_ . I’ve never actually said that word before. And that just counts the money we’ve spent. I’m not even counting the unfunded amounts. You know, stuff like pensions and medical care.” He goes on. “In the US, business investment remains particularly sluggish. Companies aren’t spending or investing. They’re just buying back their own stock. They’re funding those stock buy-backs by issuing more debt.” 

“Do you think this improves? Will companies start to invest and set the stage for real growth?” I ask. 

“I don’t. They’ve already pulled forward whatever capital expenses (CAPEX) they had in the pipeline in order to qualify for Trump’s tax incentives. Meanwhile, it costs next to nothing to issue debt; credit spreads are at record tights as every pension fund in America desperately grabs for yield. And if it couldn’t be any better, retail investors are finally wading back into the stock market en masse. Hell, if I were in the CEO seat, I’d probably think long and hard about buying back my own stock too.” 

Jerry jumps in. “Growth has all been driven by consumers. Consumption is what saved us.” 

“Yeah,” I say, “it’s what we do best. Shit, more than half of the US population spends more than their income. What about risk? What should we do with the book?” I ask. I know I need to push the Rabbi on this. This is his weakness. He’s much better at the negatives than he is at the positives. Lots of people are like this. He will tell you why not to invest, but he finds it hard to pound the table and tell you to take more risk. 

“Let’s start with what we know,” he says. “We have low unemployment and plenty of government spending. Monetary policy is supereasy. But the fundamentals are not good. Net net, I think the central banks want us to take risk. But you have to trust them.” He walks up to the whiteboard where he starts to make a list of positives and negatives. “This will all end badly, but not until they tighten, or we get some big credit event,” he says. “US stocks are expensive. Priced to perfection. We need to find cheaper expressions.” 

“What about earnings growth?” I ask. 

“Look, it’s fairly obvious what’s going on. You see growth rates coming down pretty much across the board. Take a look at Apple’s numbers. Annual gross profits are down over the past twelve months, yet the stock was up close to 75%. It’s all multiple expansion.” He goes on, “Not to be a grinch, but US economic data hasn’t really been playing ball with the green shoots, manufacturing recovery narrative.” 

“What do you mean?” Jerry asks. 

“It’s not showing up in the data. And do you know what’s really crazy?” 

“What?” 

“China now does nearly 30% of the world’s manufacturing, nearly as much as America, Germany, and Japan combined.” 

“Wow,” Jerry says. 

“Yeah, we exported our middle class to China so that we could buy more cheap shit from them. Crazy.” 

I call over to Elias. “E, what do you think are the popular trades?” 

“Boss, I think people will be shorting Treasuries, long steepeners, long equities, long banks, long UK stocks, long commodity currencies. Oh, and long tech versus emerging markets. That one has worked forever.” 

“Jerry, did you finish those models?” 

“Yeah, Boss. I retooled all of our central bank models.” 

“How about the real-time business cycle indicators? Any of the new data sets helpful?” 

“I’m still working on that, Boss.” 

“Let’s get that prioritized.” 

Jerry throws in a final comment. “One risk is with the election. If one of the communists gains some traction—Warren, Sanders, whoever—that will cause a sell-off.” 

The Rabbi cuts him short. “Do you guys realize that Netflix and Domino’s Pizza were the two best stocks of the decade? Isn’t that crazy? One a massive disrupter. The other has good tomato sauce. I’ll give good odds that Domino’s won’t repeat in the twenties.” 

“Elias, you think that the emerging markets can rally?” 

“The market will take a shot at it, but I doubt it.” 

“What is most mispriced?” 

The Rabbi points us to his last slide. “Investment grade credit’s a pretty good hedge here. At some point we will see a bunch of downgrades. Corporate debt has just exploded higher. Just a problem waiting to happen. 

Corporates are overlevered, and there will be zero liquidity in the next downturn. When that happens, it will be a shit show. Stay away from that stuff. A large part of IG universe looks like high yield to me.” 

“Definitely a variant view,” I tell him. “But probably underbaked until something happens that leads the market to agree. Let’s start small, it’s good value, but who knows when or if the market will come around to it.” 

The Rabbi keeps going. “You know, the real tail event will be a massive credit event or something that hits consumers, maybe a big bank or some other big GSE in China. That would be the Minsky moment.” 

We are in the middle of the discussion when I get a text from my sister. An old friend died. He was a guy I lost touch with, a cop when I met him. One of the top three fun people I’ve ever met, and a blue-collar guy through and through. We had some wild-ass nights. He was the self-proclaimed Asian sensation. A good guy and a good cop. He started on the streets and was promoted to the vice squad. Tough as nails. He was the kind of guy who wanted to be the first responder. I was once in a bar with him and saw him walk over to a guy he’d arrested on two different occasions. He bought the guy a beer, asked him how his life was going. And you know what? The guy respected him for doing that. It was one of the most amazing things I’ve ever seen. I would never have done that. I asked him later why he had done it, and he said he liked the guy, he had a good sense of humor. My friend had run into a drug house to make a bust. He saw the guy go running out a side door and took chase. He chased him down an alley, over two separate fences, into a dead-end. He finally caught him. The kid (he was a kid then) had a full bag of dope in his pocket. The kid looked at him with a straight face and said, “They ain’t my jeans. I just borrowed them.” My buddy couldn’t keep a straight face. 

Anyways, my friend ended up having to leave that job due to some complications. He jumped through a window. Eh, scratch that. Actually, he jumped through the window of my lake house on a night when I was throwing a big party, a total blow out. It’s a long story, and it really wasn’t 

his fault. He was just caught up in a wave that was more powerful than he was, and his wife was in bed with someone else. Caroline wasn’t there that night, and this is where it all started to fall apart between us. As Bob Dylan says, “I’ve always been the kind of person that doesn’t like to trespass. Sometimes, you just find yourself over the line.” Anyways, my friend lost his job as a cop as a result. After that, he worked to make ends meet. He smashed bags for the airlines and worked toward a pilot’s license. And then, when things got a bit better, he had a fucking stroke, a brain bleed. He wasn’t even fifty years old. Really cruel stuff for a guy with two daughters. 

I tune back into the discussion. The Rabbi is still talking. “We gotta watch for more dollar weakness. It’s correlated to current account and budget deficits. As deficits continue to widen, the dollar will go lower. I think we buy some euros.” 

“What happens in the next recession, Jerry?” I ask. 

“Deficit spending would need to go to something like 12% of GDP. It will be scary. Who will buy Treasury bonds then?” 

“Yea, but not today’s problem,” the Rabbi says. “Greek ten-year yields are lower than those in the US. There are still plenty of buyers. But you know how liquidity goes. You wake up one day and it’s gone. It’s never there when you need it.” 

I throw out a comment to the group. “Any views on how we’re set up?” 

“I still hate SoftBank but think we should trim the position,” the Rabbi says as he reaches for a bagel. “It has moved a lot and some value guys will start to step in. Did you see that Uber’s cofounder cut his stake in the company by more than 90%? He sold more than $2.5 billion in stock at the end of the year. Nothing builds confidence in a company losing billions a year like seeing its founder and former CEO sell 90% of his shares in the first three months after lock-up expiration.” 

Elias chimes in. “We need to key in on earnings. For the rally to continue, we need some earnings growth. There was no growth last year, 

and the long/short guys I talk to are not feeling optimistic. We need to see how this plays.” 

“I know I’m beating a dead horse, but my pet peeve,” the Rabbi says, “is business investment. We’ve got to see this improve. It’s not just slowing; it’s headed toward negative territory. That’s not good.” 

“I called around to our top guys,” Elias says. “They all think the biggest support for the markets are the Fed and the buybacks. If either changes, we have a problem.” 

Lifecoach chimes in. “I pulled up our notes from last year. This is nuts. Last year, the dots called for four hikes and for balance sheet reduction to be on autopilot. What a change? We got the exact opposite.” 

The discussion goes on for three hours. The moral of the story: stay long. The USA is still the world’s cleanest dirty shirt. The market will go higher. QE is still the name of the game. The bubble will get bigger. It’s still the biggest heist going. Even bigger than the one that just took place down the street from me in Knightsbridge. The thieves took $64 million in jewelry from British socialite Tamara Ecclestone, the daughter of former Formula One boss Bernie Ecclestone. Who the fuck has $64 million in jewelry sitting in some flat? I bet they planned that one in The Star Tavern. I should stop by tonight. They might be celebrating. 

## ***** 

So, the first full week of trading in the new year. Where do we stand? Pretty much where we ended. The PBOC cut rates by fifty basis points. The move will lower rates for the big banks and will release about $115 billion into the financial system. More easy money. The PBOC has now cut rates eight times since early 2018—oh, and markets are higher, new all-time highs. 

Over the weekend, Helicopter Ben made some posts to a blog. He’s foreshadowing where this is all going. He knows the central banks are stuck in a loop of more and more. His blog says we are headed to negative rates 

and further purchases of “private securities” during the next downturn. The Fed will just have to keep going to more extreme levels. 

And Mark Carney is warning about the same thing. Carney is the outgoing chief of the BoE, one of the biggest central banks. A nice Canadian guy. They say he’s a Toronto Maple Leafs fan and a pretty good goalie. Goalies can be odd-balls, very superstitious, but they tell the truth. At his swan-song press conference, he said we were heading toward a liquidity trap. I gotta respect the BoE. It has an incredibly straight-forward article about QE on its website. It’s illustrated with pictures of inflated balloons like a big bubble about to pop. At least Carney gives it to us in plain English, and with a sense of humor. 

The Rabbi and I meet at The Arts Club for breakfast. Tesla stock is already up 30% this year. People are just completely chasing risk. The dining room is packed, lots of people with pitch-books and expensive suits. The great thing about the Rabbi is that it’s not even 8 a.m. and he already looks rumpled. He always looks a bit rumpled. The rest of the room, however, looks polished and is out raising capital. There’s just too much money out there looking for returns. As we walk to our table, the Rabbi turns to me and says, “Look at all these douche bags.” He hates the look of anyone giving a sales pitch. He knows that most of it’s bullshit. 

Walking into The Art’s Club is always a bit of a scene. It’s the kind of place where people are constantly looking around to see who is there. They might be talking to someone, but at the same time are scoping out the room to see if there may be a more important target that they _should_ be talking to. We’ll sit out on the terrace this morning, which is possible in London in January. People glance at us as we walk the entire length of the dining room to get outside. 

We need to decide whether this rally is sustainable. I mean, how high can it go from here? Top economists expect US growth to dip below 2% this year. This would make the current economic expansion the slowest in the post-WW2 era, and manufacturing data is still acting like a total bitch, 

the weakest levels since the recession. As we sit at our table, the Rabbi and I decide we’re not going to chase; we’re going to hold the line for now. We have payrolls on Friday and will re-evaluate after the print. 

We get up to leave and see Elias walking into the dining room with one of our brokers. We head in his direction. As we walk up, he hands me his phone. His Twitter account is open: 

At the direction of the president, the US military has taken decisive defensive action to protect US personnel abroad by killing Qasem Soleimani, the head of the Iranian Revolutionary Guard Corps-Quds Force, a US-designated foreign terrorist organization. —US White House 

The Big D follows up, as usual, with a Tweet of his own: “Iran never won a war, but never lost a negotiation!” 

And this was countered by Iran state TV, which placed an $80 million bounty on “the head of President Trump.” 

All of this was announced via Twitter. 

The drone blew Soleimani and one of his top guys to bits. Soleimani was the world’s top living terrorist. He commanded something called the Quds Force, which ran military and clandestine operations outside Iran. 

WW3 is now trending on social media. The markets, of course, barely budge. Early in my career, I learned a good lesson from my boss. There was a skirmish in the Middle East and crude was moving higher. I was worried about our risk and how the markets would react. He laughed at me. He said it was people like me who kept him in business. “Markets don’t care about what happens over there. It’s been going on for 4,000 years.” 

We say our good-byes and walk to our office. I’m going through emails when I see one from Jerry. He needs to work from home on Friday since his wife started at the hospital and his sitter can’t make it that day. I’m not sure why, but I can feel myself starting to turn on him. I’m becoming more 

passive-aggressive and am sending him emails over the weekend on projects that he hasn’t finished, just to fuck up his Sunday afternoon. I get annoyed easily by anything he says, maybe because I’m not feeling any better about the markets and the recent data concerns me. And the markets are on fire, already up close to 3% this year, and tech is up even more, which means we are underperforming again. In this tape, to outperform, you can’t just be long risk; you have to be max long risk. No holds barred. This is just not something I’m comfortable with. 

It’s not that Jerry is doing a bad job. I just don’t like being around him anymore. And I still don’t feel the passion from him when it comes to markets. He’s becoming a distraction, and when I start to feel this way toward a person, it rarely changes. 

I stand up from my desk in my office to get a look at the TV on the trading floor. Payrolls comes in at 145K with most of the jobs added in leisure and hospitality. More fucking bartenders. More people slinging margaritas at TGI Fridays. We lost more jobs in manufacturing. Same ol’, same ol’. A shit report for income equality, but stocks are up on the news. Fuck, they just keep grinding higher. 

The weather in London makes this news even more depressing. Cold and raining slush. Hyde Park is soggy all the way through. It even got to Harry and Meghan and caused them to skip town, headed to Hollywood. The only bright spot is that Mo texted me from Vegas to say that Day Club auditions start today. This means that pool season is right around the corner. 

I should head there in March when the pools open. I could use a long weekend. 

## ***** 

It’s a new week, and I’m in the office doing a deep dive on the jobs report with Jerry and the Rabbi. I want to see if we can decipher any new trends. We are in the conference room next to the trading floor, and the door is 

open. As we’re working, we can hear the television on the trading floor. Davos is the big event of the week. Over 100 billionaires are there with $500 billion of combined net worth. Headlines are coming out of the event. Bob Prince from Bridgewater is on the tape, claiming we’ve seen “the end of the boom/bust cycle.” Even he is drinking the Kool-Aid. 

Now the big event. It’s time for the Big D’s speech. More America-first propaganda. He’s promoting its role as the world’s biggest oil and gas producer, and he goes on to tout his record on jobs, wages, and the stock market. “Today, I’m proud to declare that the United States is in the midst of an economic boom the likes of which the world has never seen before.” He has his trademark smirk on his face as he says that we “must invest in our people. When people are forgotten the world becomes fractured.” 

As we dig into thirty years’ worth of employment data, I’m thinking I would love to be able to walk through this data with him. I want to challenge what he has just said. There is such a disconnect between his message and reality. And it strikes me that the jobs market is more split than ever before. The middle-skill, middle-wage jobs are gone. Middle class is a dying concept. Working for one company your whole life and ending with a pension is the stuff of fairy tales. 

“Well,” the Rabbi says, “the data is pretty clear to me. The US economy is now a few high-skill, high-wage jobs and a ton of low-skill, low-wage jobs.” 

Jerry replies, “All the new jobs are in leisure and hospitality.” 

“Which all pay for shit,” I add. 

The Rabbi continues. “You’re now either a tech guy flying private or a bartender with no benefits. Take your pick. Either polish up on your math skills or learn how to pour a good margarita.” 

I add, “Most of these new jobs don’t even come with health care or benefits.” 

“Kind of sad,” Jerry says. “These aren’t teenagers.” 

He’s right. Most of the fifty-three million Americans working in low- 

wage jobs are adults in their prime working years. They’re fucking twentyfive to fifty-four years old. Their median hourly wage is $10.22. It’s no wonder that they gulp down every OxyContin pill they can get their hands on. 

“Now I get why everyone is on Instagram,” the Rabbi says. 

“Why?” Jerry asks. 

“You can make things seem better than they really are,” he says. 

He goes on. “The new American dream: Get a million followers and make it big. Five minutes of TikTok fame. My mom wanted me to be a doctor or an accountant. That was her dream. She still doesn’t know what I do. She thinks I’m a stockbroker.” 

“Your bedside manner would have sucked,” I say. 

“Yeah, and there’s no way I could get a million followers. I’m not photogenic enough. I’d be screwed if I were growing up today.” 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

The trading day ends and Jerry, Elias, the Rabbi, and I all head to the pub. This is for a mentoring session with Jerry. I usually do these alone with him, one on one, but I want the other guys around to help dilute down the time I need to spend with him. 

After our Vegas session, Jerry has some follow-up questions. He’s wondering how the whole financial system can be based on an unwritten promise and whether any of the big central banks have ever been caught lying. 

The Rabbi kicks things off. 

“Let me ask you a question, Jerry. How do you put a value on a government promise? I mean, not a contract, but a promise.” 

Jerry looks at him and shrugs his shoulders. 

“It’s one of the biggest things that most people just take for granted. After all,” the Rabbi says, “governments get caught lying all the time. Just 

think about the Pentagon Papers, or Iran and Afghanistan. It’s all so counterintuitive. To be a good politician you have to be a liar. Look, you have to tell people what they want to hear.” 

The bartender walks over and asks if we want another round. 

The Rabbi nods and continues. “Just think about the deficit spending in the US. We are in the tenth year of a bull market, and we are running a trillion-dollar deficit. In the old days, Democrats would be jumping up and down over this. Shit, Trump promised to eliminate it. Instead, they spend like drunken sailors and the deficit has doubled, a trillion dollars as far as the eye can see.” 

He goes on. “I don’t even know if a reasonable human being could even win an election these days. To win, you need to be a lyin’, cheatin’, human megaphone.” 

The Rabbi is off on a tangent. I jump in. I need to get this back on track. “Elias, you want to walk Jerry through our favorite example?” 

“Yeah, happy to. I could talk about this for days since I lived through this one. I was executing for one of the biggest risk takers in the market at the time. He and his whole fund blew up on this. Let me tell you about the SNB, the Swiss National Bank, and about euro/Swiss.” 

He goes on. “The SNB is the Western world’s most interventionist central bank. They’re competing with guys like us in the markets. They have traders who sit in an office near the lake in Zurich. Heck, they do so much trading they even have a trading desk in Singapore to manage their overnight activity. They’re basically just a big fucking hedge fund.” 

“Yeah,” the Rabbi says, “it’s so typical of the Swiss.” 

Elias goes on, “The SNB has a huge balance sheet for the size of the country. The SNB not only buys government bonds, like the Fed, but they also buy stocks. They own stuff like Microsoft, Facebook, Amazon, Johnson & Johnson, and Exxon. They fucking own nearly $4 billion of Apple stock. They’re one of Apple’s largest shareholders.” 

The Rabbi can’t help himself. He knows the answer but blurts out, 

“What the fuck is a central bank doing owing Apple stock?” 

Elias continues, in a slow suspenseful tone. “Central banks are supposed to be credible. They’re supposed to set slow and predictable policies, but not on January 15, 2015, not when the SNB suddenly announced it would no longer hold the Swiss franc at a fixed exchange rate with the euro. They caused massive losses for market players across the world, millions and millions in losses. The SNB ripped all of their faces off.” 

“Thomas Jordan, the top guy at the SNB, directly lied to the markets. He told everyone one thing and then did the exact opposite. He could not have been more clear. A bold-faced lie. They set the market up and then torched them.” 

The bartender returns with our drinks. 

“I remember the day well,” Elias goes on. “My boss at the time was blowing up. I saw it first-hand. Shock is what I remember. I walked outside our offices in Mayfair, right behind the Green Park tube stop. I saw people pacing, just wandering. There was nothing to say. Their careers were over. They knew it. It happens that fast. They had lost so much money. But the traders for the central bank had positioned to profit from the move. Some of my contacts told me that these pricks were doing high fives as it occurred.” 

The Rabbi can’t help himself. “It was quite the racket. They spent years creating billions of francs out of thin air. They’d take these francs and buy euros. This helped to keep the franc artificially weak versus the rest of the world—someone’s gotta buy those Rolexes you know. The euros then got swapped into dollars, and in a final stroke of either genius or insanity, the dollars into shares of Apple. All from nothing.” 

Jerry looks bewildered. “What?” he says. “They can really do that?” 

The Rabbi sets his beer down. “Yeap. I always look at their 13Fs. In March they owned $4.5 billion in Microsoft shares, $4.4 billion in Apple, $3.2 billion in Amazon, $2.7 billion in Google, and $1.6 billion in Facebook. I’m talking huge fucking size.” 

“Wow.” 

The Rabbi wants the last word on this one. “Tough to trust anyone. And in the game of floating paper currencies, it’s all a zero-sum game. People win and people lose.” 

“Yeah,” Elias says as he reaches for his beer. “I know a lot of people who lost their jobs in that one.” 

“Yeap,” says the Rabbi, as he takes a bite out of a Scotch egg. “These are the same central bank ass-holes that you’re now trusting with your futures.” 

It’s starting to get late. We have a last pint and decide to call it a night. I walk the couple of blocks into Knightsbridge. The rest of the guys head to the tube to start the journey home. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

Caroline is shaking me awake. “Get up,” she says. “You’re going to miss your train.” 

I never oversleep. Maybe I’m getting sick or just depressed. 

I run into the shower and then out the door. I’m headed to St. Pancras station to catch the Eurostar to Paris with Lifecoach. She’s on a committee that’s meeting there to discuss the changes to LIBOR. I’m headed there to meet with an old colleague to talk markets. 

We are sitting in the business section. In front of us is a couple with a new baby. It cries a lot. I’ve never liked babies, but now they scare the hell out of me. In the old days, they were an asset, cheap labor to help work the farm. Now, a big fucking liability. I see babies and I see dollar signs. They’re expensive and an enormous amount of work. You have to get them into the right schools, you have to beat a work ethic into them and make sure they have a passion. And you can’t just give kids a good work ethic anymore. It’s not enough. They need a work ethic and some capital. And if you have the capital to give them, you need to work to keep them out of 

rehab—all a big fucking headache. I’m giving Lifecoach my views when I realize that she has heard it before and is just glaring at me. I stop. 

The train pulls into Gare du Nord station. It’s always a bit chaotic trying to get a cab here. And you have to watch out for the gypsies walking around, or else you’ll lose your wallet. We finally find cabs, and I head out to meet my friend. He’s an equity derivative specialist. He has a small operation here in Paris, over by a place called La Féline, in the twentieth. He has a small group working with him. They’re always looking for some type of arbitrage. He tells me he’s disgusted. There is nothing to do in the volatility markets. Vol is too low and there are too many systematic sellers of vol. He tells me he has largely shut down his investment operation and will sit on the sidelines until we hit the next crisis. He says it’s all a problem waiting to happen. 

He says the only thing he’s interested in is some work he’s doing for a large hedge fund. They’re trying to arb out the insurance industry by buying life insurance policies on people who will die before the actuarial models predict. He compares it to the subprime market and CDOs: “I need to find the ones that are most likely to default, or in this case die earlier than expected.” 

He tells me about one of the areas they’re most interested in. He calls them “deaths of despair.” These are largely drug and alcohol deaths of Americans aged twenty-five to sixty-four. They’ve been on the rise since 2000. 

“It’s very unusual,” he says. “It’s the first time that life expectancy has actually declined in the US.” 

He tells me the data is strong and gives me an example showing that places where auto plants closed down have opioid-related deaths that are 85% higher than those in places where the plants remain open. 

“These people get laid off and don’t know how to retool themselves. They just end up drinking themselves to death or overdosing on opioids. It’s 

all kind of morbid,” he says while reaching for his croissant. “But I have to find something to do.” 

**==> picture [8 x 6] intentionally omitted <==**

The auction house my daughter works at is in Paris’s Saint Germain district. She has promised to catch me for a coffee before I return to London. That’s right. That’s her making time for me in her busy schedule. I call her from the cab. 

She’s waiting for me on the terrace of Le Café de Flore. She’s looking arty and especially Parisian in a black turtleneck and flared jeans. Her tortoise-shell glasses hold back her hair on top of her head. She looks startlingly similar to her mother, petite, small features. 

“Hi, Dad,” she says, standing to greet me. 

“What’s new? You look like you fit right in. And you’ve been promoted,” I say. 

“I’ve helped to broker a deal on a Klimt. Speaking of which, I think we should work on your collection,” she says. 

The only thing I never ask her about is boyfriends. Ignorance is bliss, I think, when it comes to that sort of stuff. 

My daughter doesn’t have longer to sit with me than the time it takes to drink a double espresso. She packs up her designer briefcase. 

“Remember you don’t need to leave a tip in Paris,” she says before scuttling off, leaving me there to people-watch. 

I direct the cabbie, in broken French, to my hotel, where I’m meeting Lifecoach for a drink. She grew up in Chicago. They have good hot dogs and good piano bars there. A lot of people say the pizza is good, but not me. I prefer Detroit style at Buddy’s. But their piano bars are top notch. 

We head to a place called Le Relais de la Huchette, which is one of the best bars. We sit at the piano. The guy takes a break, and we start talking politics. The Big D has set a new tweeting record today. Ten tweets while 

we’ve been sitting at the piano, and a total of 125 today. The impeachment hearings are in full gear, so it clearly has him mad as hell and taking it out on his Twitter account. 

“What did you think of the debate last night,” I ask her. 

“I was happy that Elizabeth Warren got in Bernie’s face over the ‘no woman can be president’ comment. These views are real. This is not made up. All of these guys think this stuff. I get fucking sick of it. You guys all say there’s a level playing field, but you know it’s not. And when a guy like that, a socialist of all things, lets it slip like this, he should be fucking crucified.” 

Lifecoach does not typically drop F bombs, so I can tell that this has hit a nerve. Maybe she’s feeling some of our underperformance stress as well. She rarely gets mad. But when she does, watch out. And the way she just raised her voice—half of the people in the piano bar can sense something is wrong. They can feel the tension coming from this side of the piano. The vibe has definitely changed for the worse. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

My cell rings in the middle of the night. It’s one of our Asian brokers. He knows not to call me unless it’s something really important. 

“Sorry to wake you but this is bad. Risk is getting crushed,” he says. “Asia is starting to freak out about this virus. Some new numbers are out and it’s spreading fast.” 

Fuck. Now I have a problem of my own. An outbreak of a new virus that began in the Chinese city of Wuhan has killed eighty people. We were only a couple of days away from month-end. We were up over a percent for January, a decent start to the year. And then this fucking virus. The market reaction is a classic global growth scare: stocks getting hit, bonds rallying, dollar and gold higher, EUR/JPY falling like a stone, credit wider, and crude and commodities getting smoked. Some even bigger moves under the 

surface: airlines down more than 5%, casino stocks getting hammered. And we even have a new curve inversion: the 2s/5s just inverted. The market is now pricing in more Fed cuts this year and saying we’re headed for a recession again. 

I call Lifecoach and rebook the first train back to London. 

The virus seems to be particularly nasty. Its symptoms sound like the flu, but it’s a viral pneumonia that attacks your lungs and causes problems with breathing. And everyone who gets it infects at least two or three other people. The Chinese central government is considering a lockdown. This is bad. The trouble with the Chinese is that if they’re admitting to eighty deaths, it probably means 800. They would not be considering a lockdown for something small. This stinks. 

We were long risk coming into this, and our portfolio is losing money. The S&P traded down from its highs to 3,245; crude down to $54 a barrel. The dollar is moving higher. The CBOE Volatility Index (VIX), also known as Wall Street’s so-called fear gauge, has jumped to 17, from a low of 12. Gold is trading at $1,580 an ounce. WYNN stock just gapped down $10, from $152 to $142. 

In a tweet, the Big D praised China for its efforts to prevent the spread of the virus. “China has been working very hard to contain the coronavirus. The United States greatly appreciates their efforts and transparency. It will all work out well. In particular, on behalf of the American People, I want to thank President Xi!” 

In this business, it’s important to start each year strong. Build up some PnL. Start to build a cushion and then increase your risk. We just gave back half of our month, and I’m not happy. The market is selling off because infections are skyrocketing, and it’s spreading fast to other countries. New numbers were just released, and nearly 3,000 people have now contracted the disease. So far, the vast majority still live in China, but we now have cases breaking out all over the world. 

We were too complacent on the issue. I’ve now been reading about this 

for two weeks on some of the conspiracy blogs, and we haven’t even talked about it in our risk meetings. Too much partying, socializing. We need to reevaluate. I call a meeting for the team. ASAP. I’m pissed off because I feel we missed this. I fucking hate losing money. We got sloppy. 

## **Notes** 

17 “Auld Lang Syne,” Robert Burns. 

## **Chapter 5** 

## **Risk Management and an Inflection Point** 

## **February 2020** 

**2/2/20—** _First COVID-19 death outside China (in the Philippines)._ 

**2/3/20—** _Pete Buttigieg appears to win the Iowa Caucus after count confusion._ 

**2/3/20—** _USA declares “public health emergency.”_ 

**2/3/20—** _ISM manufacturing bounces back to 50.9; eased US/China trade tensions likely helped._ 

**2/5/20—** _Trump acquitted by the Senate._ 

**2/7/20—** _Payrolls beat at +225K for January; unemployment rate to_ 

_3.6%; average hourly earnings to 3.1%._ 

**2/14/20—** _France announces first COVID-19 death within Europe._ 

**2/19/20—** _S&P peaks._ 

**2/23/20—** _Large swaths of Italy locked down in response to case surge._ 

**2/26/20—** _South America’s first case: Brazilian man who had been in Italy._ 

**2/26/20—** _Vice President Pence named head of COVID-19 Task Force; daily briefings._ 

**2/29/20—** _First COVID-19 death in the USA._ 

**Month-end: UST 10s at 1.15%; S&P -8.2%; Nasdaq -6.4%; dollar index +0.8%; oil -12.5%.** 

R ed lights flashing, negative numbers shooting at me, faceless people mumbling about short gamma and curve flattening, visions of people running for the exits, night sweats, visions of my boss taking away my access pass and ushering me out of the building: a PnL dream. On days like this, I wake up and the bags under my eyes are blown out like some puffer fish. They’re so dark I look as if I have two black eyes. PnL dreams suck. I stayed in bed until noon. I never do that. Luckily, it’s Saturday. 

PnL dreams are not unusual in this business. Back in 2008, I used to have dreams of CDOs exploding in my face like hand grenades. It’s crazy how quickly we forget the lessons from past crises. Already, we are stuffing these things full with more garbage loans. Heck, the Bank of International Settlements has already warned us that “the rapid growth of leveraged finance and CLOs has parallels with developments in the US subprime mortgage market and CDOs during the run-up to the GFC.” Just another omen. 

The dreams and the stress are all part of the chase for PnL. PnL is what gives you power on Wall Street. It gives you leverage. But sometimes PnL goes wrong. It’s amazing when it’s going your way. You feel like a king. You can do no wrong. When it changes, when it goes against you, you end up neutered, castrated like some stray dog. You worry that you’ve lost your touch. 

But the market stress is over for now. Apple and Amazon bailed everyone out with humongous beats, and the central banks stepped in with a couple hundred billion of additional liquidity. Jay was more dovish than the market expected, and China’s central bank injected a huge amount of dough. The markets ripped back to the highs, and the combination saved our month. We ended up +93 bps. Not bad. A decent start to the year. But it didn’t feel good. We got lucky and something is not right. Fixed-income yields are very close to levels that were deemed totally crazy last 

September, and the three-month/ten-year Treasury yield curve has inverted again. The market is telling Powell that it expects more easing. The fixed income markets are screaming, “Houston, we have a problem.” A big fucking problem. I send an email and tell the team I want to de-risk. I want to flatten out and re-evaluate. 

What keeps you in this business is good risk management. I might be wrong here. I probably am. Heck, tech still has momentum, good ol’ Uncle Mo behind it, but some really smart macro guys are saying that the market is underestimating this virus. Paul Tudor Jones is on the tape. He’s a legend. He’s worried about the virus and compares it to the outbreak of severe acute respiratory syndrome, or SARS. And people in the market have been saying that Brevan Howard traders are buying every front-end Treasury bond they can find. They think the markets are headed lower—way lower. 

Over time, macro veterans tend to develop a sixth sense about the markets and their portfolios. To a degree, it’s what separates the greats from the goods. It’s the secret sauce that investors and allocators struggle to identify through any sort of analytical lens. Something feels off, and you often can’t explicitly put your finger on it or model it out on a spreadsheet, but you know you won’t feel right until you take some form of action. Gotta move your feet, get risk to a level that helps you sleep. Soros always claimed he’d have backaches whenever the markets were about to turn; he’d wipe his portfolio clean. I have to imagine that after decades of being in the market, his biochemistry was somehow optimized toward assessing and feeling risk at a subconscious level. And then he has the conscious perspicacity to trust it. Malcolm Gladwell wrote that it takes 10,000 hours of dedicated practice to truly master a given pursuit. As far as this being applicable to the markets, I’ll take the “over” in any size. 

You have to have discipline in this business. Set stops on every position. Find the good 5:1 payouts, with multiple ways to win. They’re the ones you need in your book. One of my early bosses taught me that. He used to ask me the same questions every day on every position in our book. I thought it 

was insanity. But then I realized he was just testing my conviction, looking for any indication that my answer might be changing, even just a little bit. In the early days, it drove me crazy. Now, I think the best risk takers would be crazy not to do it. 

I turn on the TV. Talking heads are spewing on the election. The Dems arrived in Iowa for the first caucus, but it ended in a fucking disaster. Due to a coding error, an app created to count votes blew up. The results are now delayed, but they’ve been leaking out, and the best indications are that Bernie won it. And this is happening at the same time that Trump has been acquitted of his impeachment. He’s delivering his State of the Union Address, and Pelosi is on megatilt. It’s a full-on three-ring circus. Brains are melting everywhere. 

But none of it, not the news, politics, or broad markets, has turned to the virus yet. The virus has not yet caught the attention of the politicians. And equities are back to all-time highs. 

Only the fixed-income market seems to care. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

All this QE and easy policy hides problems. It sweeps them under the rug. Everyone talks about new market highs and Tesla stock, but that obscures the real view. Bubbles do that. Only the top 1% get the benefits of the markets. They’re the ones who own all the stock. Everyone else suffers. Today, a day we hit another new all-time high in stocks in a ten-year bull market, a major public pension system slashed benefits for retirees. The Ohio Public Employees’ Retirement System voted last week to cut health care benefits provided to current and future retirees to try and prevent the fund from plunging into insolvency. How about that for some of the harsh reality of the real world? You just lost your fucking medical benefits. 

I get a series of WhatsApps from Elias. 

“Another ninety-seven people died in China yesterday” 

“Total death toll to 910” 

“40,171 cases in China” 

“Another 700 globally” 

The number of people killed has now overtaken the total death toll for the SARS outbreak in 2003, which killed 774 people across the world. This is not good. What has caught my eye is that a journalist named Chen Qiushi, who had been doing critical reporting on the virus from Wuhan, went missing. It’s a bad sign when journalists go missing. This probably means things are worse than being reported. 

But fuck it. We’ve already de-risked and Wuhan is a long way from Knightsbridge. Here we are celebrating because it finally happened. We finally got Brexit. The UK has left the European Union after forty-seven years. Now they will fight for the next two years on how to split the spoils. I hate the idea of Brexit, but I guess it’s as good a reason as any to have a party. That, and the fact that GBP has been a monster PnL driver for us. 

We are going to celebrate the big day with a trip to Tape London, a nightclub. Bieber is in town to debut his new album. I’m more of a classic rock guy, but Lifecoach digs Bieber. She helps keep me relevant by giving me his latest stuff to listen to. 

We have a random group. The team is here, along with Caroline, a couple of our brokers, my yoga instructor, and a family friend from Beijing. My Chinese friend is from a wealth family in China and is still in graduate school here in London. She loves Bieber. She’s wearing a face mask, something called an N95. She looks like a freak, but her family told her that the virus is everywhere, and she needs to take precautions. 

Bieber is sporting a new look: bleached-out white hair. The place is crowded, and our table is right next to one of the Beckham kids. It makes me feel old, real old. Bieber walks out to the DJ area. He looks athletic. I like that he still goes out and plays pick-up hockey whenever he can. I wish I got to do that a bit more. I bet it’s tough for him to go back to London, not this London, his London. That’s a different one, near Toronto, where he 

grew up. They have great hockey there, but not many billionaires. I wonder if he can still relate to the people back home or if he has changed too much. 

Elias is in usual form, goading everyone at our table into shots of vodka, one after the other. I’ve always suspected that he pays the waitress to fill his own glass with water. I’ll catch him in the act one of these days. And the Rabbi is showing off his new loafers to my yoga instructor. He has more shoes than Imelda Marcos. It’s a bit fucking weird, but you learn to love the little quirks in people like him. 

Lifecoach is busy chatting with one of her former students from the graduate class that she teaches. Based on the expression on his face when he saw her, he didn’t expect to run into her here. 

Jerry’s blotto, standing on our banquette chanting, “Bieber! Bieber!” He thinks he’s being cheeky, but he’s really just being a douche. He’s not being his best self right now, and his wife looks embarrassed. She’s sporting a bigger scowl than usual. 

The night is in full gear, and Lifecoach pulls me aside. “I’m going to head home,” she says. 

“But it’s still early.” 

“Yeah, I know. But I’m sticking with my vow with these broker types. They’re all getting drunk now, and I’m sick of idiots like this pawing away at me and getting all handsy as soon as they’ve had too much to drink.” 

“Ah,” I say. 

“Plus,” she says, as she gives me a sheepish grin, “I think I’m going home with my student.” 

“Ha. Amazing. Make him bark like a dog.” 

“Tell Caroline I said good-bye. I tried to find her.” 

“She’s over with her friends from The Arts Club,” I say. “I’ll let her know. Ciao.” 

I look out at the scene in front of me. It’s like an ocean of champagne bottles. As that “My Momma Don’t Like You” song comes on, I think getting back to reality tomorrow morning is going to be a complete bitch. 

## ***** 

We’ve been underperforming since we took down risk and my stress level is in the red zone. And everything still feels a mess. Manufacturing is getting gutted in Europe even worse than in the USA. The data is collapsing back to GFC levels. The euro has collapsed. We hit a stop on our euro long and close it out. 

The virus is still spreading: nearly 65,000 confirmed cases, 5,000 new cases announced yesterday, and over 1,100 deaths to date. It even has its own name now: COVID-19. It’s nastier than originally thought. It can cause severe acute respiratory distress syndrome and multiorgan failure. The central banks respond: more liquidity. The PBOC is buying everything that isn’t nailed to the floor. And for good measure, the Fed also dumped in another $80 billion. 

Pandemics are tricky for the markets. They’re the type of exogenous shock that comes out of nowhere and can have dramatic economic consequences if not contained. The markets don’t care about the deaths. They care about how the bug will impact global growth and supply chains and consumption. 

Fallout from the virus now has the markets skeptical that growth will rebound. Commodities and emerging markets are getting drilled again. Basically, anything dependent on Chinese consumption is getting hammered. American exceptionalism and outperformance are back to being the key market theme. Wall Street is pitching new outperformance baskets of stocks that will thrive if people are quarantined. The Asian currencies keep weakening—you know, the Thai baht, Malaysian ringgit, Indonesian rupiah, and Korean won, and such stuff. Apple becomes the first major US company to say it won’t meet its revenue projections; the virus has limited iPhone production, and this is beginning to extend into supply chains. 

We are in our large conference room in the office, meeting to discuss the latest developments. 

“What’s the best hedge if COVID keeps spreading?” I ask. 

Elias replies, “This is classic growth scare, Boss. Pretty much sell anything. Let me poll the dealers and see if they have good ideas on structures.” 

“Focus on the weak links,” the Rabbi says. “Look, if people are locked at home, everything will get pounded, but stuff like airlines and hotels will really get killed.” He goes on: “Shit, I’m beating a dead horse here, but think about SoftBank. WeWork and Oyo will both be dead.” 

I begin to push the team harder. “Are markets over-reacting? Are we over-reacting?” I stand up for effect and begin to walk around the room. “We got too dour during ebola, and then spent the next six weeks digging ourselves out of the hole. It’s not a good look to tell investors we’re suddenly epidemiologists wagering their money on the path and rate of coronavirus.” 

“I don’t think so,” the Rabbi replies. “Too much uncertainty. Some Asian blogs I check are saying that this spreads much faster than ebola.” 

“So, you say fixed income will price more easing?” 

“Yeah, I think rates will rally, and Asia will be weak across most asset classes. The next two weeks are important. If it spreads to Europe or the US, it becomes a whole different proposition. That’s the key.” 

Elias jumps in. “I think that’s right. The Fed will talk up the virus at their meeting next week, plant the seeds they’re watching and prepare to act. That’ll keep risk trading okay, but if it spreads, people will sell risk.” 

I’ve sat back down, my head in my hands. I’m processing what they’re saying. “I guess these numbers are pretty terrifying. And everyone has been leaning long and are probably feeling a bit queasy here. Maybe some weak hands that still need to get shaken out of their positions, particularly in stuff that has behaved well and made people money. We see some profit taking. Okay, let’s press on some of those types of position as a tactical punt.” 

“Rabbi, what else do you like?” I say. 

“Let me dig some more. I’ll take a look at past pandemics to see what 

has moved the most: Spanish flu, SARS, MERS, and H1N1. Anything else?” 

“Let’s look at hedge fund positioning,” I say. “Let’s look for the most crowded names.” 

“Good idea.” 

“Anything else?” I ask. 

“Well,” the Rabbi says, “Kraft finally got downgraded. We will see more fallen angels. Corporate leverage has risen so much. And credit spreads just keep coming in. You’re just not getting paid to own credit. And if COVID-19 gets worse, credit spreads will really move. We should pile on.” 

“Let’s sell some more.” 

We end the meeting, and I head into my office. I want to think. 

This is the dark side of globalization. The virus helps to show how the global economy and central bank activity are connected. Since China joined the WTO in 2002, the march toward greater global connectivity has proceeded without pause. Supply chains extended well beyond domestic borders; prices for goods and services went down. And with this resultant surge in global trade, emerging markets and their middle classes leveled up on Maslow’s hierarchy. Life expectancy went up and, despite the cynics across the media and Internet, we’ve lived in the most peaceful time in human existence. But it’s not all unicorns and lollipops. As this interconnectedness has grown, national business cycles across the world have become increasingly synchronized. Expansions and recessions across countries on different continents now occur at, roughly, the same time. As they say, when China sneezes, the rest of the world gets sick. 

What happens on the other side of the world matters more than ever now. The markets have been okay because all of the central banks are printing money in harmony. But what happens if this changes? Or think about some of the other side effects. What happens if China or the virus drags us all down? This is what’s scary. It’s not all within our control 

anymore. We allowed it to get too big. The central banks will never be able to exit, and there is just too much debt. It’s as if everyone has the same positions. You can’t all unwind at the same time. The door is too narrow. 

The market psychology behind this is simple. When QE is announced, the markets buy stuff and drive up prices. That’s what has worked for the past ten years. That’s how we’ve trained the markets to behave. On the other hand, when central banks try to exit, the markets go down. Any real exit would likely cause a drastic fall in stocks. Again, that’s how we’ve trained the markets to behave. That’s their muscle memory. 

Which all raises the big question: How are we ever going to get out of this thing? Do you not think that every fucking computer in the world is trained to sell stocks the minute that tapering, the exit, is announced? 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

I decide to walk home after the market closed. It’s a nice night. As I walk, I think. 

Wall Street traders used to be the big swinging dicks. The guys at Solly, then Milken’s crew at Drexel, and then Goldman took things up a notch. Back in its vampire squid days, Goldman ruled the world. It had the best proprietary trading operation in the world. It called people muppets and used to rip people’s faces off. The firm had all the advantages: created the instruments, sold them to you, knew where all the bonds were, knew what you owned, front-ran your orders, saw all of the flows, and took the most risk. And it was even worse if you were on the ropes, if things were going against you, because Goldman was competing with you. It wanted to put you out of business and would punish you for your mistakes. The firm was a predator. 

Speaking of the devil, one of my favorite ex-GS partners just texted. This guy was an absolute animal pre-crisis. He made a fortune selling people shit they didn’t want. He’s retired now. He was at some fundraiser in 

New Jersey and the Boss, Bruce Springsteen, did a surprise gig. He sent me a video from the event, with Bruce playing “The Promised Land.” I love Springsteen. You don’t hear him much in London. I guess certain things don’t really translate well. New Jersey doesn’t resonate in Europe. 

Things have changed for the banks, postcrisis. The big banks can’t do these things anymore. And do you want to know what is interesting? We had to get Volcker back to clean this shit up—the ex-Fed guy who killed inflation, the guy who thought that a free lunch didn’t exist. Back in 2010, Obama proposed bank regulations, the Volcker rule, to reset the rules. 

So, now the banks are muzzled; they can’t take proprietary risk or warehouse positions as they used to. This means less trading-floor revenue across the Street. What do the banks do? They dump the veteran traders, the guys who have been around since the ’80s and ’90s, take a month of vacation each year and think it’s their divine right to be on the 5:15 p.m. train back to Greenwich. They’ve gotten too expensive. And this is actually pretty frightening to think about: a 2010 Wharton graduate now has a decade of trading “experience.” He has risen in the ranks like clockwork: three years as an analyst, three years as an associate, four years as a VP, and shit, he’s probably up for MD this year. And that’s assuming that I, or one of my competitors, haven’t already taken notice of his talents and made him the Vito Corleone offer. I have an army of headhunters whose singular job is to know, to the penny, how much each bank trader is hauling in at any given time. But this is who’s running the show across the Street now: a bunch of thirty-year-olds who have yet to experience a full business cycle. They only know that stocks go up and bonds go up and, in the rare instance that they don’t go up, the Fed, ECB, or BOJ will save the day. 

And when they changed the rules for the banks, the Volcker rule in particular, the bank guys got replaced, as the big swinging dicks, by hedge fund guys, and now, central bankers. Jay is the new bond king. And shit, the most powerful place in Europe is not what you expect. It’s a place called the Kitchen. This is where the ECB’s trading team works, a group of twenty- 

five or so people. It’s a nondescript room on the seventh floor of the ECB’s headquarters. It overlooks a meat market—bare walls, shitty trading desk set-ups, outdated computer monitors, and little else. This is where these pricks manipulate the markets. It’s just a bunch of kids, sitting in some boiler room, creating billions a day in new digital currency. They take this new currency and buy bonds, every fucking one they can find. These are the people who control the markets. 

I bet that most of the people in the Kitchen don’t listen to Springsteen. They’re not familiar with the Boss. They don’t know about places such as the Badlands or Jungle Land. I guess it was easier when I was growing up. With some hard work and a bit of luck you could change your position. There was still some mobility. The obstacles you needed to overcome were clearer. Today, kids are sitting in the Kitchen, creating currency, and buying more bonds—all of it radically altering the slices of the pie, wiping out the middle class. “I’ve done my best to live the right way … I get up every morning and go to work each day … But your eyes go blind and your blood runs cold … Sometimes I feel so weak I just want to explode.” Yeah, I _used_ to believe in the promised land.18 

## ***** 

All of the candidates and their traveling shows are in Las Vegas for the Nevada debate and primary. Vegas is going to determine their destinies. Biden still hasn’t won a primary and is on the ropes. Bernie is building momentum. Right now, the Dems are veering sharply to the left. I mean, like, both hands on the steering wheel and ripping that thing as hard as they can to the left. 

My boy Mo will be busy this week, keeping this group of traveling mutants well-stocked in the VIP areas. And get this, only fucking Vegas could think of this: the Dems in Nevada have sanctioned the use of a game of chance to break caucus deadlocks. If there is a deadlock, the candidates 

will each draw a single card from a deck to break the tie. The high card determines the winner. In Vegas, aces are high. 

I’m sitting with Lifecoach in our small conference room. She had pulled me aside to discuss something. 

“I thought you would appreciate this,” she says. “Watching that debate last night reminded me that I needed to tell you about it.” 

“What’s that?” 

“Well, you know I’ve been doing some online dating. It still makes me uncomfortable to admit it, but I have a good story for you.” “Go on.” 

“I set up an account and got my profile up and running. Very vanilla stuff. I’m not going to put myself out there for the world to see, you know.” “Yeap, I get that.” 

“Well, I get a couple of approaches. One guy seems decent, and we talk a bit on the phone, and he finally asks me out for dinner. He’s a fireman, which I thought would be good. The professional guys always get weird on me when they find out I make more than they do and can’t understand what exactly it is that I do. And anyway, a fireman is good fodder for stories for my girlfriends.” 

“So, did you like him?” 

“Dinner starts okay, but then he just turns into a complete fucking nutter, a complete chauvinist pig. It was unbelievable. I mean real-life things like ‘my wife will need to stay at home’ and stuff like that, and the guy just could not stop talking about his ex.” 

“Oh man,” I say. 

“Yeah, it gets bad. I just totally turned on him, start just screaming at him, and roll up a $100 bill and throw it at him. It hits him and I just storm out.” 

I’m smiling and shaking my head. 

She goes on. “He texted me this morning and asked if it was something he said. What a fucking moron.” 

Jerry opens the conference room door. “Hey, Boss, I need ten minutes with you later today.” 

“Okay,” I say. “I’ll come get you.” 

As we walk out of the conference room, I tell Lifecoach that the guy probably feels about as beaten up as Michael Bloomberg today. Last night’s debate was also a full-on ass kicking. It was Bloomberg’s first appearance, and the other candidates turned into a bunch of fire-breathing dragons. Elizabeth Warren was the first to come off the ropes. It took her about eight seconds to go after Bloomberg. “I’d like to talk about who we’re running against: a billionaire who calls women ‘fat broads’ and ‘horse-faced lesbians.’ And no, I’m not talking about Donald Trump, I’m talking about Mayor Bloomberg.” Woah, nice to meet you too! 

Interestingly, the virus did not come up at the debate. It’s not yet on the political agenda. The Big D lit up as soon it was over. “I don’t know if anybody watched last night’s debate,” he said. “And you know what? MiniMike didn’t do well last night. I was going to send him a note, saying, ‘It’s not easy doing what I do, is it?’ Bloomberg made a fool out of himself. He choked!” Trump said, grabbing his necktie and gagging. “I can’t breathe! I can’t breathe!” 

The Big D must be so happy. He’s rolling in shit. He’s winning right now. Against everyone. 

**==> picture [8 x 6] intentionally omitted <==**

I stand up in my office and make eye contact with Jerry. I motion him to come over. 

“What’s up?” I say. 

“I know this is not a good time, but I may need to take a personal day or two. Things aren’t getting any better with my wife. I need to help with the new baby.” 

“How is the new job going for her?” I ask. 

“She’s just getting going there. She’s working at the hospital near Dagenham. I think it should help.” 

“Okay. I got your back on this one.” 

The virus is now in Europe and I’m getting more worried. New symptoms are emerging: red, puffy eyes and loss of the sense of smell. The number of cases in Italy is spiking. Officials are starting to lock down villages near Milan. We now have over 2,500 deaths globally. As more information is becoming available, we know the virus appeared in midDecember in Wuhan. People are starting to pay attention. The fixed-income markets are the most worried and are trading at historic lows. 

But not the Big D. He’s not yet concerned. In a tweet, he wrote, “The coronavirus is very much under control in the US. We are in contact with everyone and all relevant countries. CDC and World Health have been working hard and very smart. Stock market starting to look very good to me!” He also pardoned Michael Milken. Milken was featured in books such as _Den of Thieves_ and _Predators Ball_ when I was first getting interested in this business. He surfed a couple of big waves. He rode one better than anyone else. Another was a complete wipeout. The third was an amazing model of rehabilitation. His waves were a sign of the times. He built the high-yield market—fucking invented it—and was better than anyone else in the ‘80s. But then the ‘80s ended and the tide turned on him. Guys like him became targets. When the government decides it wants your scalp, you’re fucked. His career is fascinating to look at in hindsight because you can see the big inflection points. 

Macro guys love inflection points, the mega-trends. When you can catch one of these you can get really rich. Getting ahead of a change in the markets is when it feels really good. Missing an inflection point is when you get roasted. 

In the markets, you lose money at a much faster rate than you make money. You can piss away a whole bunch of PnL in an afternoon. You need to manage well through those periods to have a long career in this business. 

There are lots of one-trick ponies; there are very few who can trade through entire cycles. 

Too many people miss the inflection points.
