## **Week One of the Meltdown (Sunday, February 23—Saturday, February 29)** 

The virus is getting worse. The global death toll passed 2,800, and cases broke 82,000. It’s running rampant in Italy and is spreading into other parts of Europe. Markets are all about fear and greed. Markets are squarely in the fear camp. People are beginning to freak. There are worries that the virus is mutating and becoming even stronger. There are reports of blood clotting disorders and bleeding in the brain. 

The Big D appointed VP Pence to lead a coronavirus task force. Right before putting Pence in charge, Trump downplayed the severity of the situation, claiming the number of US coronavirus cases “within a couple of days is going to be down to close to zero.” 

Today’s COVID-19 headlines: 

- California is monitoring 8,400; twenty-eight cases in the state. 

- Dozens of hospital staffers who treated a US patient with a coronavirus of unknown origin are being monitored. 

- 700 in New York are asked to self-isolate. 

- Northern Ireland confirms first case. 

- Norway confirms three new cases. 

- Germany confirms fourteen new cases. 

- Italy reports three more deaths, bringing total to seventeen; total cases hit 650. 

- Pence, Azar appoint Mnuchin, Kudlow, and Surgeon General 

Adams to coronavirus task force. 

Japan closes schools nationwide. 

- CDC fears community outbreak in Sonoma County after discovering first US case of unknown origin. 

Saudi Arabia suspends pilgrimages to holy sites. 

The dollar is starting to weaken. Risk assets are in free fall. Now it’s the USA that the market is worried about. It’s becoming a total shit show. 

The market is melting. Markets are now pricing three US interest rate cuts by the end of this year. The Dow is down 900 points at the open. Bonds and gold are ripping. Ten-year bonds are at historic lows in yields. US oil futures are below $50 a barrel. Vol is spiking. Crude is now down close to 25% this year. German yields are negative again, out to thirty years—the entire fucking German curve. 

I’m standing next to Elias, at his station. He has one of those riser desks, and he has it elevated. We are doing a line-by-line review of our risk. We flattened risk significantly a week ago, but we need to go through again. We need to know where we have any basis risk, or anything else that can go wrong. Liquidity is getting worse. Credit’s starting to widen. 

All nine of his banked monitors are flashing. He has an enormous amount of information on his screens. For most people, it would be complete overload; they wouldn’t know where to begin. But for him, everything has a place, and it’s one big mind map in action. Across one entire side are a group of chats. There must be eighty or even a hundred open chats with people. He has screens dedicated to particular asset classes. One is a global rates monitor, another shows all of the major FX pairs, and others show equity and credit markets. He’s good at his job, staying on top of every headline, knowing what every sell-side guy is thinking. 

I yell over to Lifecoach and tell her to make sure our funding is termed out. 

The desk is eerily quiet right now. Everyone is staring at screens. Just 

flashing lights. There is so much red on the screens it’s as if our whole office has a red tint. 

Today has brought the single biggest point loss in history for the Dow and S&P. The highest dollar volume traded in QQQ, the largest ETF that tracks the Nasdaq, since the day Lehman blew up. People are starting to get rattled. Federal health authorities said they now expect a wider spread of the coronavirus in the USA and are preparing for a potential pandemic. They called for businesses, schools, and communities to brace themselves and plan for potential outbreaks. 

## _Thursday_ 

It’s only Thursday but this week feels as if it has already lasted a month. It’s all happening so fast. We’ve been working around the clock. Swarms of locusts in Africa, the plague is spreading. 

The market doesn’t like any of it. Liquidity is becoming a real challenge. Market players are starting to talk about the credit ETFs, the likes of LQD, HYG, and JNK, and for good reason. Credit ETFs are designed to give retail investors bite-sized exposure to markets that would otherwise be prohibitively expensive to access. Their portfolios include offthe-run high-yielding corporate bonds that might not even trade every day. If they do, it’s likely the quantity is tiny. Even a seasoned trader such as Elias would have trouble sourcing liquidity in some of these names. And yet, the ETFs offer daily liquidity; investors can trade in and out of them as they do with Apple stock. This asymmetry is an asset-liability mismatch, and there are many more instruments like it now coming under pressure. These products are seeing huge redemptions. They are the early dominoes that begin falling when the market is in crisis. 

It’s now all hands on deck. The markets are just gapping lower. Fixed income is hitting new lows in yields every six seconds. Crude is in absolute free fall. The VIX has exploded higher to 40. EM is getting blasted. EWZ is 

at $38, down from $48 just a bit ago. Every risk asset is crashing. People just want to get out. This is the worst week we’ve had since the GFC. All of the newbies are panicking. They’ve never really seen a market rout. There are very few people in the markets now who have traded through a real crisis. The Dow is down more than 10% from its highs. European and Asian indices are down even more. It’s the most extreme sell-off in global stocks since the GFC. The bid for Treasuries, for something safe, is insane. The thirty-year bond yield has never been so low. _Never_ . There is no clear indication of what will come next. 

I pull the Rabbi and Lifecoach into a conference room at the end of a long day. It’s nearly 11 p.m., but I want to talk about our risk, not just our book but our overall risk framework and the other components that go into this, such as operational risk and liquidity risk. I think the situation is going to get worse. 

“Okay, we know that Va ~~R~~ 19 models can’t handle this, but where will the biggest problems be?” 

The Rabbi replies, “Most models will fail. Most don’t even include 2008 anymore. I think rates is the biggest problem. They’re not supposed to move like this and anything in RV space has got to be blowing the models up.” 

“VaR is a moving target right now,” he goes on. “New and higher vols going into the models are pushing bottom-line risk levels higher. And that’s not even taking higher cross-asset correlations into consideration. They’re all going to 1 or −1. Every trader is getting tapped on the shoulder by their risk managers to slash exposures. Even profitable positions are now causing problems. And remember we have March 1 coming up. For funds with quarterly liquidity, they’ll be receiving redemption notices for quarter-end this week. More cutting.” 

The Rabbi is talking so fast he’s nearly speaking in tongues. He takes a breath, scans the room, and realizes Lifecoach is writing with a red pen. “Can I borrow that pen for a sec?” he asks. 

She says, “Sure,” and hands it to him. He guns it into the trash can. “Never write with a red pen. It means losses. It brings bad luck.” 

“How about the credit markets?” I ask. 

“There, I think it’s a different problem,” Lifecoach says as she fumbles with her scarf and reaches for a new pen. “You need to think about downgrades and how that plays in. You also need to think about who has liquidity. Lots of these guys are really levered. And from a market structure perspective, these credit ETFs will not be able to handle the redemptions.” 

“What else should we be focused on?” I say. 

Lifecoach continues. “I couldn’t sleep last night thinking about this. There’s a lot. Operational risk and whether we could see settlement issues if everyone is working remotely, counterparty risk, overall liquidity risk with the dealers, our own funding … This could get really bad.” 

My phone rings. It’s Elias. “First confirmed death in the US.” 

I turn back to the others. “First death in the US,” I say. “This is bad. Let’s get all of this written up so that we can keep it in focus. I want a checklist. Go get some sleep. Tomorrow is going to be a bloodbath.” 

## _Friday_ 

Every risk asset is still for sale: stocks, commodities, EM currencies—you name it. The market is bracing for a recession. The Dow fell again today to cap the worst week for Wall Street since the financial crisis. For the week, it fell more than 12%, its biggest weekly percentage loss since 2008. On a points basis, it fell more than 3,500 points, far and away its largest weekly point loss ever. It also ended the week in correction territory, down 14.1% from its high. The S&P lost 11.5% this week in its worst weekly performance since the crisis; it’s off about 13% from its high, notched just last week. The Nasdaq lost 10.5% this week and was nearly 13% below a record high. The VIX hit a high of 49.48 today. 

We end the month down twenty-nine basis points. We lost less than a 

percent. We survived. But we have to be ready for Monday. This is going to get worse. 

## **Notes** 

18 “The Promised Land,” Bruce Springsteen. 

19 Value at risk (VaR). 

## **Chapter 6 Market Crash**

**3/15/20—** _Emergency FOMC meeting; 100 bps cut and targeted at $700B in asset purchases (could be more if needed); reopens the discount window (via 150 bps cut); back to ZIRP and QE. The Bank of Canada (BoC), BoE, BOJ, and ECB coordinate to increase dollar liquidity via swap lines._

**3/24/20—** _Tokyo Olympics postponed for a year._ 

**3/26/20—** _Jobless claims spike to 3.28M for week ending March 21._
