**Week Two of the Meltdown (Sunday, March 1–Saturday, March 7)** 

_Monday_ 

F amed economist Rudiger Dornbusch said that “in economics, things take longer to happen than you think they will, and then they happen faster than you thought they could.” If you want to be in the markets, you have to multiply that by a hundred. I wonder how Rudiger would have described it after gulping down three big horse steroids. That’s what the markets feel like right now. We’re headed toward something bad. I mean, like fire and brimstone. 

Over the weekend, the news got worse. New York’s first positive case was announced on Sunday. Outbreaks in Washington, Chicago, Florida, New York, and Rhode Island. Now, 3,000 deaths globally. Retailers are preparing for a surge in stockpiling. Overnight, the markets traded in a crazy, volatile range and are poised for another gap down at the open. Everyone is watching and waiting. In Paris, they’ve closed the Louvre. At the Vatican, St. Peter’s Basilica has been shuttered during Lent; even salvation is on hold for the virus. Manufacturing data from China has shown its steepest decline on record. The virus is bringing activity to a standstill. 

Economists in the USA are busy slashing forecasts in anticipation that activity will slow substantially in the next six months. Goldman Sachs now anticipates 0% growth; it’s a cutesy forecast. Nobody can model this mess with any amount of accuracy. It’s only a statement of severity, and an ugly one at that. The one thing keeping the market from moving even lower is a growing sense that we are about to see coordinated easing by global central banks. They will need to step in, and no one wants to be sitting balls short when the inevitable red headline hits their Bloomberg terminal. 

I walk into the office, and Elias is immediately in my face. “Boss, I’m talking to our contacts in the City. They say they’re going to close everything down.” 

“What do you think that does to the markets?” 

“I don’t know. But it’s not good. There are so many issues. Do the 

exchanges stay open? Is there any liquidity? Can the banks move their operations to one of their disaster recovery sites? Shit, can people get any food?” 

“Okay, keep me posted on what you hear.” 

Everyone always likes to talk about the black swans, the big ones. And somehow people always think they’ll somehow see them coming. But there are always precursors to the event that people miss, such as bankruptcies at Countrywide and Northern Rock that sent warning signs before the GFC. Even now, we’ve had our fair share of these precursors, the omens. The world has had a long string of one-offs, each nibbling at growth: trade wars, Chinese deleveraging, the European debt crisis … Fuck, how about all of the other signs: inverted yield curves, the repo markets, WeWork, and all the other bubble indicators? Maybe this is the one that finally tips us over. This might be the big one. 

And the Fed is in a decidedly different spot if this is the one. Despite stocks coming off all-time highs and US employment at all-time lows, the Fed has already cut three times and added to its balance sheet over the last six months. With Fed funds now at 1.50%, there’s not much room for the bank to maneuver if things go hyperparabolic. Before the GFC tremors started, policy rates stood at 5.25%. The Fed could meaningfully cut rates and capital could safely flood into the Treasury market once equities became inhospitable. There was a cushion underneath the market. This market crash would be like jumping from the forty-ninth floor and hitting the concrete at terminal velocity. 

In a meeting with Republican senators at the US Capitol, the Big D said, “This was unexpected ... And it hit the world. And we’re prepared, and we’re doing a great job with it. And it will go away. Just stay calm. It will go away.” 

Unfortunately, this does not reassure the markets. They’re all gapping lower. 

Trump’s opponents running for the Democratic presidential nominee are 

still squabbling among themselves, but three more candidates have called it quits. The conspiracy theorists say they’re dropping like flies to give Biden a clean path toward the nomination. The party machine is taking it away from Bernie. In any event, the field is getting whittled down. The race for the Democratic nomination is now a race to stop Sanders from accumulating the 1,991 delegates to win on the first ballot. Super Tuesday is tomorrow: fourteen states with 1,300 delegates up for grabs. 

The Big D has now suddenly changed his tone. In a meeting with a group of reporters less than forty-eight hours after his tweet, he tells them, “This is a pandemic ... I felt it was a pandemic long before it was called a pandemic.” He just can’t help himself. He’s the most powerful human on planet Earth and still can’t resist the urge to try to rewrite his own personal history. 

I yell over to Jerry. “Do you have the real-time models working yet on those new indicators?” 

“Almost, Boss. I need to double-check a couple of things.” 

“This is do-or-die time. We need them done.” 

The markets are in trouble. Those who are unhedged and leaning long are now praying for action from the Fed. They need the Fed to bail them out. Rumors are spreading that the BOJ is buying the market in huge quantities. When the Nikkei opened sharply lower overnight, the BOJ apparently went on a record intervention and bought a ton of Japanese stocks. As the day wears on, the rumor gets confirmed. According to Reuters, the BOJ bought 100 billion yen’s worth. This is causing the narrative to shift back in favor of the central banks. And then … there it is … Elias is standing up and yelling. 

“Powell on the tape. Powell on the tape, promising a rate cut.” 

We are in for a whipsaw. Markets start ripping back. Hold on to your seats. 

Stocks rip on the announcement and pop back from their worst week since the financial crisis, with the Dow posting its best day in more than a 

decade. The Dow closed 1,294 points higher, or 5.1%. The move on a percentage basis was the Dow’s biggest since March 2009. Shit, in the last eighty years, there were only ten days in which the Dow was up more than today. It was the largest-ever points gain for the thirty stocks average (Dow Jones Industrial Average), all on central bank hopes. 

And there it is. The Fed formally announces what Jay promised: an _emergency rate cut—_ fifty basis points. In theory, we should reposition the book: buy back more of our euros since the dollar should go lower, and buy stocks. But I’m not convinced. I want to see some follow-through. This is the first emergency cut since the 2008 financial crisis. Stocks should like this … but they feel sick … stay away for now. The yield on the benchmark ten-year US Treasury just dipped below 1% for the first time in history and the dollar is still rallying. People are still looking for safety. A rate cut alone won’t do it. A rate cut won’t fix supply-chain disruptions, and it definitely won’t cure a deadly bat-born virus. The markets want more QE. The markets need more fucking QE! 

You can feel the tension on our desk. You can almost see it in the air. It’s game time. 

Bubbles build for a long time before they pop. But when they pop it’s nasty. It’s the suddenness of the change that makes it tough. You see the conditions, you know they’re unsustainable, you take some shots at it, but you have to be careful. You can lose a lot of money in the late innings of a bubble. Just as with subprime, people saw it, but if they weren’t careful; they got carried out before the bubble burst. US house prices peaked in 2005. Everyone knew that fucking NINJA loans were an accident waiting to happen, but they still had to wait three years for the market to discount reality. One of the first books on markets that I read was _Reminiscences of a Stock Operator._ In that book, Edwin Lefevre chronicles how Jesse Livermore, one of the first great short sellers, struggled in the late innings of a bubble. This was in the early 1900s. He nearly went broke after multiple failed attempts. Livermore said, “I simply had to be sure, the next 

time I tried … The public with their eyes fixed on the stock market, saw little that week. The wise stock operators saw much that year. That was the difference. In February 1907, I cleaned up. Great Northern preferred had gone down sixty or seventy points, and other stocks in proportion.” You have to be patient. 

When bubbles burst, it’s complete insanity: Prices plunge, liquidity disappears, markets break, and regulators step in with crazy rules. Then, margin calls, bankruptcies, suicides…. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

The markets are still plummeting. We have now had the fastest sell-off since Black Monday in 1987. Black Monday was the largest one-day percentage drop in history. The Dow fell 508 points, which was over 22%. Back in ‘87, I was still in Detroit, just getting out of high school. I didn’t understand what a 22% crash meant. 

We didn’t talk much of central banks back then. I couldn’t even imagine a million dollars, let alone trillions and trillions. It was a good middle-class neighborhood. Lots of Italians. Detroit’s a place where you learn early on that your only way to get ahead is to outwork the other guy. It’s a long way from Berkeley Square and Knightsbridge. They don’t have a Nobu or Annabel’s in Detroit. The people in my neighborhood didn’t even know that this other world existed. Just working schmucks who wanted better lives for their families—and for their sports teams to win. I went to good public schools. 

It was the tail-end of the rise of the middle class. Detroit was a leader in the rise of the middle class, all part of something called the great northward migration, all of those auto jobs, good jobs with pensions. The migration had other consequences, some good, some bad. The music was amazing; the racial tensions not so much. 

Think of the excitement: “Help Wanted,” “Good Jobs Available.” Six 

million people headed North to get one of them. Get on that assembly line and help build Cadillacs—an amazing fucking mix of people and jobs. Just think about what this mix did to the local music scene. I’m talking Marvin Gaye, The Temptations, Stevie Wonder, Diana Ross and The Supremes, Smokey Robinson and The Miracles, The Jackson 5, The Four Tops … The sound evolved and we got MC5, Iggy and the Stooges, Alice Cooper, Grand Funk Railroad, Bob Seger, Glenn Frey, The White Stripes, Kid Rock, Eminem…. 

And great radio. They still have great stations. I used to love tuning into WJLB-FM for _The Electrifying Mojo_ or WRIF-FM for Ken Calvert. I still follow him on Twitter. Shit, I grew up less than half a mile from where Patti Smith was living for a while. I wish I had knocked on her door and offered to mow the lawn or shovel the snow or something. My first job was just down the street from her house at the local McDonald’s. I was the fry guy. I got to make things such as Chicken McNuggets. When my friends came in, I’d smuggle an extra nugget into their order. 

All of it was just fucking beautiful except the stuff that was not. I was born in ‘68, just after the riots, and I grew up in the Detroit of the ‘80s. I 20 ~~.~~ grew up in the Motown of Bubba Helms celebrating the World Series win 

Detroit’s a precursor for what is coming next. Detroit’s where some of the poorest people in America live today. Nearly half the kids live in poverty. Median household income is $26K. Almost half the adults are functionally illiterate. And Detroit’s violent. Detroit does violence like an art-form. 

These are the people who have already been left behind. They have no shot, no skills. And now that same rot is creeping further into the suburbs. The middle class is in the on-deck circle, waiting for its turn. A couple more rounds of QE, and they will be wiped out too. 

***** 

## **Week Three of the Meltdown (Sunday, March 8–Saturday, March 14)** 

## _Monday_ 

The markets are in full-on freak show mode: a thousand points up, 900 points down. Rinse. Repeat. 

These are the most volatile markets we’ve ever seen. California just declared a state of emergency. The tape bombs are flying. Uncertainty is high. Markets hate uncertainty. Crude is getting drilled again. The entire energy space is burned out—and airlines, travel, casinos, hotels— everything is getting pounded. The World Health Organization has just declared COVID-19 _a global pandemic_ . 

At a press briefing, Trump issues an order to control the spread of the virus in the USA: “My administration is recommending that all Americans, including the young and healthy, work to engage in schooling from home when possible. Avoid gathering in groups of more than ten people. Avoid discretionary travel. And avoid eating and drinking at bars, restaurants, and public food courts. If everyone makes this change, or these critical changes, and sacrifices now, we will rally together as one nation and we will defeat the virus. And we’re going to have a big celebration all together. With several weeks of focused action, we can turn the corner and turn it quickly.” 

“Boss, my guys at some of the big hedge funds in New York are saying they’ll start working from home.” 

“Thanks, E,” I reply. 

“Boss, they can’t get cash from the ATM machine. Banks are starting to put a limit on withdraws.” 

“Fuck. Not good. Go get some sleep. Tomorrow could be wild.” 

I wake up at 2 a.m. Futures are down sharply. The Dow is down 1,000; S&P down 120; Nasdaq down 250; crude down again; ten-year yields are under 1%. It just feels bad. It’s not even Friday yet. People are getting tired —and scared. 

I’ve told the team they can work from home now. Luckily, everyone has home set-ups and can function well enough. I like the trench in times like this, so I still go into the office. Elias is always there, always manning his bank of monitors. There’s something comforting about knowing he’s always at his station, like a sentinel at his post. But we now sit on opposite sides of the room. 

The Rabbi is firing on all cylinders. He’s in his element, emailing me fifty times a day. He has also managed to source a case of N95 masks from his connection in China. These are the best masks out there. Lifecoach is working the phones, cashing in on favors she has accumulated over her thirty-year career. Elias is executing around the clock and has deputized Jerry, who is working from home, to help monitor orders and headlines. I’m grateful he found Jerry something to do; his models, while helpful during sleepy and liquid market conditions, are basically useless during this type of mayhem—too slow, too intricate. I really need to hire an execution trader in Asia, but it’s a logistical impossibility at the moment. We need to gut it out with what we have for now. 

The day wears on and the markets start to puke. Casino stocks getting annihilated, down 15% on the day. Credit blowing out wider. Tomorrow might be a real shit show. Who wants to live through a weekend of more headlines? It sure as hell ain’t going to get any better over the weekend, only downside. When you only have downside, that’s a really shitty spot to be in. 

You know what else sucks? This downturn is going to hammer all of the part-time workers. All of those leisure and hospitality jobs we’ve been creating will be particularly vulnerable. All of those bartenders who are working part-time with no benefits will be the first to be laid off. 

Politics is moving as fast as the markets. Super Tuesday was a landslide for Biden. He won nine states. Woah! Bloomberg and Elizabeth Warren just dropped out. It’s now a two-person race. Biden and Sanders. 

## _Friday_ 

The most disorderly day yet. People are in full-on panic. I have emails from family members about their 401(k)s. People are losing a shit-ton of money. The Dow Jones has now swung by over 1,000 points five times in the past two weeks. 

I’m grabbing a cup of coffee on the way to the office when I get a call from Elias. 

“Boss, it’s a shit show. Dealers are saying there are no offers in fixed income. Bid only. VIX is back to fifty. Dollar getting pounded. Bond yields are crashing. We are headed to zero. Crude is down to $43. Gold is going to hit 1,700.” 

“Where is the ten-year?” 

“Seventy-eight basis points, Boss. Lowest ever.” 

“Shouldn’t housing rip here once this stabilizes? After all, mortgage rates fell to their lowest level on record.” 

“Not right now. It’s a full-on puke fest. Too early to start nibbling.” 

“Should we take down any of the hedges?” 

“Let’s keep them. The book feels pretty balanced right now. It’s behaving well.” 

“You worried about anything in the book?” 

“I’m worried about everything. It’s bad out there.” 

Bond yields keep crashing and equities just follow them lower. Today’s move lower in thirty-year Treasury yields is the biggest since November 2008. The VIX hit an intraday high of 54.39 today, its highest level since the crisis. 

I call the Rabbi. 

“Hey, what are you thinking here?” 

“I don’t like what’s going on beneath the surface. Really bad stuff. People are afraid of each other. They don’t want any counterparty risk. I’m focused on that.” 

The Rabbi is right. A key gauge of banking-sector risk, the FRA/OIS spread, soared to its highest level in almost two years, while dollar swap spreads widened, suggesting stresses in US markets are becoming increasingly severe. We need to be on high alert for this type of risk. The European banks have shitty balance sheets and could break the entire system. The plumbing is disintegrating. 

Oh shit. Oh shit. This is bad. Black Monday 2020-style. We can now add a price war in crude to the issues the market needs to deal with. OPEC is blowing itself apart. The Russians aren’t cooperating. They won’t restrict output and put a floor under the price. Putin now wants to extract maximum pain on the Permian Basin. Saudi Aramco is kicking off a price war to show its dominance. It’s willing to be a loss leader to win the war. 

Oil is indicated to open below $32 a barrel tomorrow, a fucking 30% move down from where it closed on Friday. It’s full-on ape-shit bad in the markets. People are losing their fucking shirts. Everything is blowing out. US stock futures are limit down, high-yield bonds are gapping wider, tenyear Treasury yields dipped below 0.5%. We had flash crashes in currencies overnight as the oil market crash sent shockwaves through the FX markets. CAD, MXN, RUB, NOK, and ZAR are all getting killed. Adding a bit of credit risk to the overall picture is the worst thing possible. Bankruptcy filings are inevitable. XOM, CVX, and BP are dripping like rocks. This is going to create a nasty feedback loop exacerbating the dollar shortage globally. Everything is correlating to one. Fucking watch out below. 

The Rabbi calls me back again. 

“Boss, this is a disaster. US credit markets have grown from $2 trillion in 2008 to $7 trillion today. All driven by more BBB and single-A paper outstanding. All the BBB will become junk. Energy is its largest component. Sell HY.” 

“Call E. Tell him to hit some bids in CDS.” 

The Rabbi is yelling into his phone, “And more pressure in repo. The entire system is about to break. The Fed is now totally fucked.” 

The Fed announced its first emergency rate cut since the GFC, and the market laughed in its face. The only thing the bank can do now is cut to zero, which is already priced, and restart aggressive QE. Doesn’t the Fed understand the market wants more QE? Just start buying everything— bonds, credit, equities—fucking everything. 

## _Sunday Night_ 

The market opens and we immediately hit a circuit breaker. Circuit breakers were introduced after the crash in 1987, Black Monday. They halt trading on the nation’s stock markets during dramatic drops and are set at 7%, 13%, and 20% of the closing price for the previous day. The market-wide halt was the first since 1997. 

The collapse in oil and the collapse in yields are as bad as we saw during the GFC. The algorithms are jumping on the momentum and pushing things lower. The machines are pushing as hard as they can to crush the longs. Credit’s blowing out. The markets are now down huge from their highs two weeks ago. This is a major dump. We are getting close to bear market territory, the first one in more than a decade. 

We get to the end of the day. Stocks have cratered. The markets have fallen close to 8%. It has been the worst one-day decline since the GFC. Foreign-exchange markets have puked all over themselves as investors fled to safe stuff such as the Japanese yen. Energy stocks in the S&P have fallen to their lowest levels in fifteen years. Risk assets are in free fall. The VIX hit its highest level since the crisis. 

It’s 2008 all over again. 

Every time it happens, you feel the jolt, like being in a car accident. There’s fear, adrenalin, and the sense that you just want to vomit. The great traders find their focus. The weak ones get paralyzed and end up selling insurance for a living. It requires 24/7 100% attention. Even the slightest 

errors will cost you huge amounts of money. I’m laser focused. The screens in front of me feel alive. 

Markets like this try to shred you alive. They take no prisoners. They try to do as much damage as possible. A lot of people don’t make it out. When stocks are moving up or down 5% a day, VIX is above fifty, and bonds are triggering a VaR warning every ten minutes, this is not the time to try to be a hero. You don’t try to make money in periods like this. It’s too late to hedge. Liquidity is gone. You do your damnedest to fucking just stay alive, maintain fewer positions, figure out where the pain is. Trade small so you can widen stops. Don’t get cute. Don’t try to be a hero. 

It’s insane how events such as this seem to get multiplied. It’s not just one. It’s global pandemic _and_ oil shock, both unexpected. Today, this combination is exposing our economy’s Achilles’ heel: too much fucking debt. That’s our soft underbelly, our weakest link. We’ve been running this scam for fifty fucking years. We built a world based on a debt bubble of epic proportions. It has tried to pop lots of times, but every time, we just shuffle the deck. Now it wants to pop again; the virus wants to expose our sins. 

The markets are in a bipolar frenzy. Stocks soared in insanely turbulent trading, wiping out much of the losses they suffered a day earlier, after the Big D’s promise of “very major” proposals to counter the economic pain from the coronavirus. The virus has spread to more than 100 countries. Confirmed cases outside China have tripled over the past week and officials today locked down all of Italy, where the virus is currently killing more people than anywhere else. We now know it’s a mysterious pathogen capable of harming the body in a myriad of unexpected, and sometimes lethal, ways. 

And more bad news for Bernie Sanders. Biden swept him last night in Michigan, Missouri, Mississippi, and Idaho. This is a serious blow to Bernie, and Biden now has the momentum. Bernie didn’t even make a speech at the end of the night. He headed to his cabin in Vermont to hide 

out. Not a good sign. It seems just yesterday that Bernie was hitting jackpots in Vegas, and now he’s on the ropes, and Vegas is closed for business. Yes, Vegas is shut. I mean like go find the keys so they can figure out how to lock the front door. They’ve never had to do that before, the casinos, the restaurants, the clubs, the day pools—everything. Wynn stock just hit an intraday low of $38 from a peak of $152 in January. 

And there it is. Stocks down 20%. The bull market is over and very likely with it, the longest economic expansion in modern history. The Fed and its counterparts pulled out every stop imaginable to keep the party going but, after 128 months, this is the end. Exhausted ammo, thrown kitchen sinks, more booze in the punchbowl, whatever your preferred metaphor for central banking largess in the postcrisis era, this is the end. The bear is here, and that’s in the USA. Globally, stocks are down even more, 30%, even 40%. The Dow finished the day down 1,465 points, or 5.9%, to 23,553. A bear market means a decline of at least 20% from its recent high. The high was set a fucking month ago. And it’s not over; it’s going to go lower. 

The markets are in complete disarray. The Fed had to throw another $175 billion at the repo problem to try to stabilize the RV market, but it’s not helping. The big bond relative value players are in trouble. Bond RV is a very specialized fixed-income strategy that tries to extract alpha from tiny but reliably profitable market inefficiencies, such as small kinks in the yield curve and basis arbitrage. These all have a small profit potential, so they require massive balance sheet leverage in order to amplify returns into an institutionally viable strategy. It’s a high Sharpe strategy, but look out when the shit hits the fan. And now, the tail they sell to be in that business is slapping them in the face. Billions of dollars in losses. The moves in 5s10s30s flys, futures basis and off-the-run Treasuries are unprecedented, ten standard deviation moves. 

Blackstone is telling its portfolio companies to tap their credit lines, draw down their revolvers. The firm thinks we’re headed to a credit crunch. 

The credit markets are in full-on capitulation. The high-yield CDX spread is back above 600 bps. High-yield energy bonds are at 1,600 bps. 

## _Wednesday_ 

Futures were halted again last night. Stocks were limit down and crude is down another 6%. At the market open, stocks hit another circuit breaker. I’m not getting any sleep. The pandemic is accelerating, and people are panicking. March Madness has been cancelled, the NBA has been suspended, and the Big D announced a ban on travel from Europe. The message is clear: bolt your doors, stay inside, and sell fucking everything. 

I’m watching the Big D’s nationally televised prime-time appearance. He announces a new travel ban and says he’ll offer financial assistance to those affected by the spread of the coronavirus. But he doesn’t give any details. Oh no, this is bad. This is not what people expected. 

He shit the bed. 

It’s so typical. Just when the Big D thought he was winning, the market gods turned on him. They abandoned him. That’s what markets do. Veterans of this business have a keen sense for things like this: when it seems too easy, when people are speaking out of turn. Jesse Livermore had his hoodoos. Joe Kennedy liquidated his stock portfolio and went short just before the 1929 crash, all because his shoeshine boy was pitching him stock tips. Whatever company, country, or market is featured on the cover of _Barrons_ will undoubtedly experience a reversal of fortune soon thereafter. For me, it’s whenever my brother-in-law calls to ask whether he should invest in the market. It’s part superstition, part behavioral finance. 

Did the Big D really think he could brag about the stock market in November? That’s just not how the universe works. That’s not how the markets operate. Just when you think you can do no wrong, they fucking crush your skull until your brain oozes out of your ears. 

Our book is starting to suffer from losses. We are defensive, super- 

defensive, but it doesn’t matter in a tape like this. Unless you’re short, you’re going to lose money. Just fucking stay alive. Liquidity is gone. All of those people in credit ETFs are totally fucked right now. Shit, you can’t even get liquidity in Treasury bonds, let alone credit. Bid/ask spreads are gapping wider in every product. People are starting to sell anything that’s liquid. That’s why gold is down when it should be rallying. The big guys are starting to unwind. Anything with any basis risk is losing money. Longs are going down faster than shorts. Rumors are starting that some hedge funds are not meeting margin calls and getting positions blown out by the prime brokers. 

We have an ECB meeting today. Let’s see if those bankers have any magic beans to help stabilize the markets. They have already bought $223 billion of corporate debt in less than four years of QE. They’re now suffering huge losses on those bonds. They’re going to need to buy the entire fucking market to stabilize this shit show. We are seeing wholesale liquidations now. 

I’m on the desk with Elias. We have the Rabbi on the speaker. It’s louder than usual. Elias has two squawk boxes running in tandem, and CNBC is on high volume. As he finishes a call, he says, “I’m getting more bad reports. Commercial paper is locking up again like in ‘08. The big ETFs are seeing major redemptions. There is no way the market can handle an unwind. And EM is getting ugly. Currencies are under attack.” 

The Rabbi’s voice comes crackling through the speaker phone. “We should be selling more of them.” 

“Okay,” I reply. “Get out there and hit some bids. Both in credit and in EM. Let’s get bigger.” 

The static comes through the speaker again. “Hey, Boss, one last thing. Name the one restaurant stock that’s up year to date.” 

“I’ve no fucking clue.” 

“You should know this, as a Detroit guy. It’s Domino’s! Shit, I may have to take back my statement about them being the _best_ performer this decade. 

They’re the _only_ one up. Good tomato sauce goes a long way. Shit, if you’re quarantined, who else you gonna call for a pie?” 

Elias interrupts. “LaGarde on the tape. More QE.” 

The Rabbi begins reading the press release out loud. “Another 120 billion euros. That’s not big enough. And what’s that comment? What does she mean, ‘We are not here to close spreads?’ Are you fucking serious? That’s your only job right now. Markets won’t like this. We need to start selling more. Hurry. What was she thinking? What the fuck is LaGarde doing? Doesn’t she realize that they have no choice. Fucking amateur hour.” 

I jump in. “E, he’s right. Hit more bids.” 

“On it.” 

The Rabbi’s voice is now increasingly distorted by the speaker. In the fuzziness, you have to listen carefully to make out his words. “There is only one remedy. Massive QE. Just start handing out the money. Get the fucking helicopters revved up and flying.” 

I turn to Elias. “This is bad.” 

The day turns into a shit show. German stocks crash down 12% on the news. European stocks have their _worst day ever_ . The Italian market was down more than 17%. 

In times like this, I tend to shorten the bench. I don’t care what the Street or even some of my own team are saying. I rely on the people who have been through these periods with me. I only want people who have seen severe pain, when the market can’t take it anymore, when people are bleeding from their eyes. I lean on the Rabbi for markets and Lifecoach for funding. These are my two lifelines. I dial them up for a conference call. 

“What are we missing here?” I ask. 

The Rabbi is the first to reply. “LaGarde’s package was too small. I don’t think the Fed makes the same mistake. She’s too new for this. She’s just making novice mistakes. Jay will go big. I expect a much bigger response in the US.” 

Lifecoach agrees with him: “LaGarde is worried about the banks. She was being too careful. We should start to think about any counterparty exposures that we have via OTCs. I don’t want any credit exposure to the European banks in this.” 

The Rabbi cuts her off. “Stuff is going to remain volatile. This is going to trade like a bear market now. Watch out for some nasty squeezes. VaR will remain elevated. We should keep position size small.” 

“How about funding?” I ask. 

Lifecoach jumps back in. “Our funding is all termed out. This is not a problem.” 

“What if they do another TALF? We should get the lawyers ramped up so that we can get in line for some cheap funding.” 

“Yeah, good call,” she says. “Mnuchin could easily follow that playbook. If they’re going to start giving stuff away for free, let’s get our fair share. I’ll call our guys now to see what they’re hearing.” 

“I think we should probably hire an epidemiologist as well. We need someone to help translate this medical data for us. Let’s get Elias on that to find someone good.” 

The Rabbi raised a good point on this being a bear market. Bear markets are a totally different game. You need to trade them differently, and not many people in the markets have any real experience with this. The markets always spend more time going up than down, so people get used to the comfort, the rhythm, of a bull market. In a bear market, you get much more volatility, lots of head fakes that suck in the weak hands and blow them to bits. You need a lot more patience and discipline. And the Rabbi is right on risk as well. VaR frameworks can’t handle these markets. Markets are not supposed to move like this. VaR models are modulated for normal markets. When your entire risk framework has been turned upside down, it’s hard to assess position size and how to really manage PnL. You need people who have seen this movie before and know the ending. You need extreme mental toughness. 

## _Thursday_ 

A new day. A new low in the markets. US equities plunge another 10%. For the S&P 500, it was the worst one-day drop since 1987. We are now down 30% from the peak. This has all happened so fast, in ten or so trading days. The VIX is back to the 2008 highs. For fuck’s sake, the VIX is at seventyfour. 

Central banks are trying to fight back. The ECB was a disaster, but globally, we are seeing more activity: interest-rate cuts, asset purchases, currency interventions, and liquidity injections. But they need to do even more. People are losing too much fucking money. Central banks need to fight back. The Fed is punching wildly to ease policy and lubricate the funding markets. It just turned the repo and T-bill purchasing program into full-blown QE. The Fed is taking it to $5.5 trillion, one of the largest amounts ever announced, $5.5 trillion to add grease to the system. The bank thought it was a bazooka, thought that the markets would be impressed, in awe, that it would drive the markets higher. But for now, the markets shrug it off as if it were nothing, a small rally and then a bellyflop right back to the lows. 

Daily life is grinding to a complete halt. #canceleverything is trending on Twitter. In the USA, every major sporting league has now suspended or cancelled its seasons. Shit, basically any large gathering is now cancelled. The message is clear: get into your fucking bunker. 

Traded halted again. Another circuit breaker. It’s the new norm. We are in a full-on market crash. 

## ***** 

In this business, you can never panic. You always need to have a plan. When the markets are going gonzo, I like to get back to basics. I read my old daily trading journals to see how I reacted in past periods, what 

mistakes I made. It’s all good process. At the end of the day, it’s all about assessing probabilities. We all play probabilities. There are no sure things. 

I read through my old journal entries from past crises. Some entries catch my eye: 

“Stay away from EM; it will get cheaper.” 

“Don’t get caught in value traps.” 

“Be careful with the banks, particularly European banks.” 

“Good balance sheets matter.” 

“Get paid for any liquidity that you provide.” 

“Don’t fight the Fed.” 

The consensus view from the Street is that the pandemic will push the global economy into recession in the first half of 2020. The error bands around this forecast are wide. The main uncertainty surrounds the duration of the virus, the government response, and how much damage is actually done. One of the big challenges in tracking the economy in real time is that it could take months for results to show up in data, which is all backward looking. We need to look forward. Most likely we will get the first clues in sentiment readings and high-frequency indicators such as new claims for unemployment insurance benefits, and eventually, in spending and investment data. Jerry is looking for anything else with real-time data. Many of the new apps such as OpenTable and Airbnb have this type of data. We can also look at auto sales, hotel bookings, and truck loadings. The credit card data we’ve been buying should also be helpful to see how consumption is changing. We need to find more of these real-time indicators. 

We are meeting to talk about what to do. We boil down our discussion to two critical issues: government response and layoffs. The big downside risk is that companies respond with significant layoffs that become permanent. If the consumer cracks, we are really fucked because it will impact future demand. Consumption is the only thing we’ve got going for us. If consumption cracks, the markets will be down 50 or 60%. How can the 

government soften this blow? We need to throw all of our resources into knowing the answers. 

I pull the Rabbi aside after our meeting. “How do you think Jerry is doing?” I ask him. He knows I’m asking a different question, one with much more serious consequences. He replies by not even answering my original question. “Yeah, I agree with you. No passion.” I decide that once the markets stabilize, I need to put together a job description and start looking for an upgrade to Jerry. 

Passion is important in this line of work. The markets need to be your top priority. 

When I started in the business you could still do it without a college education. Some of the best money makers I worked with just headed to Chicago, to the CBOE and Board of Trade to go make a living. That doesn’t seem possible anymore. The business has changed. Trading teams are no longer occupied by the fat guys on Solly’s mortgage desk. People are in shape. They look after themselves. Everyone is looking for an edge. The game has gotten much harder. You need crisp decision making. You need clarity. 

To me, the best traders are exceptional decision makers. They’re intensely competitive. They don’t let emotions affect their process. They’re not into excessive risk taking; they’re into smart risk taking. More than anything, they love finding a divergent view that’s not priced by the markets. 

To be a great trader, you need to know yourself. You need to develop a type of multidimensional self-awareness. You need to understand what motivates you, how you’ve been impacted by past events, where you sit on the risk-taking spectrum, what type of decision maker you are, what key blockers are holding you back from peak performance, and how you can create an environment for yourself for optimal performance. And then, you need to constantly improve and get better. And this is all after you’ve spent your 10,000 hours becoming a market expert and honing an investment 

process or strategy that produces reliable results and is embedded within a prudent risk framework. 

The nice thing is that psychologists have become much smarter in many of these categories. You no longer need to be born with some magical combination of these characteristics. Now, you can learn them. Go read about them or find a coach who can get you up to speed even more quickly. 

I like to think of these essential practices in my own five-step process. 

Spend the time to develop the technical proficiency required to master the markets. This is your price of entry, your 10,000 hours. Construct your own investment process, prove to yourself that it works, and then embed it in a risk framework that will allow you to produce the desired return stream. Most people spend nearly all of their time and effort on this point, but it’s really just the beginning of your journey. Everyone with any success has this. If you don’t, the markets will blow you to bits. 

Identify your own motivations, your own core values. It’s something called VBT.21 Money, power, excitement … or loyalty, creativity, and romance. What are your motivations? Is it the money? Is it learning? Maybe you like to compete and want to be the best? If you deviate too far from your core values, you’re likely to feel unfulfilled and unable to reach the pinnacle. You need to be honest with yourself on this. 

Know the cognitive biases or impairments that are impairing your decision making, that prevent you from taking good risk. Maybe you have an anchoring bias or a sunk cost bias. You need to identify them—oh, and hope like hell that you don’t have the Dunning-Kruger bias. 

Identify how these items impact your own risk appetite. When are they most likely to seep into your thoughts and impair your ability to succeed? 

Create that optimal routine that puts you in the right environment and right state of mind to be a top performer: keeping fit, eating well, staying balanced. It can even be little things. Maybe it’s a lucky sweater on days you get payrolls. 

And then, once you’ve done all of this, if you really want to be a master 

of the universe, you need to develop an entirely new set of skills. You need to learn how to scale and how to build a great business and great teams. Selecting team members, managing them, getting the right mix of skill sets and technical abilities. All of this means less time trading and more time managing. 

Many of the early hedge fund guys found their inspiration to go into this business in a guy named Ed Thorp. He wrote a couple of famous books. My first boss gave me one of them early in my career, and it got me hooked. Ed wrote _Beat the Dealer_ , which was the first book to prove mathematically that blackjack could be beaten by card counting, and _Beat the Market_ , which outlined many of the early arbitrage strategies. The guy was (and still is) an incredible innovator, everything from option arbitrage, warrant modeling, convertible arbitrage, index arbitrage, and statistical arbitrage. 

His superpower is being an incredible decision maker. He always has clarity. He made his dough in something called statistical arbitrage; he basically invented it. He was able to take his math skills and invent a whole new way of investing. 

Everything he does is supported by the odds. They’re always in his favor. This is a hallmark of people who are uber-rational, guys such as Warren Buffet, who always seems to have all of the liquidity, all of the buying power, when other people really need it. Warren knows that one of the easiest ways to make outsized returns is to be the guy who provides liquidity when other people need it, when they’re the most fucked. Warren must be having so much fun right now. Though the markets are down 30%, he’s still sitting on $130 billion in cash. CFOs and bankers are calling him as if he were the hot chick at the bar. But instead of giving out his phone number, he just scribbles “8% convert” on the back of a napkin. 

Investing has a lot of similarities with games such as poker, particularly in times like this. Knowing how to manage your chip stack and when to bet big are important parts of both. But do not confuse investing with gambling. You cannot succeed in the markets if you’re gambling. One of my favorite 

quotes from Thorp is on this point: “[Slot machines are] the most moronic devices ever, one of the stupidest activities of humankind. People play negative-expectation games. That’s something I’m not willing to do. I’ve never even bought a lottery ticket … In standard gambling games in casinos, you can generally calculate what the casino’s edge is, or if you figure out how to count cards, you can calculate what your edge over the casino is. It’s a fact, a mathematical fact, that if you play a game like this and the casino has the edge, it will eventually collect all your money if you play long enough. On the other hand, if you have an edge, your bankroll will grow and grow and grow. ~~”~~ 22 

In the markets, Thorp only invested when he had a statically generated advantage or as he calls it “an edge.” 

“I think inefficiencies are there for the finding, but they’re fairly hard to find. The markets are mostly good at predicting outcomes, but very bad at anticipating black-swan events. ~~”~~ 23 

The best macro portfolio managers hone their own process for years and years, and they’re constantly trying to improve the data upon which they’re making their decisions. This process becomes their own edge in the markets. 

Moral of the story: Find your edge and make sure the odds are in your favor. 

***** 

## **Week Three of the Meltdown Continued** 

## _Friday_ 

We have to be careful. It’s Friday the 13th. The markets are oversold and seem as if they want to bounce. There are rumors of a large fiscal package making its way through Congress. The Fed has already relaunched QE and 

is expected to make this even bigger at its meeting next week. That, combined with a mega fiscal deal, would give some support to risk. 

The fiscal side, the spending legislation coming out of Congress, will be interesting to watch. Millions of American workers have no paid sick time. Millions more could lose their jobs and already have no medical benefits. How do we expect them to deal with this? And for fuck’s sake, let’s talk about the good stuff. Let’s talk bailouts. Whom are we going to give the free money to? Small businesses, hotels, restaurants, airlines? How about the ones that levered up by buying back their own stock to juice their executive compensation packages? Do you bail them out? Just another set of moral hazard problems. But this is not my problem. My problem is to survive. 

The big event today is a press conference set for 3 p.m., an hour before the markets close. The Big D makes his appearance thirty minutes late. He declares a national emergency in response to the outbreak. This is strategic. It frees up big amounts of money for him to dole out. He also announces a number of other steps the government is planning. The markets rally 10% on the news. Yes, 10% in fifteen minutes. Complete insanity everywhere. 

On the desk, we are watching this tick for tick. We are screaming at Elias to follow the market up. This is a complete whipsaw, and we need to keep the book balanced. The markets rocket higher by more than 7% in just the final eight minutes of trading. It was one of the greatest face-ripping short squeezes of all time. In times like these, the government becomes dangerous. It will orchestrate these short squeezes, or worse yet, just change the rules of the game. This is when it goes into high gear in terms of market manipulation. 

Even with the gains, however, global stock markets have lost $4 trillion in market cap this week. It’s one of the worst on record. And it was volatile as hell. The S&P moved at least 4% in each of the five days. The last time we saw something like this was in 1929, right at the onset of the Great Depression. And there is no safety. Even in bonds. Global bond markets 

plunged today with US ten-year yields jumping 16 bps, and German tenyear yields jumping more than 20 bps as the markets anticipate a tsunami of new debt issuance due to the additional spending that governments will announce. There is no place to hide. Unless you’re in cash, you’re fucked. 

I go home to sleep. I feel I haven’t been home in a month. I’m not sure if I’ve seen my family in the last two weeks. I leave too early and get home too late. 

## _Saturday_ 

Over here in Knightsbridge, it’s starting to feel pretty shitty. Entire countries are shut down. Italy, France, and Spain closed over the weekend. Seriously, closed for business. In Italy, they’re serenading their neighbors from the balconies to help pass the time. In the USA, people are hoarding toilet paper. Stores everywhere are running out of provisions. And BoJo has announced his own version of a parallel reality. His plan is to do nothing, to infect the entire fucking country. He says the country needs to build up some sort of herd immunity. Get out to your local pub for a pint and slap a high five on your neighbor. Superspreaders qualify for a special bonus. And with the new travel ban, I don’t even know if I can leave now. I might be stuck in London. Fucking politicians. 

New York is starting to crumble and has issued a shelter-in-place order. Hospitals can’t handle the number of new cases. Medical resources are struggling to keep up. 

## _Sunday_ 

It’s a complete fucking shit show. Sundays are no longer for family time. Sundays are for emergency announcements before the markets open. The Fed has just made another emergency announcement. It cut rates to zero and launched a $700 billion QE program. More massive QE. Powell is on the tape saying, “We’re not going to be bound by $60 billion a month. 

We’re going into tomorrow and we’re going to buy strong!” This should be risk positive. The problem is that the Big D hosted a press conference at the same time. It’s a complete fucking disaster. No one can take the guy seriously. His days as a carnival barker have finally caught up with him. Karma. And why is he so orange? Why so much fake tan? I haven’t seen someone this orange since Angelo Mozilo. This is not comforting. Get him off the podium. We want to hear from Dr. Fauci. He’s the only one we trust now. Give the fucking microphone to Fauci. Futures open in fifteen minutes. 

Futures open and are limit down. They hit a circuit breaker. The Big D screwed the pooch. This is now worse than 2008. 

***** 

## **Week Four of the Meltdown (Sunday, March 15–Saturday, March 21)** 

## _Monday_ 

The markets open and immediately hit another circuit breaker. Down another 7%. The markets are in free fall everywhere. Australia’s stock market had the worst day on record. European bank stocks have hit an alltime low. The VIX can’t even fucking open this morning. Market makers can’t get a handle on prices. Volatility is spiking too high too fast. Our long/short portfolio is suffering. We had it tightly paired up, with no directional or factor bets. But it doesn’t matter. It’s just heaving back and forth like a ship in a hurricane. It’s starting to take on lots of water. The market is tearing it apart. 

The markets reopen and head even lower. The VIX is at seventy-nine. You can’t feel it in stocks, but you can feel the Fed’s presence in bonds. The market knows that the bank is about to deploy loads of cash. It will be buying. This is helping the plumbing and should help the commercial paper market, but stocks don’t give a fuck right now. The selling is indiscriminate. 

Less than 5% of S&P 500 stocks are above their 200-day moving average, the lowest since early 2009. Everything is for sale. The realization that the pandemic will result in a massive spending boost by governments around the world is causing curves to massively steepen. A wave of new debt will be hitting the markets shortly. Despite the extreme risk aversion and a historic repricing in stocks and credit, ten-year yields are climbing. Holy hell. This either means that risk parity funds are unwinding, or maybe, just maybe, folks are thinking about the fiscal dimensions of this mess. 

The last time the bond vigilantes had any relevance was eight years ago during the European crisis. This was when fiscal and budgetary concerns had a brief but disruptive day in the sun. They’ve since been relegated to the far stretches of the market discourse as heads of state promised and delivered more spending, central banks unleashed their alphabet soups of stimulus, and the world settled into this new-normal paradigm. But in finance, as in fashion, everything comes back around. Hell, maybe I should pull out my collection of BUM sweatshirts which, despite Caroline’s protests, I’ve held onto since the early ‘90s. 

The markets close. The third worse day _ever_ . The Dow is down 3,000 points, -12% on the day, the worst one-day drop since 1987. Spain and Italy just banned short selling. 

Economists are busy slashing their estimates for growth. They’re quickly revising their math downward, but they’re all guesses at this point. Boil it down to some pretty simple math and it looks like this: US GDP is $21.7 trillion, and consumption is about $15 trillion of that. A two-month decline of 20% in each, a pretty conservative estimate, would amount to $720 billion and $500 billion. Any way you look at it, it’s a big fucking shock to the system. The USA will need to run a $3 or $4 trillion budget deficit this year just to keep things calm. I need to call Ed Hyman over at ISI to see if he agrees with my math. The US airline industry asked for a $50 billion bailout just for itself. The Fed is going to have to buy a whole 

lot of bonds to support the spending. We are entering a whole new era in the QE game. 

One of the craziest things in periods like this is how you think about credit worthiness and solvency. You realize that everything is fragile and potentially vulnerable. Even great bellwether companies, companies you’ve thought of as pillars of strength, suddenly look weak and susceptible. Solvency can be fleeting in markets like these. And when things are at their worst, you can actually imagine all of these stocks going to zero. All of them ending up in bankruptcy. After all, with no liquidity you have no solvency. Big, solid companies are suddenly teetering on the verge of bankruptcy. So many companies have levered up or rely on huge volumes with razor-thin margins. So, few have a rainy-day fund. And this all happened in a fucking month. Shit, who would ever have thought that Boeing or Exxon Mobile could be in this camp? 

I gotta go meet with my compliance guy on the new government shortsale restrictions. He’s not one of the nerdy types. He’s smart and all tatted up. This is from a former life, and it gives him street smarts. He can’t be bullied. He tells me there’s no way around it. I gotta get the shit off the books. 

We are forced to close the shorts in Europe, and we are now doing our best to unwind some of the long/short positions. It’s expensive to unwind. It’s a lesson I have to relearn in every crisis. Your core book is under attack. The market is trying to demolish what you have. But anything noncore, any of your hobby trades, or anything with any hint of style drift, get absolutely annihilated. They always cause outsized losses. The only fucking thing working in our book are our credit shorts, our long dollar position, and our 2s10s steepener. Europe is now reporting more coronavirus cases per day than China at its peak. It’s now the epicenter. European markets are a mess. Their fixed-income markets are completely broken. There is no liquidity. 

Elias is working around the clock to keep us abreast of news. He’s a workhorse in periods like this. Every five minutes, there’s another email 

with the latest market headlines and color from the dealers. Every hour, he does a new scrub of our book to let us know what we could sell. I mean, if we had to. Where do bids exist? That’s one of the problems in markets like these. All of those clever, fancy OTC trades the dealers recommended to you are complete horseshit. You ask them to unwind the trades, and what you get back from them in terms of costs is a complete fucking joke. Simple is always better, but in markets like these, all of that clever shit’s worthless. You can never get out of it. I pull him aside. 

“Great job, E. I know you gotta be getting tired. Keep it up.” 

“Thanks, Boss. You know I love it, but I gotta say that I’m exhausted. This has been relentless, the worst I’ve ever seen.” 

## _Wednesday_ 

Yesterday, the markets ripped up 5%, and overnight they hit a limit down. Jekyll and Hyde. The Fed said it would launch something called the primary dealer credit facility. The move is aimed at stabilizing the commercial paper market. The bank needs to reassure companies that they will have access to short-term funds. The commercial paper market has completely seized up and the Fed needs to step in. The US dollar hit an alltime high today as investors go to the safest and most liquid thing they know. 

Two of the last three days have been among the worst performers in the history of the markets. Late in the day, the ECB convened an emergency meeting. It unexpectedly unveiled a new €750 billion QE program. The Pandemic Emergency Purchase Program will allow the ECB to buy Greek debt, broaden the range of corporate debt it can buy, and ease some collateral rules. The kids in the Kitchen are about to get really busy. 

In making the announcement on the new program, Lagarde did her best Draghi bumblebee impersonation via Twitter: “Extraordinary times require extraordinary action. There are no limits to our commitment to the euro. We 

are determined to use the full potential of our tools, within our mandate.” The problem is that she sent the tweet at 1:30 a.m. in the morning. She’s pulling an all-nighter, not reassuring when your local central banker is tweeting in the middle of the fucking night. 

Back in the USA, Treasury Secretary Mnuchin is trying to get an $850 billion fiscal package approved so he can start sending all Americans a $1,000 check—helicopter money, but not big enough. It will need to be bigger than that to have an impact. 

Globally, we now have almost 200,000 confirmed cases and 7,954 deaths. Social distancing is the new buzz word. In a sign of the times, Biden and Sanders bumped elbows at their first head-to-head debate. Biden won in Florida, Illinois, and Arizona and continues to build steam. The race is now his to lose. 

## _Thursday_ 

Today, we saw complete carnage. The PnL destruction is at a peak. Everything is getting pounded, and nearly every strategy is losing money. Our steepeners and credit shorts are providing some protection, but stocks, crude, and currencies are all moving erratically. The foreign exchange markets have now broken down. Shit, look at GBP. It just moved from 1.31 to 1.15. These are fucking ten-standard-deviation moves, GBP going to its lowest level since 1985! Developed market currencies are not supposed to trade as tech stocks do. Shares of homebuilders are down forty fucking percent this week. High yield has moved from a spread of under 300 bps to over 700 bps. Muni bonds are getting fucking demolished. Retail is getting smoked. 

We hit another circuit breaker and the markets stop trading. I run to the bathroom to take a piss. This is the day. This is the day I start thinking it can dump down 60% from the peak. This is the day I want to vomit, I get scared. And it’s not just me. Real people are starting to lose jobs. Seventy- 

eight thousand people in Ohio got canned in the past three days. Shit. It’s already starting. 

Traders develop a sort of emotional callous to headlines like these: our minds immediately hurdle over the humanity and land on the monetization. But when you have a quiet minute to allow yourself to acknowledge the human toll, it hits you. Hard. 

I walk into one of the toilets and sit down. I need to collect my thoughts. This is a day I’m happy that London has bathrooms with full doors, not those little stalls they have in the USA. I wouldn’t want the team to know I’m sitting here. I can’t let them see I’m feeling a bit rattled. I’m happy for the five minutes of solitude. 

I walk out of the bathroom and the Rabbi pulls me aside. 

“Boss, risk parity is getting pounded.” 

“Yeah, this must be the worst period for them in twenty years.” 

“It would be the mother of all unwinds.” 

Risk parity strategies are suffering major losses, and I’m hearing about big hedge-fund unwinds going through the system. They need to de-gross. If risk parity needs to dump, we are not even close to being over. And fucking crude closed at $22. This is beyond scary. Now the markets have taken over. There’s nothing you can do to a portfolio to find safety. You’re just along for the ride. If you try to sell anything, you do so much fucking damage to yourself because, with so little liquidity, you move the market. You just demolish your own marks and end up putting yourself out of business. It’s a self-fulfilling loop. People are fucking trapped with what they have. I cannot think of a worse feeling in the markets. The markets now have complete dominance over everyone. Standing on top of me, with their high heel bearing down on my throat. 

## _Friday_ 

Finally, triple witching day. These are historically volatile days, due to the 

expiration of futures, index options, and stock options. Today, the pundits say it will be good for risk. They say we could see a heck of a rally. The week has already been a disaster. The largest US investment-grade bond ETF (LQD) has given up a decade’s worth of gains in a matter of days. Credit has finally been hit hard, and the markets are down another 11% going into the day. Yes, futures are down 11% before the markets even open. The pandemic is now all-consuming. Fear everywhere. The news just gets worse. People are losing their jobs all over the place. 

I decide to walk into work so that I can think. I don’t want to overtrade, and I don’t want to overreact to things before the US markets open. London is not yet on lockdown, but most people are now staying at home. I walk through Belgravia to Hyde Park Corner and then cross through Green Park to get into Mayfair. London is eerily quiet. It’s just getting light and Green Park looks peaceful. People don’t know it’s a repurposed cemetery. It was originally a swampy burial ground for lepers, but that all changed when the royals moved in nearby. 

I arrive at the office and turn on CNBC before the US market opens. The CNBC anchors are working from home. It’s an interesting sneak peak into their lives. I feel like a voyeur. I don’t remember them playing such a prominent role in past crises. They’ve become a critical conduit of information for everyone. It has got to be tough. They’re winging it too. They don’t have their usual support, and no makeup. I feel I know them now and Cramer has had a good call on these markets. He’s a guy I respect. Raised in working class Philly, he worked his way through Harvard. He rose through the business to eventually manage a successful hedge fund. He then retired from the trench and launched a CNBC show aimed at helping folks manage their money. It’s a noble pursuit. And he doesn’t charge them two and twenty for the effort. Let’s see what he has to say. 

Not good. He sounds concerned. He’s far from bullish. 

The market opens and every financial asset falls at once. The economy seems to be imploding. A crash is always scary, but this time you have to 

live it in isolation. You don’t just worry about your positions; you worry about getting too close to the person next to you. It’s all just fear and dread now. The markets close. In the past few weeks, we’ve seen the worst oneday free fall since 2008, the worst three days, and now we’ve seen the worst week since the 2008 crisis. The markets are down 17% for the week. 

## ***** 

## **Week Five of the Meltdown (Sunday, March 22–Saturday, March 28)** 

## _Sunday_ 

We head into the fifth week of mayhem. The markets start the week limit down. Another puke. 

It’s only Sunday, but this is the new reality. The markets are down as soon as futures open in Asia. Trading is halted. Tomorrow morning feels as if it’s a month away. 

I can’t sleep. I get up to go through emails. I see one on hedge fund performance. I’m happy to see hedge funds adding some value in these markets. With the S&P down 32% or so from peak, macro funds, on average, are down about 3%; multi-strategy funds are down around 7%. But no time for a victory dance. This can all change in an instant. 

The number of COVID-19 cases worldwide doubled over the weekend. The numbers are getting big and are accelerating. Deaths are skyrocketing. No one is immune. People are bracing for millions of job losses and a historic drop in economic output, Great Depression-style numbers. Better start queuing up for that bread line now. 

But, and this is a big but, we are fast heading for the biggest, baddest, policy support ever delivered. This will be the mother of all bailouts, way more than 2008. This should stabilize the markets if you can keep your seat until then. The problem is that after the month we’ve had, lots of people 

have blown out. They lost too much fucking money. They didn’t manage risk. Thank you, come again. 

New York is the new ground zero of the virus with 23,000 cases and nearly 300 deaths in just days. The city is in lockdown. Even the exchange floors have been closed. Streets are deserted. It looks like some postChernobyl scene. Hospitals in the city are overwhelmed, with masks and ventilators in short supply. But on the streets, there’s an eerie stillness. The only thing booming is take-out from Carbones. 

Japan and Germany are now in recession, deep fucking recessions. Economic activity is collapsing around the globe. The entire global economy just got sucker punched. 

## _Monday_ 

Futures are still down, with two hours until the US open. Things feel like shit. Everything is even weaker. Another panic. But wait, wait. Here we go. Red headlines on my Bloomberg terminal. Another emergency announcement from the Fed. Holy crap! The Fed has just announced openended QE. _There is no limit_ . Whatever is needed. They also announced the ability to buy corporate bonds, investment grade debt, and commercial mortgage-backed securities. Holy fuck. QE infinity. _This is the mother of all Fed announcements_ . The bank will buy it all. 

The markets gap back: a 1,000-point swing in futures in about thirty seconds. But fuck, the markets can’t hold the rally and start selling off on rumors swirling from Washington that the government will not get the spending bill passed. There is an impasse. I flash back to September 29, 2008. Lehman had been put down two weeks prior, and the market was still trying to figure out who, or what, was next. We had CNBC on as the House was voting on the first version of the federal bailout. Through all of the confusion, I still remember our first-year analyst’s voice cracking as he digested the yeas and nays tally and uncharacteristically announced, “It’s 

going to fail.” Sure enough, it failed. With the markets applying maximum pressure, the House had the gall, or balls, depending on how you look at it, to vote it down. The market hemorrhaged into the close; the Dow ended down 777 points. Back then, it was the biggest single-day point drop in history. That bailout package was only $700 billion. I can’t help but feel a bit wistful about those days when $700 billion was a big deal. 

Same story, different rodeo. The only difference is the postapocalyptic empty streets. The markets head straight back to the lows on the leaks from Capitol Hill. They want a spending package as well. David Tepper, a famous hedge fund founder, is on CNBC. This will be market moving. He made the best calls in 2008. He sounds concerned. He says he’s “nibbling,” but “[his] balls are still in his jockstrap.” You gotta love Tepper; he does not mince words. He wants to see more progress on the medical side and the fucking politicians gotta get this legislation through. The markets close. The S&P is down another 3.5%, a new low. 

## _Tuesday_ 

S&P futures are limit up—another circuit breaker, in the opposite direction, on hopes that the spending bill finally gets done. More Jekyll and Hyde. More ripping apart of portfolios from both sides. Overnight, the Nikkei was up 18% from yesterday’s lows. Complete insanity. Un-fucking-tradeable. 

Our portfolio is now leaning long risk. This is dangerous, but I think the sell-off has gone a bit too far and QE is back in full gear. Do not fight the Fed. Do what it tells you to do. This bet has hurt for the past few days, and we need some PnL relief. 

People don’t use many words on a trading desk. There’s no time for extra words. You’re taught to get to the point. I’m thinking about this because I’m sitting next to the Rabbi and heard him on the phone with one of his sales guys. It went like this: 

“Where do you see the ARGY twenty-ones?” 

## A mumbled reply. 

## “Are you fucking kidding me?” 

Click. 

No “hi” no “bye.” No salutations needed. No value-add in those words. Central banks are now throwing in the kitchen sinks. They’re doing everything they can now to soften the blow until we get more fiscal packages approved. Between the Fed funds rate going to zero, the expansion of the balance sheet, and all the other announcements, the Fed has done more in the past two weeks than it did in the entire first year of the GFC. It’s in overdrive. 

Wow, the Dems just proposed a new spending bill: $2.5 trillion in spending. 

The Dow surges on the news. It closes up 2,000 points, or 11.4%, in the biggest rally since 1933. The S&P 500 climbed 209.93 points, or 9.4%. Very early on Wednesday morning, we finally get a deal for a $2 trillion stimulus package, the CARES Act. This is almost twice as big as the package in 2009. I gotta hand it to the Big D. Even with all the political dysfunction, he just got a whopper of a package passed. The legislation will provide direct checks to most Americans, expand unemployment insurance, offer hundreds of billions of dollars in loans to both small and large businesses, and give health care providers additional resources. It also offers the largest bailouts since—they are the biggest ever. 

It’s the largest rescue package in American history, but the markets expected it to get through Congress and the relief package has now been priced by the markets. More often than not, by the time a headline like this hits the Bloomberg terminal, it has already been baked into prices. By the time it hits CNN or FOX, it’s already old news. Buy the rumor, sell the fact. 

Futures are down again. The markets know we are in a race against time. The USA is embarking on its sharpest downturn since the Great Depression. In two weeks’ time, the period of a typical paycheck, many workers will struggle to make ends meet. Most people are living from 

paycheck to paycheck. They have no rainy-day fund. During the Great Depression unemployment went to 25%. 

The Rabbi calls me early. “Ford downgraded to junk. The biggest fallen angel.” 

“You think more are on the way?” 

“Yeah, definitely. I bet the rating agencies go into overdrive. They don’t want to get sued again like they did in 2008. I bet they start downgrading everybody.” 

“Okay, let’s get the rest of the team. I want to talk things over as soon as I get to the office. Send out a Zoom invite.” 

I walk into the kitchen for more coffee before I head to the office. Caroline is there. 

“I heard you get up early,” she says without turning around. 

“Yeah, markets are crazy.” 

“I was watching TV yesterday, and they said some of the worst days since the crisis.” 

“It feels just as bad.” 

“It would be nice if you spent a bit of time with your daughters. They’re home you know and won’t stay young forever.” 

“Okay, I hear you, but I have to get to the office now,” I say, as I head back down the hallway to the front door, thinking this is not the time for a family lecture. 

I hop in a cab. It takes me ten minutes to get to the office. I sit down and log in to my Zoom screen. They are all already looking into their video cameras, waiting for me. 

“Where are we wrong?” I say. 

Elias starts. “A lot of guys I talk to don’t trust this rally. They think we could retest the lows.” 

“But why?” 

“No vaccine, economy completely shut down, Great Depression-style unemployment. It’s all pretty dour.” 

“What’s the single biggest mistake we’ve made over the past ten years?” I ask. 

“We didn’t trust the Fed, Boss. We would get too bearish.” 

“Bingo. I’m not making that mistake.” 

The Fed has been in turbo mode the past two weeks. It has made about a dozen significant announcements, all bigger than in 2008. We now have a whole new list of acronyms. They make the Cash for Clunkers program look like something out of _Leave it to Beaver_ . I’m talking PDCF, MMLF, CPFF, MSBLP, PMCCF, SMCCF, and the big one: TALF 3.24 The bailout legislation that passed on Friday now allows the Fed to directly finance businesses, and maybe even states and cities. For each of the programs, the bank will create an SPV, a special purpose vehicle, funded with billions upon billions, if not trillions, of dollars. It will then start buying in each of the areas to prop up the markets. For fuck’s sake, the 200 traders Jay has working for him are not enough. He had to go hire BlackRock, the biggest asset manager in the world, to help him buy all this shit. And you know what you’re supposed to do now? Start buying it before they do. 

Here is my favorite part. Guys such as Jay and Mnuchin have learned they get the biggest bang for their buck if they start to comingle assets between the Treasury and the Fed. Remember, the Fed is independent; it doesn’t have any oversight. It can do whatever the fuck it wants. 

One of the funny little provisions of the CARES Act is that Congress gave the Treasury $450 billion to backstop losses at the Fed. What this means is that the Fed is going to take that money and leverage it ten times and issue a whole bunch of guarantees and credit support facilities. Jay said as much at his press conference. “Effectively one dollar of loss absorption of backstop from Treasury is enough to support $10 worth of loans. When it comes to this lending, we’re not going to run out of ammunition.” That, my friends, is how you turn a $2 trillion bailout package into a $6.5 trillion bailout package in the blink of an eye. All like magic. No wonder the 

markets are moving higher. With the announcements so far, we are probably really talking about something closer to $10 trillion in size. 

**==> picture [8 x 6] intentionally omitted <==**

It’s my birthday. London is not yet fully shut down, but there are rumors it will happen any day. Caroline has arranged for the team to meet us at the Gore Hotel near the Royal Albert Hall. It’s supposed to be a surprise, but the Rabbi blew it earlier today when he asked if he should bring something. We are all wearing masks and social distancing, but this is easy because the place is nearly empty. The bar is famous for hosting the Rollings Stones when they released their _Beggars Banquet_ album back in 1968. I like the place, called Bar 190. It looks exactly as it did when the Stones hosted the party, as if it were frozen in time. 

It’s a somber scene. This is not what a birthday party is supposed to feel like: dark, depressing. The team is tired, the place is empty, it’s raining outside. Shots are ordered, but there is no energy. 

I’m stuck near the back of the bar with Jerry and the Rabbi. Jerry is making small talk. 

“Rabbi, you think the new QE works?” 

“For whom?” 

“Well, you know, normal people.” 

“Nope.” 

“Why?” 

“They’ve already tried that. It gets into the wrong channel, fixes the wrong problem.” 

“What can they do?” 

“Not sure there’s really anything. Now, if you ask me about Congress, that’s a different story.” 

“What should they do?” 

“There’s really only one way to fix it.” 

“What’s that?” 

“Give the banks more money so they can bring back all of the old CDO guys from 2007.” 

“What do you mean?” 

“Yeah, the next bailout should be to allow the banks to hire them all back. Give them five-by-twos.” 

“What’s a five-by-two?” 

“You know, a five-million-dollar guaranty for two years running.” 

“Wait. How is that going to fix things?” 

“Because the banks need to take all of these bank reserves that the Fed just dumped on their balance sheets and lever the hell out of them. Those guys are the only ones crazy enough, and creative enough, to make it happen. That’s the only way out: by creating a bubble so big that the Japanese are buying our golf courses again.” 

As Jerry walks away from us, shaking his head at what he has just heard, the Rabbi looks over at me. 

“He thought I was joking. He’s not going to make it.” 

I don’t reply. I just shrug. 

Three more shots and the mood is improving. The Rabbi has now taken over the music selection from the bartender. He has “The Killers” blasting in the background. 

“Boss, this place is amazing,” Elias says. “I love this picture of the cake.” 

They have photographs of the Stones’ event on the walls. Mick and Keith look like kids. And Brian Jones is still in the band. The picture that Elias is referring to shows the aftermath of a food fight. Mick and Brian had been throwing cake at each other. It’s all over their suits. Mick has a fork through one of the buttons of his jacket. Brian looks as if he’s having the time of his life, maybe even too much fun, if you know what I mean. 

That particular picture is sad to me. A couple months after that event, Brian was dead. They found him floating in a pool. He missed one of the 

greatest waves ever of rock-n-roll and wealth creation, all because he was too afraid to let the party end, and he needed more and more of the stuff to keep it going. 

QE has been the same. Back when the Fed did QE1 in March 2009, it needed $300 billion. QE2 in November 2010 doubled that amount to $600 billion. QE3 in January 2013 was $800 billion. And now, we are talking trillions. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

In the markets, greed is back even though three million people lost their jobs last week, the largest increase in history. But the markets don’t care about that. There’s money to be made. The Dow rallies more than 1,300 points, capping its biggest three-day surge since 1931. The markets are up 20% from the lows on the same day we found out that 3.3 million people lost their jobs. That’s some real fucking compassion. 

“Was I just dreaming, or was the S&P really trading below 2,200 at some point last week? Does anybody else remember this, or was it all a dream?” the Rabbi mumbles. 

We are doing another scrub of our book at our morning meeting. The facts have changed a lot in three weeks. Sometimes, the reasons you got into a trade are no longer relevant. Instead of dumping the pig, however, you stick with it. You get emotionally attached to the position. A lot of people hate locking in a loss and have a hard time doing that. But in this business, you cannot allow that to happen. If you’re wrong, you’re wrong. Dump it and move on. You will sleep better at night. 

Finally, month-end, quarter-end. The fucking worst quarter _ever_ for the markets. The S&P 500 fell 13% for the month and 23% from its highs for the quarter, its biggest decline since 2008. At its low point, it was down 35%. The Dow was down even more. We were down 2.95% for the quarter. We are down but not out. At least we survived. It feels as if it should be 

Friday so that I can get some sleep. But it’s only Tuesday. The alarm clock will be painful tomorrow. 

As I head home, my sister-in-law calls. She needs a loan. Her small business back in the USA is on the brink. They had zero revenues the past two weeks. She had to lay off the people who work for her. I immediately call Caroline to let her know. Her phone just rings through to voicemail. She never picks up anymore. I just bailed out her sister and she can’t answer her fucking phone. 

The USA is now in a recession. It happened that fast. But QE is bigger than ever.
