# _**Part 3: The Aftermath**_ 

## **Chapter 7 QE Dreaming** 

## **April 2020** 

**4/1/20—** _Fed loosens large bank capital requirements to free up credit._ **4/1/20—** _ISM manufacturing down to 49.1._ 

**4/2/20—** _Global virus infections top 1M; 51K dead._ 

**4/2/20—** _Jobless claims to 6.65M for week ending March 28: 10M people unemployed over the last two weeks._ 

**4/3/20—** _ISM nonmanufacturing at 52.5, a 4.8 point decline; employment in leisure and hospitality plummeted by 7.7 million jobs, or 47%._ 

**4/3/20—** _Payrolls -701K; unemployment jumps to 4.4%; first net loss of jobs since Sept. 2010._ 

**4/6/20—** _Boris Johnson moved into intensive care._ 

**4/6/20—** _Fed further expands lending and credit facilities. New programs: Main Street Business Lending Program to buy debt of companies with less than 10,000 employees, or revenues under $2.5B; and Municipal Liquidity Facility Program to buy $600B of munis._ 

**4/9/20—** _Jobless claims at 6.6M for week ending April 4._ 

**4/14/20—** _IMF warns global economy headed for worst downturn since Great Depression._ 

**4/16/20—** _China reports Q1 GDP -6.8%._ 

**4/16/20—** _Jobless claims at 5.25M; 22M unemployed over the last month._ 

**4/20/20—** _Front-month crude trades negative, low of -$37/bbl._ **4/23/20—** _Jobless claims at 4.43M; 26M unemployed since start of outbreak._ 

**4/23/20—** _Congress passes additional Paycheck Protection Program funds._ 

**4/26/20—** _Global death count hits 200K._ 

**4/27/20—** _Fed expands access to Municipal Liquidity Facility Program to include smaller cities and counties._ 

**4/29/20—** _First estimate of US Q1 GDP at -4.8%._ 

**4/30/20—** _Fed expands criteria for Main Street Lending Program eligibility._ 

**Month-end: UST 10s at 0.64%; S&P +12.8%; Nasdaq +15.5%; dollar flat.** 

# **Week Six of the Meltdown (Wednesday, April 1–Saturday, April 4)** 

E very market crisis is different. There’s no one size fits all. World-wide, we now have more than 860,000 confirmed cases and 42,000 deaths. There are 3.5 billion people under some sort of coronavirus lockdown. Overnight, the Big D projected that the USA could face between 100,000 and 240,000 deaths. Mid-April is expected to be the peak, two weeks from now. 

The shutdowns have caused a complete collapse in economic activity and job loss is reaching Great Depression levels. I bet you can guess who’s getting laid off. The people in those low wage jobs are the first to go. The unemployment rate is going to create a new post-Great Depression high in coming months. The current high was 10.8% in 1982, and we will probably be there next week. All of this despite the fact that our lives seem increasingly dependent on the people in those dead-end, low-wage, nobenefits jobs. If you’re lucky enough to keep your job, working part-time as a cashier or health care worker, boy, do we need you now more than ever. You’re the ones keeping some semblance of normality in our lives. 

The economic data is so bad you don’t really know what to do with the readings. They look like mistakes. Italy’s Services PMI took the prize for the worst economic data collapse in all recorded history. All of this is far beyond what is normally imaginable. And in a sign of the times, Dr. Fauci now has an armed security detail. He was receiving death threats because some people disagreed with his assessment of the virus. 

London is now completely closed for business. Even Harrods is shut. Provisions are running low. You can’t find paper towels or hand sanitizer anywhere. 

## _Tuesday_ 

It’s nearly 10 p.m. in London. Zoom happy hours are the new in-thing and I’m up late to join one. One of my high school buddies back in the USA has set one up for a group of us. 

I log in and see that there are even more people than I was expecting. The core group are my lifelong friends. The others are the ones you’d see at a reunion or hear about if something bad happened. The Zoom starts and falls flat. With the larger crowd, we run out of things to talk about. Unless you’re on Zoom all day, there’s an awkwardness to the contrived group setting. It’s not the same as being together. Something’s off. I always get annoyed when people’s microphones don’t work, or they’re having technical problems. Once they’re past the small talk, they largely revert to stories of the glory days, that big party in ‘84, and the football game. I listen but just keep thinking I should get to bed. I need to get up early tomorrow. These people don’t need to worry about markets, and this entire experience is not resonating with me. I’m not sure I relate. I sign off and head to bed. 

## _Wednesday_ 

I’m up early to news that China’s official sector PMIs bounced back sharply, signaling a rebound in activity. This gives ammunition to the bulls. It shows there is a light at the end of the tunnel. You can see how they might run with this: the virus started in China and hit Europe a month later, before heading to the USA a month after that. So the bulls will want to use this bounce in the China data to set a timeline for when the rest of the world will start to recover; it’s a temporal anchor that economists, strategists, and the media will eagerly jump on. And then the game transitions into secondderivative mode: watching the rate of change of the rate of change and trying to figure out which of these small blips in data will excite a bearish market. It’s not always about arriving at the correct fundamental conclusions, the Keynes beauty contest. It’s about knowing what the markets will run with. But of all things to risk your month on, Chinese data is probably the most ass-puckering of options. Beijing has all but admitted to making up China’s economic data. Still, I have to watch this closely for the sole fact that others are doing the same. 

The markets open and are down again sharply. They were all down big overnight. Day one of the second quarter and the major equity indices close down 4%. 

## _Friday_ 

Payrolls day. It’s also the day we passed one million confirmed cases. Payrolls hit, and it’s not pretty. Nearly seven million people applied for unemployment benefits last week, and ten million people in the last two weeks. In a typical recession, you would lose that number of jobs over two or three years. This time, it took two fucking weeks. 

Our book is still leaning long risk. We’ve added to our RV positions and to everything else that the Fed is buying. We are staying away from energy, even with the pop in crude, and other products the Fed is not directly supporting. The Fed is active in the markets. It’s buying with both hands and its balance sheet continues to climb at an unprecedented rate. It’s close to $6 trillion now and moving higher every day. 

I’m on the phone with the Rabbi, talking about our next steps. I’m now working from home since our office building called the coppers on me and Elias for continuing to go into work. We’re no longer allowed— _personae non gratae_ . 

“Energy stocks are on the move. Bank stocks look cheap, but I don’t really see a catalyst. I think rates are just stuck here near zero,” he says. “How about credit?” 

“It’s already moved a lot, so I don’t know.” 

This is where I need to push him, where his instincts make it easier to say no than yes. 

“But the Fed has just started,” I say. He ultimately concedes and we buy more. 

We are entering a second chapter, no more indiscriminate selling, and more dispersion. Good balance sheets versus bad balance sheets, and 

sectors are also showing broad differences, with retail and transportation coming under more pressure than others. This is turning into a good environment for stock pickers. I bet the long/short guys will do well in this. Our portfolio is working. The RV positions have all snapped back, and our long bias is beginning to pay off. We need to keep pushing. Killer instinct. Take maximum advantage of our position. 

“Mayo San pulled the plug on WeWork.” This pops up on my chat from Elias. 

“News?” I type. He calls. 

“They backed out of the share purchase. That means that SoftBank can walk from the $1.1 billion in debt financing.” 

“Ouch. Sounds like lights out.” 

“Yeah, total shit show.” 

I look at the news story. WeWork posted a $1.25 billion loss for the quarter—and that was before the coronavirus hit. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

I’m writing an email to the organizer of the New Orleans conference I attended. He had emailed me and asked for background on why macro was outperforming so far in this crisis. 

As I finish a quick summary of my views, I tell him I’m happy to see the global macro guys holding up well in this tape. It has been a number of years since they outperformed, but the beauty of the strategy is that it tends to have its best years when the world is falling apart. These last two months have taken us back to ‘90s-style macro. This is discretionary macro in its finest hour. Back to outperforming the markets. 

***** 

**Week Seven of the Meltdown (Sunday, April 5–Saturday, April 11)** 

## _Monday_ 

We now have 1.2 million cases and almost 70,000 deaths, but the US data seems to show a pattern of slowing. Maybe the shutdown is working, but it’s really hard to tell. We just don’t have enough data. The politicians and talking heads are just making stuff up. But the markets are up big overnight. 

We are having our Monday team meeting. We are on Zoom. 

“What should we be doing here?” I ask. 

Jerry jumps in. “One thing I’ve been spending time on are the different fiscal responses around the world. I think we should broaden out beyond the USA. For example, Japan is where the situation is changing fastest. Prime Minister Abe is rolling out a huge fiscal support package, 20% of GDP. That’s crazy big.” 

“Well,” I say, “as is typical of the Japanese, the actual number will be much smaller but a good start.” 

I’m thinking this is an Econ 101 statement. He should know better. “What else?” 

The Rabbi’s face jumps to the middle of my screen as he begins to talk. He looks as if he hasn’t shaved in a month. “Crude trying to stabilize here. Shit, it’s up 40% in two days on the OPEC deal. Let’s look at the energy names, across the capital structures.” 

“E, why don’t you dig around for some of the best possible expressions if he’s right.” 

“Will do. I also think that we should be looking at FX, as well. Some of the commodity currencies.” 

I change the subject. “What about GBP?” I say. “It has gotten demolished.” 

“I think we stay away for now,” Elias replies. “With BoJo in the intensive care unit, let’s wait for that to play out. We don’t need that type of risk in the book. Hey, you guys see the story on Oyo?” 

“No,” the Rabbi says. 

“An indefinite furlough of employees.” 

“Oh, wow. That means the banks will ask for more collateral on those loans the CEO took that Masa Son guaranteed. That will be a monster margin call. What a disaster. They will end up selling shares at a massive discount. This will turn into a death spiral.” 

We end the call, and I start to unroll my yoga mat in a little studio next to my office. My daily routine is all fucked up now. No office, no gym, no restaurants. I’m going stir-crazy. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

Across London, face masks are the new fashion statement. Most governments are recommending them. Today, on my daily trip to the grocery store to get out of the house, I saw someone with a Rolling Stones tongue and lip version. I guess it gives a whole new meaning to their song _Ventilator Blues_ . 

I’m walking home from the grocery store while I’m talking to my mom. She’s still in the Detroit suburbs. Detroit’s now the latest hot spot for the virus. The outbreak has been tough for the state’s black residents, particularly those in inner-city Detroit who can’t work from home and beckon lunch to their doorstep via Grubhub. 

She asks me about the family. 

“Well,” I say, “everyone is home now. The house is crowded, and Caroline loves that. It has been a long time since we’ve all been together, with boarding schools and Paris.” 

“What about the girls?” 

“Elizabeth is finishing her school year remotely, on the computer, so she’s been busy. She misses her friends. They’ve been doing something called Tik Tok. Have you ever heard of that?” 

“No. You know I’m behind the times. How is Caroline?” 

“She’s good. She likes having the girls at home. She’s thinking about 

going back to school.” 

“School?” 

“Yeah, I think it would be good for her.” 

“I suppose you’ve been working a lot with the markets and all,” she says. 

“Well, Mom, as you always say, no rest for the wicked.” 

My mind begins to wander. It’s all sort of incredible. The solution to the crisis, so far, comes from the same playbook we’ve used for the past fifty years: more debt. Governments are now extending loans to businesses at a time when private debt is already at historical highs. We are making the debt bubble even bigger. And the USA is going to run its largest deficit since WW2. The best estimates are around $3.5 trillion right now. And that’s just in the USA. Globally, we are probably talking another $50 trillion. Already, almost 100 countries have asked either the IMF or World Bank about a potential bailout. 

“What is Dad up to?” I ask. 

“He is on his way to Tony’s Sports Bar with Bill. He wanted some of their chicken wings in case they have to close with the virus.” 

I’m deep in my thoughts but still listening to Mom, sort of like when you’re skimming a book and looking for certain words to stay keyed into the conversation. I hear her mention my nephews’ sports. 

“Mom, what are you watching with no sports, on TV?” She loves her Red Wings, and this time of year is usually great with the playoffs. 

“There is not much on,” she says. “I have so many channels but tend to just watch the news.” She begins talking about what they were saying today on the channels she watches. Those channels get her worked up, and I can hear her voice rising. 

I again half-listen to the reply and turn back inside my head. The central banks are expanding their balance sheets as fast as they can right now to do more QE. Wall Street now expects the Fed’s balance sheet to hit $10 trillion by the end of the year. 

“How about the election?” she asks. “Who do you think will win? Now that Bernie has stepped aside, Sleepy Joe is going to get his shot to beat Trump.” 

“Well,” I say, “we’re only thirty weeks away, but that’s a long time in light of what’s happening. A lot can change. Right now, the polls seem very even. I think it depends on how the recession plays out.” 

As I get close to my mews, I begin to fade out of the conversation. “I’m walking into my place, Mom. Gotta go now. Don’t watch too much of that news channel. It’ll rot your mind you know.” 

“Have a good night,” she says. “Remember to call your sister. You’re the oldest and need to call her more.” 

I head inside to unpack the groceries. Caroline is sitting, reading, and nods as I head to the kitchen. We order food for everyone and call it an early night. 

***** 

I wake up early to an uneventful morning. No big news overnight, and the markets are waiting for direction from the USA, once it comes in. We are back on Zoom but not talking, just working on our own projects. 

It feels as if it will be quiet, just another day of nothingness, another day of having no clue how long it will be until things get back to normal. Nothing is really moving. All of a sudden, I hear something. Elias is screaming at me from the other room. 

“Boss, Fauci on the tape, saying all clear.” 

“What?” 

“Not quite all clear, but he’s downgrading the virus. Markets gonna rip on that.” 

“Get out there. Start buying SPY.” 

The Fed piles on, releasing an announcement right on the heels of Dr. Fauci’s interview. The bank’s now buying _everything_ : municipal debt via 

the Municipal Liquidity Facility, high-yield debt—you name it—fallen angels, CLOs, CMBS, and leveraged loans. Any remaining fig leaves of monetary prudence have been torn off, and the Fed is now the naked guy at the party doing half gainers into the pool. They’re bailing out the entire fixed-income market. Between Dr. Fauci and the Fed, this has changed everything. 

Today is now my own version of Black Friday, which is a lot different from Black Monday. Black Friday is not a market crash. Black Friday is a day when you wake up early and wait outside before the stores open. As soon as they do, you run in and start pushing people out of your way to fill your cart and buy stuff, jamming it all in that shopping cart as fast as you can. That’s what we’re doing today. The markets are going to gap higher. 

These are defining moments for a trader. Over the course of a career, you only get this type of feeling a couple of times: that rare moment when you know you should bet big. Fuck the long-term negative consequences of what the Fed is doing. This is not the moment to care. This is the moment to take advantage of the rules and get rich. 

Almost seventeen million people have lost their jobs in the past three weeks. That’s almost twice as many people who lost jobs during the GFC. But the markets are screaming higher. The market bottomed on March 23, just a bit over two weeks ago. This has all happened so fast. 

On top of the markets, I’ve something else on my mind. I think I need to send a message and fire Jerry. That will help kick the rest of the team into high gear. They will be forced to wonder whether they’re next. Managing through fear is still a powerful motivator. I’ve been planning to do it for a while now, but I think today is the day. I text Jerry and tell him to meet me at the office. 

When the markets close, I take Jerry into the conference room. The labor and employment lawyers always taught me to keep these meetings short and sweet, so that’s what I’m planning to do. I’ve done so many of these now that I feel detached; it’s like an out-of-body experience. 

“Jerry, I’m sorry to do this, but we are going to part ways.” “What?” he says. 

“Yeah, I’m not going to go into things, but I just don’t think it’s the right 

fit.” 

He eyes start welling up with tears. He starts talking about the mortgage on his new flat, about the baby. But I’m not hearing him. I’m on autopilot and just want to execute what I have to do, just get him the fuck out of the building. 

I take Jerry’s access pass and walk him to the elevator. 

I should feel terrible, but I’m feeling relieved, a weight off of my shoulders. In some ways, I’m feeling lighter, more alive than I have in years. 

I go back to my desk and send the team an email announcing the change. 

Tomorrow is Good Friday. The markets are closed. We can all use a break. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Week Eight of the Meltdown (Sunday, April 12–Saturday, April 18)** 

## _Monday_ 

We are opening a new chapter. Medical curves are flattening, and the markets are strong. The S&P is up 25%, or so, off the lows of three weeks ago. The market had one of its biggest weekly gains ever last week. The Dow posted its seventh-best weekly performance, rallying 12.7%. The S&P 500 had its biggest one-week gain since 1974, jumping 12.1%. We’ve obviously seen massive monetary and fiscal packages, but now we head into earnings season, and then the tough work of figuring out when we can reopen the economy. 

Globally, we have almost two million confirmed cases and 115,000 

## deaths. 

Our performance is back to positive year-to-date. But this is not the time to ease up and judging by the weekend spike in email volume, the team has gotten the message. This is the time to get really greedy. 

The Rabbi pings me on Zoom. The chat comes through in rapid-fire succession. 

“You there?” 

“Want to buy some WeWork bonds?” “They’re yielding just under 40%.” “Trading at 35 cents on the dollar.” “The market expects them to default soon.” “Dealers have supply.” “Someone needs to get out.” “I still hate the company.” “Not even the Fed will buy those,” I reply. 

Today, April 13, is the start of earnings. The banks are always among the earliest to report. JP Morgan is first. Profits down nearly 70%, the biggest dump since Lehman. But we want to hear about loss reserves. How much has the bank set aside for bad loans? The call starts. The largest US bank recorded a surge in credit losses to $8.3 billion, up from $1.5 billion a year ago, mostly in the form of reserves as the bank braces for a surge in defaults. Wow. That’s a huge number. Jamie Dimon, their chairman and CEO, thinks the recession will be bad. And deep. 

Earnings season in this environment is like watching a car crash. You know it’s going to be bad, but you want to slow down and see how bad. What we want to see is any clue to when the bank thinks it can return to profitability, or whether it’s talking about more layoffs. 

SoftBank reports and announces its worst annual performance in its history. It had an $8.4 billion loss, led by WeWork. 

Retail sales hits. Down almost 9%, the largest decline ever, a superlative statement that will undoubtedly be bludgeoned to death as this cycle of 

economic data reveals itself. People stockpiled food and toilet paper but cut back on everything else. And about two million homeowners are skipping their monthly mortgage payments. 

The data is all bad, worse than everyone expected. Industrial activity in the USA just plunged to the lowest level in seventy years. A weekly update on US jobless numbers is due out this morning. There it is: another five million people lost their jobs. That makes twenty-two million in total. There are only about 150 million working people in the USA, of which twentytwo million have just lost their jobs. The numbers are staggering. 

And this phenomenon is global. No one is immune. “It’s very likely that this year the global economy will experience its worst recession since the Great Depression, surpassing that seen during the global financial crisis a decade ago,” Gita Gopinath, the IMF’s chief economist, reports. 

The market is showing a big disconnect. Equities are up 25% from their lows, but rates are still at record lows and crude is back at $20. The credit markets have all popped since the Fed started buying. And big is better, a microcosm of our winner-takes-all world. When you exclude the Big Tech market leaders, you realize that the other 80% of the market is still down 20% or more from the highs, even with all the gobs of support from the Fed and Congress. Four out of every five stocks are still in a bear market. But the markets as a whole are on fire, led by names such as Facebook, Amazon, Apple, Netflix, Google, and Microsoft. The biggest companies have rallied the most. We’re seeing market distortions like this everywhere. 

## _Thursday_ 

Here in the UK a new tradition has developed on Thursdays. At 8 p.m. people step outside their houses and clap for the NHS and all of the medical workers. It sounds a bit corny, but it’s emotional. As I’m clapping, I think about Jerry. I wonder how he’s taking the news. I wonder if things are better 

between him and his wife now that she has had some time working at the NHS. 

After the tribute, I head back into my office. Thinking about Jerry reminded me that I need to put together a job description to find someone to replace him. What I want it to say is I’m looking for a really smart animal who is willing to run through walls in order to make money. Instead, it comes out a bit more flowery, something like “Looking for someone with strong self-awareness that’s matched by a track record of success and a solid career trajectory. Needs to have process orientation and attention to detail.” It goes without saying that all candidates must write their own code. I don’t even need to include that anymore. And I want a strong pedigree, no more long shots: an Ivy, Oxford, Cambridge, or LSE. 

As I put the finishing touches on the job description I see a headline come across my Bloomberg. More than 4,500 people in the USA died from the virus yesterday. 

But here, we are adding to our long stocks, long dollar, and long credit positions. 

## _Friday_ 

I’m feeling good. Our performance is strong. Back in black and the book is working. I organize a Zoom happy hour for the team. I have food and drinks from Hakkasan sent to everyone’s flat since the restaurant’s doing delivery now. This is the first time in a long time that I really feel like celebrating. I can’t wait for the pubs to reopen. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Weeks Nine and Ten of the Meltdown (Sunday, April 19–Saturday, May 2)** 

Everyone in this business eventually learns and relearns that the market isn’t the economy and vice versa. That’s why economists don’t make 

successful traders. This situation, however, is taking that concept to a whole new dimension. We are seeing an incredible battle play out before our eyes. The largest stimulus ever versus the sharpest economic slowdown in history. Godzilla versus King Kong. 

COVID-19 has now infected more than 2.4 million people and killed 165,000 worldwide. More than 40,000 people have died in the USA alone. Here we are in the meat of earning’s season. A fifth of the companies reports this week. 

## _Monday_ 

My sister’s birthday. I have to remember to call her. I’m up early and see crude getting pounded. It’s down almost 40%. The May contract traded down to $11. As the market opens, it’s in complete free fall. Some speculator got caught buying it without the ability to take physical delivery. It’s inevitably some rookie who looked at his screens and thought he was about to become an instant millionaire by catching the falling crude knife: 

“Everyone knows $20/barrel is the floor!” 

$15 offered: “Hmm, this is extending more than I thought. Should I buy more?” 

$13 offered: “Oh shit.” 

$5 bid: Puke! 

Like Jerry’s tequila-soaked GI tract in Vegas, sometimes there just isn’t room to store any more. 

Throughout the day, we start to see the cracks get bigger, the largest still being in crude. The CME just put out a press release announcing that futures contracts can go negative. Holy shit. This is going to blow up people’s risk models. They think zero is their downside. Not anymore. The potential for unlimited loss creates a new round of chaos. Everything begins selling off, and that May contract is back in free fall. The price of the West Texas intermediate crude contract for May delivery ends the day at negative 

$37.63—yes, _negative_ . The sellers paid the buyers to take the oil, a stunning drop that’s never happened before. The world’s most important commodity is pointing toward a much deeper global economic crisis. It all leads to more selling, and the equity markets give back 5%. 

“Well, Boss, I bet you never thought you’d see that.” It’s the Rabbi. He’s talking about crude. He wanted to hop back on Zoom with me and Elias to do a recap of the day. The markets have just closed in New York, and it’s late here in London: 9 p.m. You can tell he’s pumped up from the day. We’ve been trading the markets well and did a good bit of day trading today, which made money, and he just wants to shoot the shit. 

He throws the next comment out to Elias. 

“How many screens you got set up at home?” 

“I have my laptop plugged into one of those huge Apple screens, and then two iPads hanging off the sides. It’s technically only four, but the big one allows me to configure it all just like in the office.” 

“How is your dating life these days?” 

“Let’s not talk about it. I need to get back to the gym. I’ve gained two stone.” 

“Rabbi, you need a haircut,” I tell him. 

“Yeah, no shit. I’m too fat to try to look like Bon Jovi.” He goes on. “Hey, I got one for you. I was doing some quick math. Those $1,200 checks went out in the mail this week. You know, the ones from the CARES Act. So ordinary folks get $1,200. But get this: If you assume that the top 1% has a net worth of ten million bucks and most of that’s in the market, they probably got a bailout of like $3 million. How about that? Would you rather get the $1,200 or the $3 mil? It’s a fucked-up, crazy world!” 

You gotta love the Rabbi. He doesn’t give one fuck about what he looks like on Zoom. There is no preprogramed background, no staging. He will give you his worst angles possible as well as a view of stuff you’d rather not see. I tell them both I have a potential candidate to replace Jerry. I want them to meet with her over the next few days. 

We sign off to get some rest. 

We are now one month from the lows of the market on March 23. Over this period, twenty-six million people have now lost their jobs. It was as if someone just flipped a switch and all of a sudden, everyone was out of work, not knowing how to pay the next rent check. One day, you’re sitting in your apartment in Brooklyn, taking the subway into work every day, and the next day, you’re getting the fuck out of town and sitting in some rental in the middle of New Jersey. The only saving grace is working from home. If you can’t do that, you’re fucked. 

Bonds are trading as if we were still in the midst of the panic. Rates are back to the lows and appear to be headed to negative numbers. But stocks are having their best month since 1987. A huge disconnect, all part of the new reality. We are having one of our biggest months ever. But fuck, I forgot to call my sister. Another belated birthday card for her. 

The unemployment numbers are mind numbing, and I think the Big D needs a vacation or come November, he’ll become a new addition to the pool of unemployed people. He’s cracking under the pressure. His daily press briefings have turned into full three-ring circuses. Today’s set a new level of absurdity. The Big D went on a free-style rant. He was encouraged by findings that warmer weather may dampen the virus, and he used his daily press briefing to ask whether light could become a medical treatment. “Supposing we hit the body with a tremendous—whether it’s ultraviolet or just very powerful light,” Trump said. “Supposing you brought the light inside the body, which you can do either through the skin or in some other way.” He wasn’t finished with that. “Then I see the disinfectant, where it knocks it [the virus] out in a minute. One minute. And is there a way we can do something like that, by injection inside or almost a cleaning? Because you see it gets in the lungs and it does a tremendous number on the lungs.” 

When the markets close, I go for a walk with Caroline. Even with the fiasco in crude, the markets were quieter this week, sort of like London, 

where almost everything is closed. We head over to Whole Foods on Kensington High Street. It’s a bit of a walk from my mews, but it’s a nice way to get some exercise. We stroll down the south side of Hyde Park, past the Royal Albert Hall. 

I like this walk. Reminds me of good things. Some people are out in the park, exercising, but not many. We decide to take a quick detour to walk past Freddie Mercury’s old house. They say that his first girlfriend, and lifelong confidant, still lives here. I like to pass by when I can and try to imagine what the old parties were like here. They must have been spectacular. And if it had all been just a few years later, Freddie would probably still be alive. There’s still no cure, but you can slow that virus down. COVID-19 is bad, but HIV puts it to shame. 

As we make the loop back up Logan Place to head toward the grocery store, Caroline hits my shoulder. I’ve had my AirPods in, and she has been trying to get my attention. 

“I forgot to tell you,” she says. 

“What’s that?” 

- “Meredith is going to be on the _Housewives_ !” 

- “What?” 

“Yeah, she made it. She’s going to be on their new series.” 

_The Real Housewives of_ [XYZ city] is the pinnacle of reality TV. To me, reality TV and social media are all a sign on the times, an exercise in building your personal brand by pretending that your life is more glamorous than the schmuck who doesn’t have a million followers. Somehow, this has been turned into must-watch TV. 

“Wow,” I say. “I guess that is amazing.” 

“I wonder if she can become the next Lisa or Bethany,” she says. 

“The easy stuff has been done on those shows now,” I say. “To get famous, she would need to do something really extreme.” 

We pick up our provisions from Whole Foods and head back home. 

## _Saturday_ 

The papers are filled with articles questioning the speed at which we can move beyond a crippling fear of the virus, rediscover old routines, and get back to work. We are going to see partial business reopenings in the next two weeks, driven by the “healthier” places in the country. Vermont and Minnesota are easing some restrictions, and Texas and Ohio are preparing to reopen. But this process is likely to be a slow, rolling one, with ongoing shutdowns. On the medical side, there are a half dozen promising drugs in the pipeline that could be available by Fall. The quality of the data that we have to deal with this is still quite poor, which makes all of this less precise than normal. 

COVID-19’s toll on the world is now three million confirmed cases globally, and 200,000 deaths. Although the numbers are slowing, they will keep growing. But all in all, we are in a different chapter now. The virus is subsiding in the USA, like a wave rolling back to the sea. We are now entering a slow recovery stage, where the markets will continue to diverge from the economy and from the jobless. Over thirty million Americans have now filed jobless claims. That’s more than 18% of the workforce. But a basket of the Big Tech names has already set new all-time highs. 

This is not just a big month for us; this is now one of our biggest months ever. We are getting a payoff for our long-risk profile. Our QE bet is paying huge dividends. 

The headlines in the US papers scream “The Worst Is Over.” But we know that’s not really the case. We’ve entered a new era of haves and havenots. We’ve now twisted capitalism into a new form. Now, even fewer will benefit. The rest will have to struggle to survive in this next chapter. 

I hate to admit this, but I feel damn good. 

## **Chapter 8 Economic Data Worse than the Great Depression** 

# **May 2020** 

**5/1/20—** _ISM manufacturing drops to 41.5._ 

**5/1/20—** _FDA approves remdesivir for emergency use._ 

**5/5/20—** _ISM nonmanufacturing declines to 41.8._ 

**5/5/20—** _Fed eases liquidity requirement for banks participating in emergency lending facilities._ 

**5/6/20—** _US reports 90K nurses contracted COVID-19, 260 dead._ 

**5/8/20—** _Payrolls -20.5M; unemployment rate to 16%; “real” unemployment to 22.8%._ 

**5/15/20—** _Fed loosens bank capital requirements again._ 

**5/17/20—** _Germany and Japan enter technical recessions based on Q1 data._ 

**5/27/20—** _US fatalities top 100K._ 

**5/31/20—** _George Floyd protests increasing in major cities; looting and riots in some._ 

**Month-end: UST 10s at 0.65%; S&P +4.8%; Nasdaq +6.8%.** 

I[gotta get the fuck out of London. It’s probably two months behind the] USA in terms of the virus, and it’s not going to open any time soon. I can’t wait another sixty days. I’m starting to go stir crazy. Every day feels the same: just one long day. And I’m quarantined with a woman who really doesn’t like me, an ironic punishment for past sins. I think I’ll head to the USA, but not New York, still too messy there. Maybe someplace warm. Miami. I can take Caroline and Elizabeth until the coast is clear here in London. Lots of hedge fund guys have already moved there to get lower taxes, so at least I’ll know a bunch of people. I’ll start looking for a flat for us to hide out from the virus. 

As I reflect on the Fed’s meeting from last week, I can’t help but think we’re running the biggest, craziest policy experiment in the history of mankind. QE is bigger than ever, and our government debt is skyrocketing. And Jay was clear they weren’t done yet. Already, Congress has provided $3 trillion in economic assistance measures over the last two months. “Will there be a need to do more, though? I think the answer to that will be yes,” Jay said at a news conference conducted remotely. Those policies, he added, “will come with a hefty price tag, but we would come out of this eventually with a stronger economy … This is not the time to act on those concerns. This is the time to use the great fiscal power of the United States.” 

The great fiscal power of the United States? By my count, we’ve been firing on the fiscal side for the better part of twenty years. Bill Clinton was the last president to achieve anything even vaguely resembling a balanced budget. Since then it’s been a shell game of tax cuts, social spending, and expensive wars. In its most effective and prudent form, fiscal spending should be countercyclical: increased outlays at lulls in the business cycle, restraint during the heady periods. Its current incarnation, however, hints at something more sinister. 

We know that QE will fuel more inequality, and the growing debt will be like a cancer to future growth. Whether central banks are buying directly 

from the sovereign or through the secondary market, they can increasingly be seen as monetizing the debt. What this means is that governments seem to be borrowing without any plans to pay the money back. They just sell bonds to the central bank to finance whatever spending they want to do. Treasury Secretary Mnuchin said that “over time” the USA will need to look into the sharply rising budget deficits. But we can’t just keep throwing more debt at things to solve the problem. The pile of debt will metastasize and will make us sicker and sicker over time. 

I’m listening to a _Macro Voices_ podcast. Dr. Lacy Hunt is presenting. He’s one of the smartest economists around. He’s talking about our growing mountain of debt, saying we’re headed for more deflation. Each dollar of new debt is producing diminishing returns. “More is not more. It’s less,” he says. We’ve borrowed too much from the future and are misallocating capital to zombie companies. In the short term, he says, we are headed for more deflation. If he’s right, people will get pay cuts. In a deflationary environment, wages go down. The real danger would be a period of deflation followed by a nasty dose of stagflation. 

After unsuccessfully trying to solve a debt crisis with more debt, policymakers will need to do something else. He thinks we could be headed to something called modern monetary theory (MMT), but this would bring serious inflationary implications. The central idea of MMT is that governments with a fiat currency system can and should print as much money as they need to spend. It all sounds enticing but seems to fly in the face of stuff like working hard, living modestly, and building a nest egg. 

The Fed has already pushed things to new extremes by buying assets it has never bought before. For almost one hundred years, the Fed purchased only government bonds. Now it has announced a wide variety of programs to buy various forms of corporate and other debt. The Fed is also financing bank purchases of municipal debt, and given the present trend, Fed purchases of equities cannot be too far behind. Some would say that the bond markets have already been nationalized. 

And all of this is in addition to the creative destruction problem: the natural process of allowing weaker companies to die a normal death. Uh-oh, the Fed has squeezed guys such as Warren Buffett out of the markets. The people who were prudent, who were sitting on all the liquidity didn’t even get to deploy any. The markets all ripped back too fast. So much for Schumpeter’s equation. Buffett might as well go open a Robinhood account and buy the FANG stocks as well. 

I tend to agree with Dr. Hunt. He has been right on this point for a long time. But look, there are plenty of people who would disagree with him. Their argument is that we will see inflation even faster since the central banks are printing. It’s premised on the concept of debasement, or increasing the money supply, and on something called the velocity of money, and credit expansion. 

Central banks are always debasing their currencies. It has been a constant throughout history. In the old days—I mean really old— governments would debase their currency by adding stuff to the gold or silver content of the coins, like drug dealers cutting the nose candy with boric acid. This way, they could create more coins. Government officials don’t want to lose popularity by raising taxes and cutting spending. Instead, they employ this type of juggling trick. Roman Emperor Nero was a chronic debaser. He started reducing the silver content in coins from 100% to 90%. Over the next 150 years, the silver content was reduced to 50%. When a currency is debased, sooner or later people catch on and demand higher prices, resulting in inflation. 

This entire process can end really badly. Germany is a great example. In the 1920s, the government reduced the value of its currency, the deutschmark, from around eight per US dollar to 184 per US dollar, by printing money to meet its financial obligations. It soon become worthless. It collapsed. The economy collapsed too. Hitler came to power. 

***** 

I found a new flat in Miami, about fifteen miles north of Miami, in Sunny Isles Beach. They call it Billionaires Beach. It’s in a high-rise condo right on the ocean. There’s a Zuma, Hakkasan, and Nobu nearby, so I feel at home. Well, at least I will once they open. I have a nice outdoor patio and can see all the way from Ft. Lauderdale to Miami. I gaze out my window and look at the empty beach. They are still closed. The ocean patrol drives along the beach in all-terrain vehicles, chasing people away. A lone plane flies by. It’s one of those planes you usually see with a sign reading “Two for One Happy Hour at Duffy’s,” or “KASKADE at LIV tonight.” It has a different message now: “Brighter Days Are Ahead.” I wonder who paid for it. 

The flat in Miami is quite a bit different from the one in Knightsbridge: no red mahogany and two-thirds of the living space is not underground. There are lots of windows. It’s what they call a flow-through unit. It’s all white with marble floors and hints of blue. It’s a crisp modernist look where less is more. This seems to suit my OCD and my allergies. Caroline is already working with a designer to help fill it with nice things. 

Living in a flow-through unit means occupying the same space, twentyfour hours, with Caroline and Elizabeth. It means working from home. From the marble island where I sit behind my laptop, I have a view of Caroline on the terrace doing summer reading for the class she’ll take in the fall. 

Elizabeth usually doesn’t slump out of her bedroom until late morning. When she does, I look at her over the top of my glasses. Her bun is slipping off the top of her head, and she rubs her eyes while reaching into the fridge for the milk. She dumps Frosted Flakes into a bowl and before drowning the cereal in milk, she takes a sip straight from the carton. I see her check over her shoulder to make sure Caroline isn’t in the room, watching. I’m not sure she really notices me. She’s not used to having me around. Caroline hates Elizabeth’s habit of drinking or eating straight from the container, just as she hates my dipping my head and neck down to drink 

right from the kitchen faucet. In quarantine, I realize I have things in common with my daughter. Elizabeth takes her bowl of cereal back to her bedroom. 

Here, in our beach apartment, we get off easy in this COVID-19 environment. The dough helps you do that. I eat the same food, I talk to the same people, and I can get tested whenever I want. And the fact of the matter is that COVID-19 is as fucked up as the rest of the world. It disproportionately affects people that can’t really afford it. 

In fact, there are only a few things that have really changed for us. The morning commute has turned into a walk from the bedroom. Zoom is the new office. Caroline gets in even more steps with the dog. I buy more stuff on Amazon. The team are all but virtual characters on the other side of a web cam, like video game action figures. Where it has changed most is the feeling of being a caged animal, not being able to go exactly where you want when you want. 

We don’t have real problems—you know, stuff like making your next rent payment, or being crammed in a tiny place, or feeling scared you’ll get sick, or that profound sense of alienation I’ve read about. 

The markets have stabilized and the VIX is back to thirty. The Big Tech stocks are making new highs. The focus is now on restarting a more normal life. It will take time and is going to come with more strings attached such as temperature checks, capacity limits, working from home, and surgical masks. Here in the USA, it took trillions of dollars to stabilize the markets. Globally, the toll is much higher. And we will need more. If you look at past pandemics, the data tells you it takes almost two years for things to get back to normal. The virus tends to come back, not as bad as the first occurrence, but it likes to remind you it’s still around. 

I’m up early. It’s payrolls day and it’s beautiful outside. The sunshine and warm weather feel amazing. I have a bounce in my step. I think it’s going to be a big day and want to get a run in before the markets get busy. I head toward Surfside and South Beach. There’s a nice path right along the 

beach that’s still open. The days are getting longer and feel much longer than in London. The beach looks amazing this morning and it’s already getting hot; good to get my run in early. 

After my run, I shower quickly to get ready for the markets. As I get to my desk in my home office, US two-year yields are falling to new lows. More traders are betting that the Fed will be forced to cut rates below zero despite Powell’s insistence that negative rates won’t make sense for the USA. I can only laugh. Standoffs like this have decidedly gone to the market time and time again. Powell can grandstand all he wants, but the idea of disappointing the bond market will become anathema. New parents vow to let their baby cry it out, but we all know what happens when the wailing starts at 3 a.m. This is Soros’s reflexivity and, especially in this mollycoddled era, it’s a painfully difficult cycle to stop. 

The jobs number hits. Another twenty million people have lost their jobs. Austan Goolsbee, one of Obama’s economists, is on CNBC as the number comes out. He says, “This must be the most heartbreaking day in the history of the jobs markets.” But stocks don’t care. They’re up big. They’re grooving to the beat that the bond market is putting down. It’s surreal to see the markets rip as the USA delivers its worst jobs report in history. 

The Rabbi is live, blogging the report in our chat. He’s reporting the news in rapid fire fashion: 

“20.5 million.” 

“Unemployment rate to 14.7%.” 

“Ton of people leaving the labor force so numbers actually even worse.” “Leisure and hospitality getting drilled: -7,653,000.” 

“Minorities lost the most jobs.” 

“Average hourly earning soars. Not sure what this means.” 

“Got it. Means that the lowest paid are losing the most jobs.” 

All of a sudden, his face pops up on the Zoom video screen, inches from the camera. “Hey, Boss, this is amazing. The fastest growing sector of the 

labor market was employment at central banks. They need to hire more traders to buy all this stuff. They’re the only ones hiring. Fucking unbelievable.” 

The chat resumes. He’s going through the Street’s reaction to the number now. 

“Street saying real unemployment number probably closer to 20%.” 

“Lowest-paid workers bearing brunt of layoffs.” 

“Restaurants and bars lost 5.5 million jobs in a month.” 

“More women than men lost their jobs.” 

“We could see big revisions next month, which would make these numbers even worse.” 

His face is back on Zoom. “Pretty clear what all of this means,” he says. “Now, you’re either rich or you’re poor. We just wiped out a decade of job gains in a month.” 

And in more bad news, the BoE has predicted that the coronavirus crisis will push the UK economy into its deepest recession in 300 years. 

## ***** 

I made a new hire to the team to replace Jerry before heading to Miami for quarantine. She’s more junior, a Brit who went to Oxford. She says things such as “queue” and “loo” and showed up for her interview in a pair of Hunter wellies since it was raining. I had to meet her in Hyde Park since everything else was closed, and I was not going to make a hire without an in-person interview. At first glance, I didn’t think there was any way she would cut it. Her clothes looked a bit too formal, and she had her eyes done in a typical London way with flecks of black at the corners. But then I asked her to describe herself, and she said, “I like to compete at everything.” She had me with that answer. 

In one of my first Zoom meetings from Miami with her in London, I 

explain what the central banks just did and how this will impact the markets. 

I’m focused on debasement. For a long time, no one has really cared about this, but I have a hunch that it will be a hot button issue for the markets, and I want to get her up to speed. I tell her that “we need to focus on M2.” M2 measures how much currency is in circulation and helps to look at debasement. 

“The purchasing power of money has fallen by 99% in the past 100 years,” I say. “That’s an average decline of 5% a year from inflation and the printing of more money. The central banks intentionally dilute you down this way, real slow so you don’t notice. The central banks use nice terms for this, such as ‘increasing the monetary base,’ or ‘increasing the supply of money.’ It’s the same thing that’s been happening since almost the beginning of time. That’s why that house your grandfather bought back in 1952 for $25K is now worth $367,000. And a semester at college that used to cost $200 is now $22.5K. Wages have obviously not risen by the same amount. This means that those who rely on their salaries to get by, those living from paycheck to paycheck, have suffered an incredible fucking step backwards.” 

M2 is the best way to measure debasement. “In the old days,” I say, “it was hard to measure how much gold they took out of the coins. Now, the Fed publishes data every week and you can just go to the St. Louis Fed’s website and see the most recent chart.” I tell her to “Google it and pull it up on your screen.” 

“Wow,” she says. “It looks like a hockey stick. It goes straight up!” 

“Yeah, they’re pretty remarkable in showing how much new currency is coming into the world. In theory, it should signal how quickly inflation will grow. Now, remember,” I tell her, “this is way more complicated than the old days, due to our digitized world and the fact that most governments have fiat currencies.” 

“What do you mean?” 

“It’s much harder to measure its impact on inflation. In fact, that’s probably the biggest conundrum in all of economics right now, and the central banks have been wrong on this for a long time. Let me get back to where I was going on this,” I say. “The Fed’s dual mandate is to balance unemployment and inflation. One of the ways it has historically done this is by manipulating M2 money supply. This supply has grown from about $4.6 trillion in January 2000 to just under $18 trillion currently. That’s a lot of new dollars floating around, a lot of new bank reserves. As a rule of thumb, the Fed likes to target about 2% of new currency each year. Obviously, in times of economic weakness, the Fed will run things much hotter than that. Right now, we are on fire, close to a 25% year-over-year increase.” 

“Wait,” she says. “Since 2000, the number of dollars in circulation has literally gone from $4.6 trillion to $18 trillion?” 

“Yeap.” 

There is a pause as she’s calculating the numbers in her head. 

I go on. “The most extreme periods of growth occurred in September 2001, January 2009, and January 2012, when the rate of M2 expansion topped 10%. They were all periods of crisis. We are putting that to shame. Now, we are running above 25%.” 

“You mean we’ve essentially removed 75% of the gold from the coins over that period?” 

“Exactly.” 

“Bloody hell,” she says. 

***** 

I turn on my television. It’s one of the ones that hides as a piece of artwork during the day and comes alive when you press the button on the remote. I flip to the Robin Hood virtual telethon. One of the benefits of being back in the USA is that I get to watch American television programming again. I like some of the Brit shows, but generally, I miss the stuff back home. Plus, 

I need to catch up on my _Tiger King_ episodes. Joe Exotic is America at its finest. 

The Robin Hood charity used to be primarily a big hedge fund event, but now it has gone way beyond that. It was founded by guys such as Paul Tudor Jones, Peter Borish, and Glenn Dubin, all hedge fund guys. They’ve already raised a ton of money for needy causes in New York since COVID19 started spreading. 

I call Lifecoach, who has also returned to the USA to be back in Chicago to ride out the virus. 

“Remember when we went to our first one?” I say. 

“Yeah, must have been 2007. I remember seeing Gwyneth Paltrow. I loved her in those days. She got me back into yoga.” 

“Those auction items were insane,” I say. 

“All I remember,” she says, “is that a dinner for ten with Mario Batali was auctioned for something like $1.3 million.” 

“That was the height of the bubble.” 

“I guess dinners with predators get really expensive in bubbles,” she says. “I don’t think Harvey bid on that item, but I do remember him being there that night. He gave me the willies just looking at him.” 

Robert De Niro opened this most recent telethon with a call to action: “Our city is under attack, but we’ve been here before … You can take your best shot, but you cannot break our spirit.” Tina Fey addressed the audience from an empty 30 Rockefeller Plaza to remind New Yorkers that many people were in bad shape: “If you had breakfast today, you’re better off than two million of your neighbors, who woke up hungry.” 

We are on Zoom as more data hits. US inflation drops. The monthly decline was the largest ever recorded. And to top things off in the month of April, the federal government spent more money than it ever has in a single month. 

“I just think long-end bonds have to sell off,” the Rabbi says. “These 

deficit numbers are just unbelievable. We are going to see a huge amount of new issuance.” 

“Yeah, let’s add to the steepeners,” I reply. 

“Get this,” he says. 

“What?” 

“Was just taking to my guy at Deutsche Bank. They have a new chart. It shows the zombies.” 

“What?” 

“Yeah, companies that have debt servicing costs greater than their profits, the zombie companies.” 

“What does it show?” 

“It’s unbelievable, like the walking dead, 20% of public companies now.” 

“Wow,” I say. 

“I have a new theory,” he goes on. “What’s that?” 

“You need to avoid the loud talkers. They’re the spreaders. I read it on one of my germ blogs.” 

As we start to give him shit about being a loud talker himself, Elias posts a tweet in our chat. It’s from the Big D. 

“As long as other countries are receiving the benefits of negative rates, the USA should also accept the ‘GIFT.’ Big numbers!” 

“Fucking great,” the Rabbi says. “Typical real estate guy. He doesn’t even know what he’s saying. He doesn’t realize how bad this would be.” 

To top things off, Jay gave a real downer of a speech. A real buzzkill, enough to drive stocks down almost 2%. His speec ~~h~~ 25 is worth repeating: 

The scope and speed of this downturn are without modern precedent, significantly worse than any recession since World War II. We are seeing a severe decline in economic activity and in employment, and already the job gains of the past decade have been erased … A Fed survey being 

released tomorrow reflects findings similar to many others: “Among people who were working in February, almost 40 percent of those in households making less than $40,000 a year had lost a job in March.” This reversal of economic fortune has caused a level of pain that is hard to capture in words as lives are upended amongst great uncertainty about the future. 

## ***** 

I’m walking on the beach, getting some weekend exercise. My phone rings. It’s Elias. 

“Hey,” I answer. 

“Boss, Jerry’s wife tested positive.” “What?” 

“She has COVID. She was treating patients in the hospital in 

Dagenham. They’re testing the baby now.” 

“Shit.” 

“Another nurse in that same hospital just died.” 

I start to Google on my phone, looking for stories, and find that more than 100 NHS workers have been killed by the virus. And Elias is right: One of the nurses in Dagenham just died. She had told her family she felt she had a duty to work and help. In April, the number of NHS workers killed by the coronavirus nearly doubled in a week, and the numbers keep going higher. 

## ***** 

As I walk back to my flat, I’m listening to a podcast that summarizes recent news stories. Another three million people lost their jobs last week. It’s not slowing down: 36.5 million Americans have now filed unemployment applications. Industrial production, a measure of factory, mining, and utility output, saw its steepest drop in records dating back over 100 years. 

But the S&P 500 is now up 30% from its lows in mid-March and back to where it was last October, when the outlook for 2020 corporate earnings looked sunshiny. Companies have sold record amounts of debt in recent weeks. Junk bonds, historically dodgy during an economic swoon, have roared back. 

To end the week, Obama made a virtual appearance. He never mentioned the Big D in his video commencement speech, but it was clear to everyone in the coronavirus lockdown that he was throwing shade. “Doing what feels good, what’s convenient, what’s easy, that’s how little kids think,” Obama told a multinetwork audience of millions of high school seniors, who won’t have an in-person graduation this year. “Unfortunately, a lot of so-called grownups—including some with fancy titles and important jobs—still think that way,” the former POTUS added, “which is why things are so screwed up. It would have been bad even with the best of governments. It has been an absolute chaotic disaster when that mind-set of ‘what’s in it for me,’ and ‘to heck with everybody else’—when that mindset is operationalized in our government.” 

I walk into the Miami flat to tell Caroline about Jerry’s wife. 

I overhear her talking on the phone in the adjoining room. It doesn’t sound as if she’s on with her mom or her sister. 

She says she likes Miami. Shit, from the sound of it, she likes being quarantined. “Well, I’ve been slowing down,” she says, “cooking more, reading more, and looking forward to simple grocery-store trips or walks near the beach. I’ve started walking to the grocery store that’s just across the street. I made a new friend here, who lives in the building next door, and we’re planning a long walk for tomorrow. I guess I like it this way. At least I have someone who appears to be listening to me when I speak. The simplicity is nice. I’m not sure I want to go back to a more frenetic life in London, but I miss you.” 

Well, I think, who exactly does she miss? And anyways, I can’t wait to 

get back to normal. I miss the camaraderie of the desk and my favorite bartenders. I want to get back in my seat and back to my routine. 

She finishes the conversation in an awkward tone, and I walk in to tell her about Jerry’s wife. 

She doesn’t say anything, just starts crying. 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

Jay’s appearance on CBS’s _Face the Nation_ was the highlight of the weekend. Jay said that “there’s a lot more we can do,” and just so everyone understood what the Fed does, he added, “We print [money] digitally ... We have the ability to create money digitally, and we do that by buying Treasury bills or bonds or other government-guaranteed securities.” Hey, at least he admits it now. No more wink-wink from the Fed. Never let a crisis go to waste. 

Jay’s comment that the government was ready to do even more buying created a new round of euphoria and sent the markets ripping, the dollar sharply lower, and commodity currencies higher. We’ve been dipping our toes into the commodity currencies, and they were strong on the news. Norway’s krone and the Australian dollar led gains among G-10 currencies. It has also helped that crude rose back above $30 a barrel for the first time in two months. It’s interesting, since the fixed-income markets won’t budge; they are still anticipating a difficult road ahead. 

We had a huge PnL day. And to top things off, the restaurants have opened again in Miami. They’re at reduced capacity, of course, but they’re open. I feel great, and from the looks of things here on the patio at Casa Tua, people are downright giddy to be out. Customers, servers—everyone is just plain happy to be getting back to normal. 

Earnings season is now largely over, but we are back in a heavy period for macro. The big news over the next few weeks will be economic data and any news on vaccine development. Pharmaceutical giants developing some 

of the most promising vaccine candidates are making progress. The main macro releases will be the May flash PMIs from around the world, along with the US weekly unemployment claims report. There’s also a raft of central bank events this week. Finally, on Friday, the National People’s Congress will open in China. 

All fifty states have now taken steps to reopen. Connecticut became one of the last to loosen some of its restrictions. But large parts of states, including major metropolitan areas, are still largely shut. In most places, the reopenings are happening in phases. 

Elias pops up on Zoom. 

“Hey, Boss, you see there was a shooting at a shopping center in Phoenix?” 

“No, I missed that.” 

“At least quarantine was good for stuff like that,” he says. 

“Yeah, the upside of quarantine: fresh air, no shootings and clean beaches.” 

**==> picture [8 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

Now that Miami is reopening, everything is al fresco: dining, workouts, haircuts—everything is outside. 

Globally, we have 5.2 million cases of COVID-19, including over 337,000 deaths. The USA makes up more than 1.6 million of those cases and nearly 100,000 deaths. The toll on the economy has been unspeakable. Workers filed another 2.4 million claims for unemployment insurance last week. Now, nearly one in four workers has filed for unemployment. 

## _Friday_ 

In Miami, it’s a new morning, a glorious morning, bright sun, and even a bit of a headache from all of the wine at an outdoor dinner with friends last night. It’s the Friday before the long Memorial Day weekend. 

The big news overnight was from the National People’s Congress, China’s big political power conference. China broke with more than a quarter-century of tradition by not issuing an economic growth target for 2020, a reminder that all of the numbers are in flux. More importantly, the country’s going to crack down on Hong Kong. While the world’s leaders are singularly focused on infection curves and economic fallout, the Chinese are reasserting their dominance and control. They’ve had enough of the protestors. They’re also not going to let a crisis go to waste. 

As a result, stocks in Hong Kong were down big overnight, and we’ll see more pressure in FX. China set a reference rate for the yuan at its weakest point in over a decade. Beijing wants a weaker currency; it helps foreign buyers purchase their goods and keeps them competitive. The PBOC set a daily midpoint for the yuan at 7.1293 per dollar, the lowest level since February 2008. Last year, the USA labelled the bank a currency manipulator when it hit a similar level. 

And Sleepy Joe Biden was on CNBC this morning, his first major appearance on the network. He’s entering the ramp to the November election, bringing it back into focus. In a rambling speech, he promised higher corporate taxes. I wonder why he has made that his opening salvo on CNBC of all places, particularly when most companies have been shut down and are struggling. Higher corporate taxes would not be good for the markets, come November. He needs to tighten up his act. 

All of this is like an extra period of recess in elementary school. There’s money to be made on all of these issues, a whole new array of investment themes. Idea generation is in high gear. This is what makes macro great. You get to learn about epidemiology, crude oil, Hong Kong Basic Law Article 23, currency manipulation, and US elections. I’m having fun again. 

I want to close out the month strong. I want to see some more PnL in these final days. Futures are up big as I wake up. Germany just sold more two-year bonds at auction. It was the strongest demand they’ve seen in 

nearly fifteen years. And the demand was there even though the bonds have a negative yield of −0.66%. We are living in a fucked-up world. 

We’ve had another good month, another big month. We are outperforming. Our book is working right now. Even the Argentine bonds we bought as a flier are up ten points on restructuring news. Everything is going our way right now. In trading, as in everything, it’s important to be a man of your time: understanding what animates the circumstances you’re given and trying to stay a step ahead. Play the game as it presents itself. And play to win.
