25 Jerome Powell, speech at Peterson Institute for International Economics, Washington, DC, May 13, 2020, www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/speech/powell20200513a.htm. 

## **Chapter 9** 

## **Lessons from the Gilded Age; Back to Market Highs** 

## **June 2020** 

**6/1/20—** _ISM manufacturing at 43.1; employment at 32.1; small upticks._ 

**6/3/20—** _ISM nonmanufacturing at 45.4; employment at 31.8._ 

**6/3/20—** _Fed again expands access to the Municipal Lending Facility Program. Illinois is the first state whose bonds were purchased by the Fed._ 

**6/5/20—** _Payrolls +2.5M; unemployment rate declines to 13.3%._ **6/8/20—** _Fed expands access to Main Street Lending Facility Program: lowers minimum size, increases maximum size, increases maturity from 4 to 5 years._ 

**6/10/20—** _US COVID-19 cases reach 2M._ 

**6/10/20—** _FOMC meeting; rates on hold. SEP says no hikes until at least 2022. It will continue its pace of Treasury and MBS buying. Powell’s pursuing a whatever-it-takes approach._ 

**6/30/20—** _Fauci warns US cases could reach 100K/day._ 

**Month-end: UST 10s at 0.66%; S&P +2.0%; Nasdaq +6.0%.** 

I[guess you can say the market crisis is over now. We are back in bull] market territory. If you’re a top 0.01%-er and had access to the best managers and bought the right stocks, you’re now richer than ever. Everyone else is screwed. Global coronavirus cases will likely hit ten million cases and deaths will pass the 500,000 mark this month. 

Even the news is back to normal with stories on mass shootings, rising racial tensions, and more allegations of sexual harassment. Minnesota is in flames. Quarantine ended only days ago, and we have mass protests breaking out in Atlanta, Detroit, Minneapolis, New Orleans, New York, and over twenty other cities. The protests are quickly turning to riots. I’m talking full-on violence, with burning cars, ransacked stores, and “Eat the Rich” graffiti painted on Rodeo Drive. It’s happening amidst calls for justice over police killings of black Americans. A man named George Floyd was killed in Minneapolis when a white police officer pressed his knee on Mr. Floyd’s neck for nearly nine minutes. The cop suffocated him in the middle of the street because he thought Mr. Floyd might have passed a counterfeit $20 bill. Mr. Floyd told the officers that he could not breathe. 

The pandemic-induced tinder box was set on fire when the Big D seemingly threatened protesters. He loves confrontation. When images of fires and destructive protests in Minneapolis hit the airwaves, the Big D tweeted: “These THUGS are dishonoring the memory of George Floyd, and I won’t let that happen. Just spoke to Governor Tim Walz and told him that the military is with him all the way. Any difficulty and we will assume control but, when the looting starts, the shooting starts. Thank you!” 

City centers are now being closed. The markets are up on the news. They’re doing their own version of smash and grab right now and are brutalizing the shorts. 

It’s all new click bait for the media. It’s the first time in three months that no one’s talking about the virus. 

The Big D has taken to Twitter again: “I’ve done more for Black 

Americans, in fact, than any president in US history, with the possible exception of another Republican president, the late, great, Abraham Lincoln ... and it’s not even close.” 

I’m looking at things to buy on the Internet. I know I shouldn’t be doing this. You never count your money while you’re still sitting at the table. But I’m back in synch with the markets. I know I’ll keep making money. I’m thinking I’ll finally get that place in Cannes or St. Tropez for our summers. But I want a couple of new toys as well, and I’ve always loved those vintage Land Rovers, the old military ones. 

Caroline and I are on our way to Newport, Rhode Island. We are going for a socially distanced wedding. Caroline grew up in Rhode Island and still has tons of friends here. I am looking forward to seeing her friend, Chris. She is a school-teacher in RI and a good shuffle-board player. We are flying from Miami to Boston and will take a car down to Newport. The airport is still quiet, no lines. Everyone’s wearing masks, but like everything in America, there’s a big division. There are those that hate them and just leave them dangling from their ears, and there are those in full-blown homemade hazmat suits, looking menacingly at the ones who are more relaxed. It’s a funny scene. People are odd that way, always at extremes. At least the Admirals Club is still open. As we sit in the club, having a drink, I check Bloomberg and see that airline stocks are still near their lows. 

We arrive in Newport to some light rain and drive down the main strip with the huge sailboats and marinas. Caroline seems preoccupied and slightly amused by a text message she just received, quickly replies, and then appears to immediately delete the conversation. Newport has a fascinating history. It was one of the original settlements back in 1639. Shortly after, a group of Jews were welcomed to settle in Newport; they were fleeing the Inquisition in Spain and Portugal but had not been permitted to settle elsewhere. The Jewish community was full of good traders, bringing Newport commercial relevance and creating a magnet for others. 

But like most places, Newport has a dark side as well. Newport was a major center of the slave trade, active in the triangle trade, in which slaveproduced sugar and molasses from the Caribbean were carried to Rhode Island and distilled into rum, which was then carried to West Africa and exchanged for slaves. In 1764, Newport had twenty-two rum distilleries. 

Sixty percent of slave-trading voyages launched from North America issued from tiny Rhode Island—in some years, more than 90%. It was illegal for much of this time, but people did it anyway. A few Rhode Island families got really rich in the trade. William and Samuel Vernon were Newport merchants who later played an important role in financing the creation of the US Navy, which still has a base here. They made a fortune. They even found a way to get other people involved. They created a scheme whereby ordinary shopkeepers and tradesmen could purchase shares in slaving voyages. 

Beginning in the mid-nineteenth century, Newport changed again as wealthy southerners seeking to escape the summer heat began to build summer places on Bellevue Avenue. Now it was becoming the social hotspot, the place to be seen, the Hamptons of the day. Newport is famous for its mansions, and by the late 1800s, the uber-rich were summering in Newport. They took things up a notch. They built “cottages,” such as Marble House, The Breakers, and The Elms. 

This period got so over-the-top excessive that Mark Twain coined the term _Gilded Age_ to describe it. He stole the idea from a line in Shakespeare: “To gild refined gold, to paint the lily ... is wasteful and ridiculous excess.” It obviously was not a compliment. 

Newport still oozes privilege. It’s a place that still values the blue bloods. You look around here and don’t see much diversity. I haven’t seen any protesters in Newport. 

We check into our hotel, near Bellevue Avenue, and I step out for some exercise. The rain has stopped, and I plan to do the Cliff Walk, which is a 3.5-mile path that sits between the sprawling back lawns of the mansions, 

and the rocks of the beach below. It’s a real sneak peek into the Gilded Age. You can just imagine the elaborate parties that took place on these lawns during the summer social season. 

Walking along the path, these humongous mansions remind me of _The Great Gatsby_ . I never really got Gatsby. I mean, come on, find a way to fit into the new life and stop pining over that chick. I didn’t make Gatsby’s mistake. I found a way to belong. I found a way to play the game. 

The reception for the wedding is at Belcourt of Newport, a famous spot where Jennifer Lawrence got married. It was built in 1894 and has been part of Newport lore ever since. It was designed to look like the hunting lodge of Louis XIV at Versailles. Belcourt was ground zero of the Gilded Age. 

As Caroline and I arrive for the event, we are given N95 masks with the bride and groom’s names on them. It’s not a traditional reception. Think of it more as open-air cocktails and then a long, spread-out receiving line. There’s no ballroom filled with people and food and no band or dancing. We are told that the bride and groom needed to have a virtual appointment with a city clerk to obtain the marriage license. 

We are allowed to wander through parts of the property and given pamphlets on its history. It all sounds a bit fucked up to me. I had no idea it was built by a bachelor and the entire first floor was for his horses. He had a marble trough for the beasts to drink out of. It’s 40,000 square feet, but there’s only one bedroom. What a fucking way to blow your inheritance. Typical trust-fund kid, he spent about $85 million in today’s dollars to build it. In 1940, his heirs had to give it away to get rid of it. They sold it for $1,000. 

I find the story of the bachelor annoying. I don’t like hearing about people being this stupid with their dough. A moron like that should not have that kind of fuck-you money. 

We are standing in the courtyard, drinking champagne, and waiting for the bride to arrive when I get a phone call from Elias. 

“Sorry to interrupt,” he says. He knows I’m at the wedding reception. 

“That’s okay.” 

“Just want to let you know I think you need to do something.” 

“What do you mean?” 

“Getting some blowback, some back-channeling going on.” “On what?” 

“On Jerry. Team thinking you’ve been too harsh.” “Ah. Thanks.” 

I guess I shouldn’t be surprised. Jerry is a likable guy. And not only did I fire him, but I haven’t really done anything since his wife’s COVID-19 diagnosis, and she’s still in the hospital. At least the baby doesn’t have it. I need to find a way to make amends. As I’m thinking about what to do, the bride and groom make their appearance, and we’re ushered into an area for the receiving line, all perfectly measured to keep people at six-foot intervals. 

As we leave the reception later that night and head back to our hotel, I tell Caroline that the Gilded Age is a good parallel for us. It was the last time we saw off-the-charts wealth inequality and lots of other social problems. They say this period of time was responsible for the rise in populism, socialism, and even more murderous political developments. And it ended badly. The end of the Gilded Age coincided with the panic of 1893, and then a nasty depression. It set the stage for the world wars that followed. And these mansions we are driving by are symbols of the period, symbols of the excess and the inequality. 

I’m not sure she’s even listening to me. 

## ***** 

For all the sound and fury of the last months, Wall Street is back in high gear. I’m seeing lots of reports from the big banks, such as: 

Our overall strategic stance remains constructive, on the view that an improving trend in economic data will matter more than a poor level, 

risk premiums generally remain above average, policy support is aggressive, and sentiment is reasonably cautious. We continue to think that the best way to express this positive view is buying corporate and securitized credit, selling equity and credit volatility, and overwriting equity and credit indices.—Morgan Stanley Research Report. 

My brother-in-law, who lost his job as an HR executive, is thinking of becoming a day trader. He texts me every day with ideas. 

“Check out DFEN.” 

“Blue chip across the board.” 

“I also reviewed TQQQ over the last ten years and can’t figure out why I’d just buy QQQ instead.” 

“I’m searching for a 3x airline ETF but couldn’t find one, so I bought TPOR instead.” 

“I’m not quite sure how the 3x leverage structure works. Do you know?” 

“I turned 44K into 95K in just three weeks trading these crazy things.” “It’s way more fun than work.” 

“Are you watching HZT? On fire.” 

Well, I guess that means that we should take some profits here. He’s my best signal, after all. And I should probably tell him that Hertz is a doughnut. The company has already filed for bankruptcy. The equity is a couple billion dollars out-of-the-money. 

~~.~~ 26 He Jay gave a virtual Q&A session hosted by Princeton University defended the Fed’s aggressive actions. “We crossed a lot of red lines that had not been crossed before,” he said. “I’m very confident that this is the situation where you do that and then you figure it out.” 

I’m back in Miami but wake up in the middle of the night for the SoftBank earnings call. SoftBank is based in Tokyo. I log into my computer and see the Rabbi is already online. He loves these calls. 

Wow. Earnings are a disaster. Vision Fund lost close to $18 billion in the 

most recent fiscal year, triggering the worst loss ever for the Japanese company. SoftBank had to write down the valuations of the big unicorns it owned. WeWork’s value has fallen by 95% this year. 

Masa Son concedes he’s unlikely to draw outside investors for another Vision Fund, an initiative he once proclaimed as the future of SoftBank as it moved away from the telecom business. About fifteen of the fund’s startups will likely go bankrupt, he says. 

In the deck of materials used for the earnings call, one slide shows white unicorns running up a mountain, implying performance. But there’s a ditch, or some sort of valley, that two of the unicorns have fallen into. This is labelled “The Valley of Coronavirus.” Only one unicorn looks as if it has made it out of the valley and back up the mountain. “If I were to describe it,” Masa Son tells his online audience, “unicorns that had been climbing uphill hard are suddenly faced with a coronavirus valley, and they are falling into that valley.” 

“Vision Fund’s results are not something to be proud of,” he says. “If the results are bad, you can’t raise money from investors. Things aren’t good.” 

Neumann, the WeWork CEO whom Jerry liked to stalk, will be keeping busy with his lawsuit against SoftBank. He sued the bank for scrapping the bailout, which had included a plan to buy $3 billion worth of stock from Neumann and other shareholders. But the bank scrapped the deal, claiming WeWork hadn’t met the conditions of the contract and citing legal inquiries by the US Department of Justice (DOJ) and the SEC. According to _The Guardian_ newspaper, reporting on this latest lawsuit, “Mr. Neumann put his trust in [SoftBank and SoftBank’s Vision Fund] to be stewards of WeWork, which he and thousands of others had worked so hard to build,” only to be met with “brazen” abuses. 

It just goes to show that lots of things end really badly when a bubble starts to pop. I close down my computer and go back to bed for a few hours. 

***** 

Lifecoach says she wants to play beer pong. She’s in Miami to help me scout out office space. I think we could use a small office here. Beer pong sounds like a good distraction, and I can use some time out of the house. Caroline and I had a blowout last night. In the middle of dinner at Komodo, with three other couples, she fucking lost her mind. She just melted down and started screaming. The jabs came one after the other: 

“How can you not even care about Jerry and his wife? How can you just sit there and be so smug? You think you’re so smart. Big deal. You just got bailed out like everyone else. You’re totally out of touch with everything.” 

It was an outpouring of negative energy, a river of venom. And look, it sucks to be disliked by someone who is genuine, as Caroline is. She’s got her issues. She spends too much and can be self-centered in her own right, but she’s not a bad person. To feel the absolute hatred rolling off her tongue was a bit tough to take, especially in front of the other couples. She broke the cardinal rule and took the argument outside the family. 

So, I did what made the most sense: I ordered a round of tequilas. And then another. A simple way to diffuse the situation. By the time we got to the Soho Beach House, I was blotto. 

Fast forward to now. I’m sitting in a car with a killer hangover, heading with Lifecoach to a place called McSorley’s Beach Pub in Fort Lauderdale. This place is designed for shit-faced spring-breakers. It’s perfect right now. It will get me over my tequila headache. 

We are sitting in the back of a black Suburban. Lifecoach checks her phone. 

“You see the news on SoftBank?” 

“No.” 

“Launching a new fund.” “What?” 

“Yeah, called the Opportunity Growth Fund. It’ll only invest in companies led by founders and entrepreneurs of color. They are trying to take advantage of the social unrest.” 

“Are you shitting me?” I say. 

“No, even by Wall Street standards that’s a crazy act of opportunism.” 

She pauses for a minute and then asks, “Who were those other people at Soho House last night?” 

“Just some people we know from London.” 

“How about that Italian guy?” 

“He’s one of Caroline’s trainers over at The Art’s Club. Why?” I ask. I don’t like the way these questions are coming out. 

“I don’t know. They just seemed a bit close if you know what I mean. Caroline is not usually like that. I don’t normally hear her laughing like that. It’s probably nothing. It just kind of stuck with me.” 

As we drift toward silence, my phone rings. It’s a reporter looking for a comment on the markets. 

We arrive at the bar. The place is packed, and I’d bet that most of the people here don’t have jobs. A couple of drinks into things, and I feel I’ve had a pure shot of adrenaline. My blood chemistry is off because of too much booze last night, and now it’s being supercharged. I’m ready to go, ready to play. On days like this, I can be more than fun. I can be dangerous. 

Lifecoach is in good form. She’s making her shots. She quickly becomes a crowd favorite. 

Her comments are sticking in my head and the booze is starting to twist them into demented shapes. He does always seem to show up at odd places when we are out. She spends more time at the fucking gym than anywhere else. And what the fuck was he doing in Miami? Shit, she loves to dig through my phone for dirt. It’s been a while since she found anything. Maybe I should start doing the same. And back when I was in Vegas, there were those charges from the Farmhouse. Who the fuck was she with? 

My thoughts take me back to the Vegas trip. I remember the guys from beer pong. Those poor bastards, not only did they lose at beer pong, but they didn’t even get a graduation. And they’re really fucked now: there won’t be any jobs for them. The Fed has studied this in the past. If you’re 

one of the unlucky ones who graduate during a recession, it impacts your lifetime earnings. It follows you around for the rest of your life. And it’s not just money. In midlife, recession graduates earn less and work more. They’re less likely to be married and they have higher death rates when they reach middle age. Yeah, they’re more likely to be one of those “deaths of despair” that my friend in Paris is looking for. I bet that big mean one will be a miserable son of a bitch when he turns forty, dreams shattered. The daily grind will pulverize the little decency he had in him. 

More tequila and the national debt clock flashes through my mind like a bad PnL day. Our debt is up huge since I was in Vegas. It’ll probably be heading to $29 or $30 trillion by the end of the year. I wonder if they still have the clock ticking or whether they turned it off when they shut down the Strip. 

Day drinking soon becomes night drinking, and it’s now taking a toll. I and my beer pong game are shattered. I have a very sour taste in my mouth. 

## ***** 

One of my favorite Warren Buffett stories comes from a lecture he gave to a group of students at the University of Florida’s Warrington College of Business ~~.~~ 27 I heard them talking about it once on CNBC. 

Buffett told the audience to imagine a barrel with 5.8 billion balls, one for everybody in the world in those days. Think of them as the same little Ping Pong balls we were using for beer pong. Each ball would set out your lot in life and determine your birthplace, IQ, gender, ethnicity, skills, and parents. 

“If you could put your ball back into the barrel, and they took out 100 balls at random, and you had to pick from one of those, would you put your ball back in?” he asked. 

In addition to not knowing which ball you’ll get, there’s another catch: “Of those 100 balls, five of them will be American. So, if you want to be in 

this country, you’ll only have five balls to choose from,” Buffett explained. Of the 100 balls, “half of them will be women and half men. Half of them will be below average in intelligence and half above average in intelligence.” 

He asked the students again: “Do you still want to risk taking a second shot at life?” 

“Most of you won’t want to put your ball back,” he said. “So, what you’re really saying is I’m the luckiest 1% of the world right now, sitting in this room, the top 1% of the world.” 

He’s right. Ain’t no way I’m putting my ball back in that barrel. 

But I know there are lots of people who would take the risk, take a flier on a better life. If you’re one of the ones left behind, with no real shot to change your position in life, why not? Go ahead and roll the dice. 

And with where things are headed, I can’t help but think that more and more people will be willing to do the same. Obama said that inequality was “the definitive challenge of our time.” But inequality has steadily increased under both parties ever since we started the QE and easy money bandwagon. Somewhere along the line, we all got confused. We thought it was right against left, blue against red. We were wrong. It was more like class warfare, an outright attack on our ethos. 

And that’s the sad part. Of the trillions and trillions of dollars we’ve just spent, none of it went into education or anything that will improve the prospects of future generations. It was all spent to try to preserve the status quo, all a big Band-Aid. 

Our saddle-’em-with-more-debt approach is premised on a growing economy. We are supposed to grow out of the debt. The narrative is that as the economy improves, we pay back the debt and everything is fine and dandy. But that’s a pipe dream, a best-case scenario that will never happen. The US economy could take a decade to recover. The Congressional Budget Office, a group of nonpartisans, said the current depression won’t allow us to recover until the fourth quarter of 2029. And if things don’t bounce back, 

some people are now saying it won’t really matter. We can just do more QE and spend more. 

28 ~~s~~ Just yesterday, June 16, 2020, Jay warned Congres on these points: 

The United States federal budget has been on an unsustainable path for years now, and that just means the debt is growing faster than the economy, so debt-to-GDP is rising. That is, by definition, unsustainable … The burden of a downturn has not fallen equally on all Americans, instead, those least able to withstand the downturn have been affected most. 

I guess that’s what really bothers me: this idea that we can have our cake and eat it too, there are no real costs to what’s happening. Anytime we get close to a downturn, central banks can just print money, and we can spend more. 

But policies on negative interest rates, QE, and deficit spending, among others, are doing damage to the underlying economic engine. They misallocate capital, create inequality, kill savings, and promote fast money over hard work. They kill the American dream. 

I guess it’s fitting for the age. Everyone wants to make things seem better than they really are, a whole world filtered through rose-colored glasses. Isn’t that really the defining characteristic of our age? It’s everywhere from social media to reality television to our President hawking “the greatest economy of all time.” You can’t get away from it if you want to. 

And that’s what people don’t seem to understand. QE and spending are the new weapons of mass destruction. They’re the new CDOs. It’s happening every day, and there are winners and losers. 

In some ways, it’s unfair of me to point my finger at the Fed. After all, that central bank is just following its mandate. It has good intentions, not wanting people to suffer. But it must follow the rules, and unfortunately, the 

rules only offer a couple of options. The Fed can adjust interest rates, print dollars, or buy more stuff, all the things that drive up the price of stocks and bonds. The Fed doesn’t really have the ability to help regular people. 

And the folks over in Congress—you know, your elected officials in the House and Senate—well, they have much greater powers, such as the ability to tax and spend, the power of the purse and of legislation. But politics is messy. You can’t count on politicians to fix things. They aren’t really looking out for you. 

So, there you have it. Most folks are swimming against the tide. The rules are stacked against them. They’re sitting in the casino playing a game they know will take all of their money if they play it long enough. And those who are winning by the rules will want to tilt the field even further in their own favor. 

I’m going to end my journal here. I began writing with hopes of capturing a moment in time, a watershed event that can be parsed and memorialized when the system finally collapses under its own selfcongratulating weight, when the saviors of the world are unable to save, and when their flocks finally see them for what they are, and reality for what it is. 

It just hasn’t turned out that way. Instead, thirty-three million people are unemployed, civil unrest is in the streets, the gap between rich and poor is wider than ever, and the markets are back at their highs. It has turned into one of the greatest wealth transfers of all time. 

But these are not my problems. As I told you early on, my job is to turn a pile of money into a bigger pile of money. I’ve done that. Knowing how the game is played, I’ve called it right, just as I did during the last crisis, and just as I’ll do during the next crisis. I’m back on top. I got into this business hoping to be a man of his time. I was then. And I am now. 

Mo just texted. The pools in Vegas are about to open. I gotta go and book my trip.

## **About the author** 

Colin Lancaster is a 25-year Wall Street professional. He has run two of the highest profile global macro businesses for the top-performing hedge funds in the world and has worked directly for a number of the icons of the investing world. He has managed investment operations in London, New York, Hong Kong, Singapore, Chicago, and San Francisco. 

He and his wife have three daughters.
